  - **The 27 blocks went the same day**, on the same reasoning — see the blocks bullet above. Between them the two deletions removed 289 files that looked available to the generator and were reachable by none of it.
- **`font` (52) and `theme` (5) are NOT installed, and unlike the examples this is not a judgement call — they are mutually exclusive by construction.** A font item is a declaration (`{family, provider, variable: "--font-sans", dependency: "@fontsource-variable/geist"}`) and all 52 set the same variable, so installing them all means 52 npm packages of which one takes effect. A theme item is a full `cssVars` palette that REPLACES `background`/`primary`/`destructive`/`chart-1..5` in both modes — installing five means the last one wins, and any of them overwrites the black-and-white brand. **These are a pick-one, and picking is the owner's call.** Worth raising: every generated site is on the system font stack today, and `@fontsource-variable/*` is self-hosted, so there is no CDN and no CSP problem.
- **The five chat primitives are in because they EXIST, not because a business site needs one.** Nothing in `PAGE_RULES` points at them; the lint only cares that a cited component is real.

## The third hundred (2026-07-30)

**The kit is 300: shadcn's 61, plus 239 we wrote.** Whole flows rather than more parts — a basket, a booking, an account, an admin table.

- **Commerce and booking are the two that were genuinely missing.** A platform that makes barber shops and cafés had no `cart-line`, no `order-summary`, no `calendar-month`, no `day-schedule` — the model rebuilt each inline every time. `week-strip` is the date picker a booking page actually wants, and `calendar-month` DISPLAYS a schedule rather than picking a date, which shadcn's `calendar` does not do.
- **Ten formatters, all `Intl`-based, adding no dependency.** `money` gets the symbol on the correct side and the right separators (€1.234,56 vs £1,234.56 is one call); `plural` uses `Intl.PluralRules` because languages with more than two plural forms exist and appending an `s` silently produces nonsense in them; `ordinal`, `list-format` and `date-format` likewise. `date-format` always puts the ISO value in a real `<time>`, so a crawler can read a date it cannot parse from "Tue 3 Aug".
- **`highlight-match` escapes the query before it becomes a regex.** Unescaped, a bracket throws — a crash triggered by somebody typing `(` into a search box.
- **Refusals worth keeping.** `code-block` has no syntax highlighting: shiki is ~1 MB for something a business site uses once. `payment-methods` uses names, not brand logos, which need licensed artwork and ship a remote image into every page. `date-range-picker` is presets rather than two calendars, because "last 30 days" is what people pick.
- **Auth forms give ONE error for the whole form**, never per-field — saying which of the address and the password was wrong tells an attacker whether the address has an account here. `reset-form` answers identically whether or not the address exists, for the same reason.
- **`feedback-widget` only asks for words after a NO.** Asking everyone for a comment gets very few; asking the person who just said it did not help gets the useful ones.
- **`split-view` shows ONE side on a phone**, chosen by whether anything is selected: two panes squeezed into 375px is two unusable panes. `sticky-cta` appears only after the reader has passed the real one, and only below `md`.
- **`bulk-actions` states the count**, because acting on "all" when you meant the visible page is the expensive mistake that pattern causes. **`filter-bar` shows applied filters as removable chips** — filters hidden inside a dropdown are how somebody concludes their data has vanished.
- **`priority-badge` carries an arrow as well as a colour**: three levels of red is exactly where colour alone fails. Same discipline as the second hundred.
- Prompt cost ~587 → **~929 tokens** for 300 components. `tsc` 6.6s. 574 unit tests, site-build 33/33, no new npm dependencies.

## The second hundred (2026-07-30)

**The kit is 200: shadcn's 61, plus 139 we wrote.** Utility-first — the shapes a page needs over and over, rather than more ways to decorate one — and every one rendered before it was committed.

- **Monochrome by rule, not by palette.** State is carried by fill, weight and a written label, never by colour alone: `status-dot` is filled / half / hollow rather than green / amber / red, `comparison-table` uses a tick and a dash, `metric-delta` draws an arrow AND says the direction in `sr-only` text. Colour alone does not survive greyscale, print, or anyone who cannot see it. `metric-delta` also refuses to assume up is good — for cancellations it is not, so the caller says.
- **Accessibility is where most of the value actually is**, because it is what a model writing a page inline reliably drops. `form-row` ties label to control by generated id; `tab-nav` is links with `aria-current` rather than a tablist, so the back button works; `radio-cards` is a real `RadioGroup` underneath, so arrow keys work where the usual div-with-onClick is unreachable; `external-link` carries `rel="noreferrer"`, without which the opened page can reach back through `window.opener`; `visually-hidden` is `sr-only` rather than `hidden`, which would remove it from the accessibility tree too.
- **Forms carry the right `autoComplete` and `inputMode`, which is most of what makes a form fillable on a phone.** `currency-input` is `decimal` not `numeric`, because a numeric keypad cannot type 4.50. `address-fields` exists mainly so a browser can fill the whole block in one tap. `name-fields` defaults to ONE field: splitting a name assumes a shape many people's names do not have, and most forms join them back together anyway. `password-input` can reveal, because hiding it from the person typing causes more failed sign-ins than it prevents shoulder-surfing.
- **`busy-button` is the difference between one booking and three.** A customer who presses submit and sees nothing happen presses again.
- **Three things deliberately use no library.** `progress-ring` is plain SVG, not a chart. `image-strip` is scroll-snap, not a carousel — a swipe works because it is the browser's own scrolling. `reveal` is IntersectionObserver plus a CSS transition, and renders VISIBLE when the API is missing or reduced-motion is set, so content can never be trapped invisible.
- **`video-embed` uses the privacy-preserving hosts** (`youtube-nocookie`, Vimeo `dnt`), so a small business's site does not drop a tracking cookie on a visitor who never pressed play. **`cookie-banner` gives Accept and Decline equal weight** — a prominent Accept beside a buried Decline is the pattern regulators fine people for.
- **One thing broke on the way, and it was a v3/v4 gap again**: `DropdownMenuItem` has no `variant` prop in this template's copy, so `row-actions` styled its destructive item by class instead. Same family as the button-size failures.
- Prompt cost ~274 → **~587 tokens** for 200 components. 574 unit tests, site-build 33/33, typecheck clean, no new npm dependencies.

## We write our own compositions now (2026-07-30, owner's call)

**The kit is 100: shadcn's 61 primitives plus 39 we wrote.** They live together in `src/components/ui/` because that is the one folder the builder reads — `UI_COMPONENTS` and `lintPages` both key off it.

- **The 61 are all PRIMITIVES.** Button, input, dialog. A business site is made of COMPOSITIONS, and every generated page was rebuilding them inline from `card` and `div`: a hero, a price list, an opening-hours table, the four states of a list. That is exactly what every paid registry sells — shadcn.io alone lists 6,220 blocks.
- **Bought was measured and rejected.** shadcn.io has 402 components and **gates every download behind an account token** (the index is public, the source is not), so it is a paid dependency inside the build path of every site we publish. The open registries — Aceternity 109, Tailark 77, Magic UI 77, Kibo 40 — serve source freely but skew animation-heavy: `motion` is on 165 of Aceternity's 270 items.
- **The decisive measurement: storing components is nearly free and DESCRIBING them is not.** 400 components is ~1.3 MB of `.tsx`, tree-shaken by Vite and excluded from the initial typecheck — nothing. The cost is the prompt, and names alone understate it: a font name is a complete instruction, a component name is not. Useful usage notes for 400 components run 5,000-8,000 tokens **per generation**, which is the territory that made all-2,096-fonts unaffordable.
- **So writing them beats buying them, and not mainly on price.** We own these APIs, so the usage notes are accurate by construction. 400 foreign components are 400 APIs the model half-understands — and today proved the pipeline cannot catch that: `message-scroller` typechecked, bundled, and **hard-crashed the page** because its context lives in an npm package and nothing said it needs a provider.
- **`data-list` is the one that pays for itself.** Loading skeleton, error sentence, empty state, rows — all four in one component. `GENERATOR.md` requires all four on EVERY list and the model hand-writes them on every page of every site; it is the most repeated code we generate, and getting one wrong is invisible until a visitor hits it.
- **`availability-grid` closes the booking gap.** Taken slots struck through and disabled, so a visitor sees 09:30 is gone BEFORE submitting rather than being rejected after. That is the `publicView` case, unserved since the data layer moved to Neon.
- **`safe-image` makes rule 7 unnecessary.** A picture column is filled in by the owner AFTER the build, so a fresh site has empty values and `<img src="">` paints a broken icon on every card. The rule asks the model to remember a guard on every image of every page; this makes forgetting impossible.
- **`marquee` is two CSS keyframes and stops for `prefers-reduced-motion`.** Every registry that sells one ships `motion` with it — ~50 KB of animation runtime for an effect that needs none.
- **Two deliberate refusals.** `location-card` does NOT embed a map: a tile map needs a provider key, bills per load and drops a third-party tracker onto a small business's site, when what a customer wants is the address and a Directions button. `phone-input` does NOT validate: international numbers defeat every simple rule and refusing a real number is worse than accepting an odd one — it raises the numeric keypad and nothing else.
- **Every one is PROP-DRIVEN, not children-only.** A composition that only takes children is decoration: the model writes the layout inline anyway and the component earns nothing. These take the shape of the data a site has, so a page hands `useRows(...)` straight to `DataList`.
- **All 39 were RENDERED before being committed**, on a real barber shop page. Compiling is not working — that is the lesson of `message-scroller` and of the charts that typechecked while rendering grey.
- Prompt cost went ~150 → **~274 tokens**. 574 unit tests, site-build 33/33, template typechecks clean.

## The component kit — 54, and why not 61 (2026-07-30, superseded the same day)

- **The registry is the source of truth, not the docs sidebar.** `ui.shadcn.com/r/index.json` lists 62 `registry:ui` items; the repo's `apps/v4/registry/new-york-v4/ui` has 61 `.tsx` files. The docs sidebar was checked first and was wrong twice — it presented `combobox` and `native-select` as if installable and omitted `form`.
- **The template declares `"style": "new-york"` (v3), and eight components exist only in `new-york-v4`.** That is the whole reason the CLI answered "not found" for them: it resolves by declared style. They fetch fine from `/r/styles/new-york-v4/<name>.json` and can be added by URL. `attachment` · `bubble` · `combobox` · `direction` · `marker` · `message` · `message-scroller` · `native-select`.
- **`native-select` was taken that way** — self-contained, no deps, typechecks. A plain `<select>`, which is lighter than the Radix one for a three-option dropdown, and that is most of them.
- **`combobox` was REFUSED, and the reason generalises to every other v4 component.** It fails to typecheck against our Button (`"icon-xs" is not assignable` — it expects v4's size variants) and pulls in **`@base-ui/react`**, a second primitive library alongside Radix. Taking it means upgrading Button to v4, which cascades through the kit and re-reverts this template's local edits. **So: a v4 component is only safe here if it is self-contained. Check `registryDependencies` and typecheck before keeping one.**
- **`toast` is refused on purpose** — `sonner` is here, and two ways to raise a toast get picked between inconsistently across pages of one site. Pinned by a test, because "we forgot it" and "we decided against it" look identical in a list of names a year later.
- **The five chat primitives are skipped as irrelevant** — `attachment`, `bubble`, `marker`, `message`, `message-scroller` are for building a chat app, not a barber shop.
- **Never run the CLI with `--overwrite` here.** It silently reverted this template's local edits to `button.tsx` (`cursor-pointer`, `disabled:cursor-not-allowed`, added back after Tailwind v4 changed the default) because a new component depended on it. Answer `n` to the prompts: `yes n | npx shadcn@latest add …`.
- **The drift guard runs BOTH ways** (`test/page-gen.test.mjs`): a component on disk that the model is not told about, and a component the rules cite that does not exist. It was one-directional until a mutation proved the second half missing — renaming a cited component passed the entire suite.

## Credits & monetization (live)

Every generation is metered in credits (1 credit = $0.008 fal cost). Postgres RPCs (all SECURITY DEFINER, auth-only): `get_credits()` / `use_credits(cost)` (grant **20** credits on first touch), `add_credits(target,amount,cents,ref,mint_key)` (**service_role-only** EXECUTE, mint-key gated, idempotent on `purchases.ref` — called only by the Stripe webhook with the service key), `credit_back(target,amount)` (**service_role-only**, ≤10 credits/call — reverses an orchestrator fee when the Claude call failed; no purchases row), `is_paid()` (has-ever-purchased). **Credits are `numeric(16,6)` (fractional, migrated 2026-07-14)** — `use_credits`/`get_credits`/`add_credits` are numeric-typed; generations still charge whole credits (`ceil(fal_usd/$0.008)`), but the **AI Orchestrator is metered per-call through the same ledger** (no longer a subscription): `/api/direct` charges `orchestratorCostMicros(step,effort)/8000` credits at the gate before the Claude call (ask 0.375 · prompt/error 0.5 · compose/revise High+ 3.125 · research 4.375), 402→client falls back to raw. The **Orchestrator + Video Editor $19.99/mo add-ons were removed 2026-07-14** (Video Editor gone entirely; Orchestrator on for everyone, no padlock, spends credits). Only Plus/Pro/Max memberships + top-ups remain. Worker: `/api/credits` returns `{balance, paid}`; charge-AFTER-fal-accepts flow (readCredits → fal submit → useCredits → cancelFal on race). `/api/checkout` (Stripe subscription/one-time session) + `/api/stripe/webhook` (verifies the `Stripe-Signature` HMAC, mints via `add_credits` with the service_role key on `checkout.session.completed` for top-ups and `invoice.paid` for memberships). **Stripe activated 2026-07-08** — the four payment secrets (`STRIPE_SECRET_KEY`, `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, `CREDITS_MINT_SECRET`, `SUPABASE_SERVICE_KEY`) live in GitHub Actions and upload to the Worker each deploy; the webhook endpoint is `https://isibi.ai/api/stripe/webhook` listening for `checkout.session.completed` + `invoice.paid`. The `private.mint` singleton holds the SHA-256 of `CREDITS_MINT_SECRET` (rotated 2026-07-08). Free accounts (`!is_paid`) get watermarks: images burned in client-side (canvas → JPEG → `/api/save` base64 path, server rejects raw-URL image saves for free users), videos get an on-screen badge on every playback surface (chat player, gallery card, lightbox) — the FILE itself is still clean; the fal blend-video burn is designed and its CI test bench is committed (`.github/workflows/fal-wm-test.yml`, runs on pushes touching its own files) but blocked on the fal balance top-up. Pricing page = `openCredits()` (three tiers Plus/Pro/Max at $24.99/$49.99/$99.99, rolling launch-offer countdown); top-ups = `openCredits(true)`. **Gallery storage is a subscription benefit (added 2026-07-08)** — saving to the gallery requires a paid tier, capped by size: **Plus 10 GB · Pro 50 GB · Max 100 GB** (GiB-bytes). DB: `user_plan` table (tier + plan_until, RLS own-select), `set_plan(target,tier,until,mint_key)` (mint-gated, called by the webhook `invoice.paid`, tier derived from minted credits, plan_until = now+32d), `storage_status()` RPC → `{used, cap, tier}` bytes (lapsed plan_until → free/cap 0; sums the caller's `storage.objects` under `media/<uid>/`); `delete_account()` also clears `user_plan`. Worker: `storageStatus()` helper + `GET /api/storage`; `/api/save` returns **402** `{reason:"free"}` when the account has no storage (free/lapsed/top-up-only, cap 0) and `{reason:"full"}` when the new object would exceed the cap (fails open if the ledger is unreachable). Frontend: gallery usage bar (`galleryStorage`, `refreshStorageBar`/`paintStorageBar`, tier badge + warn/full tint, upgrade CTA when cap 0 or full); `trySave` treats 402 as terminal (returns a `block` field: `'free'`/`'full'`) — no doomed retry queue, delivers the media on a temporary link with a friendly note. **Edge case:** a top-up-only buyer (credits but never a membership) has cap 0 → can't save; only memberships grant storage. **Gallery import (2026-07-16):** the gallery header has an ⤒ Import button (device files, ≤12/batch, image/video/audio, client caps 8.5/29/14 MB → `/api/save` base64 — an audio b64 branch was added, MP3/WAV/OGG/M4A by magic bytes, ≤20M chars) and an import-from-link box (`.gi-url` → `/api/import/fetch` → `/api/save`); both are storage-gated like every save (cap 0 → `openCredits()` client-side, 402 server-side). Errors surface via `sbToast` (bottom-center toast, defined 2026-07-16 — earlier guarded callers were silently no-oping).

## Payments — the site owner's OWN Stripe key (2026-08-06)

**Not Connect (owner's call).** The owner pastes their own `sk_live_` into Secrets, the Worker decrypts it and calls Stripe AS THEM — their account, their payouts, their fees; isibi is never in the money flow and takes no cut. No platform account, no OAuth, no application fee.
- **Why this is platform code and not something the model writes at build time**, which is the standing direction: two hard walls. **The database cannot speak HTTP** — `http` and `pg_net` are not installable on Neon (measured 2026-07-30, confirmed against the database and against Neon's docs), so no model-written SQL can make the HTTPS call a charge requires; same reason `site-notify.mjs` lives in the Worker. **Say it as "no HTTP", never as "no internet"**: `dblink` and `postgres_fdw` ARE installable, both are outbound Postgres connections, and network egress is therefore OPEN — see the sandbox-boundary bullet below, which records that the "cannot reach the network" claim was made once, measured, and found false. This line said "the database has no internet" until 2026-08-07 and so contradicted that bullet in the same file; the conclusion was right and the reason was broader than the evidence, which is the shape of claim this file exists to stop. And **the key cannot be in the page** — a published site is static files on R2, so a page calling Stripe would ship `sk_live_` in the bundle. Payments sit where sign-in sits: the model decides the site HAS the feature and builds the pages, the platform owns the credential and the network call. The model still declares, per site, whether money changes hands, which table holds the prices, which column, and the currency.
- **`site-secrets.mjs`** — AES-GCM, fresh IV per call, and the key VERSION travels IN the ciphertext rather than being inferred from which env vars are set, or adding the dedicated key later makes every earlier row unreadable at the moment it is added. An unversioned blob reads as v1 (the D1-era shape). **Writing is stricter than reading, and that asymmetry is the point**: the v1 derivation worker.js already uses ends in the literal string `"isibi"` — fine for reading a row written under it, and not fine for writing a payment credential, since with neither key configured every secret would be encrypted under a constant that is in the file and in git. `writeMaterial` refuses it, which is also what makes `addSecret`'s 503 reachable — before that split the fail-closed branch was dead code, found by writing its test and being unable to make it fire. Names normalise on the way IN (or `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` and `stripe_secret_key` are two secrets and an owner rotates the one they can see). Listing returns names and a hint only, never a value and never the ciphertext; the hint is a known public Stripe prefix plus last4, but an OPAQUE token gets no prefix because leading characters there are entropy.
- **The keys live in the SITE's own Neon database (`_secrets`), per site per project — NOT a central table** (owner's call). The checkout path must already hold that connection to price a basket from the site's own `products`, so the key costs no extra round trip on a path a customer waits on; a central table restores the cross-region Supabase hop `site-routing.mjs` exists to remove. Deleting a site drops the database and takes the keys with it — no second cleanup path, the class that left Neon projects billing forever. **Safe because grants are emitted PER DECLARED TABLE**: `_secrets` never passes through `grantsFor`, so `anonymous`/`authenticated` hold no privilege on it, and there is no `GRANT ON ALL TABLES` or `GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA public` for the Data API roles. Created by `applySiteSchema`, which every build runs — the `_sessions` lesson, where a table existing only where somebody used a feature 500s everywhere else.
- **THE ONE RULE: the price comes from the site's own rows, never the browser.** `parseCart` reduces a basket to the only two things a client may decide — which row and how many — and DROPS a posted price/total/discount/currency rather than validating them, because a field that never enters the calculation cannot be wrong about it. `toMinorUnits` reads digits rather than multiplying (not to fix a rounding bug — a sweep of the accepted domain found ZERO divergences from `Math.round(x*100)`, so that mutant survives on purpose — but because it needs no rounding step and **zero-decimal currencies share it**, where a hardcoded ×100 charges a hundred times the price). An unreadable price REFUSES rather than becoming zero; more precision than the currency has is refused rather than rounded.
- **A payable table gets NO PUBLIC INSERT GRANT AT ALL** — not a validation, a missing GRANT, and that is the whole security model. Leave it insertable and anyone POSTs an order with `payment_status:"paid"` and a total they chose. With no grant the only way an order can exist is `POST /api/db/<slug>/checkout`. The payment columns are dropped from the DECLARED list too, not only the DDL: `schemaDigest` prints every declared column with no managed filter, so a declared `payment_status` would be described to the generator as an ordinary field and land on the checkout form.
- **The order row is written BEFORE Stripe is called**, on purpose: written on the webhook instead, somebody who paid while the callback was lost would have no order and the owner would hold money with nothing to ship against. Both return URLs are built from the site's own origin and never from the body (a caller-supplied `success_url` is an open redirect on our domain). `payment.from` must name a DECLARED table, or a payment block naming `_secrets` would read the vault. A key that will not DECRYPT is not treated as absent — calling Stripe with an empty key tells a customer with a good card that their payment failed.
- **`POST /api/stripe/site/<slug>`** is the site's OWN Stripe, verified against the site's OWN webhook secret, separate from `/api/stripe/webhook` (isibi's billing) and asserted never to touch the credit ledger. **Fails closed with no secret** — it is the thing that marks orders paid. Verified over the RAW bytes; the update is idempotent because Stripe delivers at least once. An unknown slug answers **200** so a deleted site does not generate retries for days; a failure to RECORD answers **500** so it does, because the money is real. `payment_status` is an allow-list of exactly `"paid"` — an earlier draft accepted `status === "complete"`, which also matches `no_payment_required`.
- **Two testing lessons worth keeping.** A mutant making `priceCart` prefer a client-sent price SURVIVED the whole suite, because every test reached it through `parseCart` — so the security property was a fact about the STRIPPER with nothing holding the layer that computes money. And **`indexOf(a) < indexOf(b)` passes VACUOUSLY when `a` is deleted** (indexOf returns -1), so a mutation removing the webhook's SIGNATURE CHECK survived everything; all three ordering assertions were written that way and now go through a helper that proves both anchors exist first.
- **`payments smoke`** checks the live routes on every deploy at $0 — no model call, no Neon project, no Stripe account. Its own first draft reported "a forged 'paid' event is refused" as a PASS against the pre-deploy Worker: refused by a 404. The signature checks are gated on the route existing and print an honest SKIP otherwise, and acceptable statuses are NAMED rather than "anything but 200".
- **`SITE_SECRETS_KEY` IS LIVE ON THE WORKER (verified 2026-08-07).** The deploy log is the proof and the method is worth reusing: `SITE_SECRETS_KEY: ***` — GitHub masks a secret that HAS a value, so an unset one would print empty — followed by `✨ Successfully created secret for key: SITE_SECRETS_KEY` out of `wrangler secret bulk`, 10 of 10. So stored credentials encrypt under the dedicated key rather than deriving from `SUPABASE_SERVICE_KEY`, and rotating the service key no longer strands them.
- **STILL TO DO: no card has ever been charged.** One test-mode key on a real site is a separate, deliberate spend.

## What the platform provides, and what is BRING-YOUR-OWN (owner's call, 2026-08-06)

**The line: we run the site. Anything that spends the site owner's money or sends mail as their business is their own key.** Stated because it decides what gets built — the answer to "the model can't do X for a site" is only sometimes "build it", and for a third-party account it is "the owner pastes a key into Secrets".

- **WE PROVIDE, per published site, at no extra credential from the owner:** hosting (the built React app on R2 at `/s/<slug>/`, plus uploads at `/u/<slug>/`), the **database** (a Neon project per site, schema applied and `display` tables seeded), the **data API** (Neon's Data API + the RLS policies `site-rls.mjs` emits), and **sign-in for the site's own members** (Neon Auth, proxied through `/api/db/<slug>/auth/*`). Auth is easy to forget in this list and is the largest of the four — the owner never registers anything to have customer accounts on their site.
- **BRING-YOUR-OWN: payments.** The owner's own `sk_live_`/`sk_test_` in Secrets, their Stripe account, their payouts, their fees — see the section above. Not Connect; the platform is never in the money flow and takes no cut.
- **BRING-YOUR-OWN: email FROM a site TO its own customers** — EVERYTHING a published site sends: the confirmation to the person who booked, the receipt to the buyer, AND the notification to the owner that a booking arrived. **There is no exception**, and there was one until 2026-08-09 (see below). **LIVE since 2026-08-07. This bullet said the declaration path was "severed in two places" and that "nothing sends", and that stopped being true the day it was written** — `site-mail.mjs` is imported by `worker.js` and `sendConfirmation` fires on the write path, with scheduled jobs registered through `persistSiteJobs`. Only `docs/owner-notes.md` was corrected at the time, so this file kept describing a dead feature that works. The key is still the owner's own (`RESEND_KEY` / `SENDGRID_KEY` / `POSTMARK_KEY` + `EMAIL_FROM`), resolved by name out of the site's own secrets, lazily, never returned to a caller — the Stripe shape, and the platform has no fallback sender for a site.
  - **The cron is no longer the ONLY trigger, which is what that limit was about.** A confirmation sends on the write path the moment a row lands, so *"email the customer the moment they book"* — the thing a booking site actually wants — is reachable. The 2-minute cron still drives anything with `schedule_minutes` on it, which is the digest case.
- **`env.EMAIL` IS OURS AND THE BUILDER MAY NOT TOUCH IT (owner's call, 2026-08-09).** It sends platform mail to people holding a Go Farther account, and today that is the login code (through the Supabase Edge Function, which is on Deno and so uses the REST API + `CF_EMAIL_TOKEN` rather than the binding) plus `mailDomainLive`, a one-per-domain notice that an owner's certificate is issued. **Nothing a published site does may reach it.** More platform mail will come; none of it comes from the builder.
- **`notifyOwnerOfSubmission` WAS THE EXCEPTION AND IS NOT ANY MORE.** It told a site owner a booking had arrived, on `env.EMAIL`, firing on every `collect` insert on every published site — so customer sites spent **our** 200/day allowance, the same allowance the login code depends on, and one busy booking form could have stopped people signing in to the platform. It now goes out on the key that site's owner pasted into that site's Secrets, through the same `site-mail.mjs` path the visitor confirmation one branch over already used. **The cost, stated: a site with no mail key gets no notification** — the same condition the confirmation already lived under, which is the point (one rule, not two), and the owner still sees every submission in the Data panel.
- **One vault reader, shared.** `siteMailSecrets(env, db, slug)` is the single place `RESEND_KEY`/`SENDGRID_KEY`/`POSTMARK_KEY`/`EMAIL_FROM` are decrypted, used by both the notification and the confirmation — two readers of one vault are two things that can disagree about which key is live. **Guarded by a DERIVED test**: every caller of `sendMail(env` in `worker.js` is found and the set must be exactly `mailDomainLive`, so a new one added later fails there rather than quietly re-coupling the builder to our sender. (Its first draft counted `async function sendMail(env, …)` — the declaration — as a call and accused an innocent function.) A missing provider key and a missing `EMAIL_FROM` are named separately, because with no guard at all the failure still happens but reports as *"Cannot read properties of null"*; asserted on the CONDITION, since a mutation to `if (false)` leaves every message string in place and survived the first sweep.

## The builder reads links and searches the web — and the Attach button was dead (2026-08-07)

**Three input channels, one of which had never worked.** `builder/site-context.mjs` owns all of them, as a plain module with its side effects injected, so the whole tier is tested with no network, no model and no Worker.

- **THE ATTACH BUTTON DID NOTHING FROM THE DAY THE REACT ENGINE SHIPPED.** The composer collects up to 3 images and posts them as `images` on every build AND revise; the react-build route read them zero times, and `page-gen.mjs`/`publish-pages.mjs` never mentioned images at all. A shipped control with a tooltip promising "a logo or reference image" — and the obvious workaround for "copy this look", attaching a screenshot, was equally dead. Blocks now ride in the **user message**, after both cached blocks, so an attachment does not invalidate the ~27,000-token prompt cache; with no images the content stays a plain STRING, so no request that does not use the feature changes shape. **Page generation only, not the schema call** — that fee is flat, so tokens there are unrecovered cost, and a linked page is better schema evidence than a picture anyway.
- **A URL IN THE BRIEF WAS JUST CHARACTERS, and the failure was the invisible kind.** Nothing fetched it, so the model read the domain, inferred a trade and invented a business: a plausible answer that is not a copy, with nothing saying a fetch never happened. **So the reporting is half the feature** — `contextSummary`/`contextSentence` carry a failed read all the way into the chat, and `contextBrief` deliberately does NOT tell the model a link failed (that invites an apology written into the site). Bare hostnames count, because "copy sharpfadebarbers.com" is how people write it; anything shaped like a filename is refused, since a builder brief is full of `index.tsx`. Uses the existing `safeFetch` SSRF guard — a URL out of a brief is exactly as attacker-chosen as one out of the import box, so it gets the same guard and not a gentler second copy.
- **WEB SEARCH WAS STRUCTURALLY IMPOSSIBLE, not merely absent.** Both calls force one tool with `tool_choice: {type:"tool", name:…}`, which forbids any other tool — so adding `web_search_20250305` to the array would not have been enough. It is a SEPARATE Sonnet call instead, which also keeps `pagesRequest` at one call a build and its cache intact. **The gate rides on `design_schema`** (`needsWeb` + `webQueries`), which already reads the brief and already returns structured output, so gating costs no extra model call. **Started right after the designer and awaited just before page generation**, so the seconds spent provisioning Neon absorb it; `.catch` is attached at CREATION, not at the await, or a rejection in that gap is unhandled.
- **`shouldSearch()` is a FUNCTION because a mutation proved an expression could not be held.** Inline in `worker.js` it could only be asserted by matching a substring, and mutating it to `true || …` left the substring intact and survived the entire suite. It needs BOTH the flag and a query: `needsWeb` with nothing to look up is a model that answered the gate and not the question. Strictly `=== true`, so nothing merely truthy opens a paid call.
- **Billing obeys the rule already there:** research usage is `priorUsage` on `publishPages` and is charged only when a real app publishes — free on every path ending in the placeholder, like generation. A search is priced **per search** (`SEARCH_USD`, $0.01) from the same one table, because it is invisible in the token counts; four searches is 5 credits, more than a small generation's input. `totalCost` adds the CALLS' DOLLARS and rounds once, since adding credit totals would round each up and charge twice for the rounding. (It was `sumUsage`, adding the four token counts into one object — correct only while every call in a build was on one model; see the Builder-picker section.)
- **Fetched text is framed as reference, never stripped.** It is attacker-written prose going to a model that writes code. The blast radius is one customer's own site and `lintPages` still bounds what can be produced, so the framing is proportionate — filtering prose for "instructions" is a filter nobody can write correctly, and a half-working one reads as protection that is not there.
- **Two findings from not trusting the first result.** The mutation sweep first reported 31/32 with nearly every mutant blamed on ONE test — the baseline was already red from a stale assertion, so the sweep proved nothing; it verifies the baseline before it starts now. And the chat note rendered as a run-on paragraph, burying *"couldn't read your link"* mid-sentence, because `.st-msg` has no `white-space: pre-wrap` — every test passed, and it was caught by LOOKING at a render. The note is its own `.st-note` element and its own message field now.
- **A guard that could not fire, found by its own test:** the image total cap was 9,000,000 against three images of 2,800,000 each, so 8,400,000 could never reach it. 6 MB now, which binds.
- **Not proven live AT THE TIME** — the Anthropic account had no balance then and every build 503'd at `stage:"design"`. It was topped up 2026-08-08 and builds have run since; this was never re-verified end to end. 1443 unit tests, 35/35 mutations from a verified-green baseline.

## Charge for what is used, and the builder can be asked a question (2026-08-08)

**Owner's call, two rules: "every time a model is used it needs to charge the user based on our price model", and our fault is our cost.** The second half of this landed because the first one exposed it — you cannot charge for a question on a surface where a question is impossible.

- **THE BUILDER COULD NOT BE ASKED ANYTHING, and that was not a missing feature but a live bug.** `siteSend` had exactly ONE decision in it — `isBuild = !sitePages(site).length` — so the first message on a project built a site and **every later message ran a full revise**: designer, page generation, container compile, republish. Type *"can you read a URL?"* at an existing site and it cost ~21 credits **and rewrote the customer's pages into an answer to the question**. Type *"hi"* at a new project and it built a site out of "hi". The cheapest interaction there is was routed down the most expensive path there is.
- **`builder/site-ask.mjs` is ONE model call, and that is the whole design constraint.** Classify-then-answer as two calls makes the cheap path cost double, which defeats it — so the forced tool returns `{intent, answer}` together, Haiku, ~0.3 credits. **`/api/site/route` NEVER 5xxs**: a failure answers `intent:"build"` with a 200 and the client proceeds down the path that already worked.
- **EVERY unclear case resolves to `build`, deliberately asymmetric.** A wrong "build" costs a build the customer can see and undo; a wrong "ask" answers *"add a booking form"* with a paragraph and silently does not build it — indistinguishable from the builder being broken. An unreadable response, an unknown intent, a model error, a missing tool call, **and an `ask` that came back with no answer text** all fall through to build.
- **A MUTANT PROVED THE INTENT CHECK ITSELF WAS COVERED BY NOTHING.** Inverting `input.intent === "ask" ? "ask" : "build"` to `=== "build" ? "build" : "ask"` passed the entire suite, because every junk case in the "unclear resolves to build" test happened to have an EMPTY answer — so the answerless-ask guard one line below caught them all and masked the check above it. A model capitalising its enum (`"ASK"`) or returning `"question"` **with** an answer would have had every build request swallowed. The test now drives the unclear intents a second time with an answer attached.
- **An attachment SKIPS the router entirely.** A file plus a sentence is the shape of "use this"; the router would have to guess, and guessing "ask" answers a build request with a paragraph and drops the attachment on the floor. The one case where paying for the call is worse than not asking.
- **The digest the router sees is NAMES ONLY** — brand, url, page paths, table names. A `collect` table holds customer names and phone numbers, and this call fires on every builder message. Guarded by reading the client's own digest literal for `rows`/`msgs`/`html`/`conn`/`brief`.
- **The charge rule is now `ourFault(stage)`, and this is its THIRD position — each earlier one was right about something the next one kept.** (1) *"Charge before the output is judged"* was true about our cost and produced the measurement that killed it: a build spent 68s, returned `pages: []`, took 23 credits off an account granted 20. (2) *"If the customer got the placeholder, the pages call is free"* fixed that and over-corrected — it made a model writing a page with a type error in it indistinguishable from our container dying. (3) Now: **billed on measured usage, exempt when WE broke.** `validate` · `home` · `typecheck` · `published` charge; `build` · `publish` · `generate` · anything unclassified do not.
- **`build` is the judgement call and it fails toward NOT charging.** That stage covers a container drained mid-bundle (ours — measured live, `vite build was killed by SIGTERM` 2.5s into a 20s bundle, two seconds after a deploy) and a genuine vite error the typecheck let through (the output's). Nothing in the error text separates them. Being wrong toward free costs an occasional real bundler error; being wrong the other way bills somebody for our rollout. **An unrecognised stage is ours too** — a failure mode nobody classified is not one to hand the customer a bill for.
- **`publish` is an our-fault stage with NO branch implementing it.** A throw leaves the function with `useCredits` never called, because the charge sits after `deps.publish`. The exemption IS the ordering, which is why the source-read test asserts it and a mutant moving the charge up dies against six tests.
- **ONE place spends money, success included.** An earlier draft let the published path charge directly and left `settle` for the failures — two charge sites, which is how a build eventually bills twice AND how the "charge comes after publish" source-read starts matching the wrong one. `"published"` is in `CHARGED_STAGES` rather than short-circuiting past it, and the guard anchors on `deps.useCredits(` (the actual spend) rather than on the name of whatever wraps it.
- **The schema call's flat `SITE_BUILD_FEE` is now a DEPOSIT, not a price.** It still charges up front, because `use_credits` is atomic and row-locking and is the only thing stopping an empty account starting a paid model call — a balance read races. `schemaSettlement` then trues it up: positive charges the difference, negative refunds it, **and a missing usage report KEEPS the deposit**. Refunding on an unreadable meter turns "the provider renamed its usage field" into "every build is free", which nobody notices for a month. A refused build now refunds **what was actually taken**, not the fee, or the settlement is quietly kept on a build that 422s.
- **The one place the rule bends, and it is deliberate:** a schema call that returns no tables still refunds in full. A page-generation failure keeps its charge because the customer is left a live database and a placeholder — real work, retained. That path returns before anything is provisioned, so they are left with literally nothing, and "charge for what you use" was never meant to mean charging for an empty hand.
- 1489 tests, **20/20 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline. **Not proven live AT THE TIME** — the account had no balance then. Topped up 2026-08-08; not re-verified end to end since.

## The builder asks a question before it builds (2026-08-08)

**Owner's call, three parts: clickable options rather than a typed reply, on EVERY new project but never on a revise, one question at a time.** It rides on the routing call that already happens, so a question costs nothing beyond the ~0.3 credits routing already spent.

- **THE RISK IS THE MIRROR OF THE ONE THE ROUTER WAS BUILT AROUND.** `readRouting`'s rule — every unclear case builds — exists because a wrong "ask" answers *"add a booking form"* with a paragraph and silently does not build. A wrong "clarify" is worse: it stops in front of a brief that was already good enough, on the one path where somebody is waiting to find out whether the product works at all. So every guard is the same guard: **nothing here may become a reason a build does not happen.** A malformed question, an unknown shape, a question with one option — all build.
- **THREE THINGS ARE IN CODE RATHER THAN IN THE SCHEMA DESCRIPTION**, because a cap a model is merely told about is not a cap. `MAX_CLARIFY` is 3 and is spent by arithmetic in `routeMessage` before the model is asked; `canClarify` is false on every revise; and `readRouting` **overrules** a model that answers "clarify" anyway. Asking politely is how "one at a time" becomes "one after another" — *"have you got another question?"* is a prompt a model nearly always says yes to.
- **THE FAILURE THIS IS ALL WRITTEN AROUND IS LOSING THE BRIEF.** The composer sends the message just typed, and after a round that message is *"Book a time slot"* — so building on it makes a site about booking a time slot and *"a barber shop in Leeds"* is gone. The round holds the original; `clarifiedBrief` puts it back in front and appends the answers as plain Q-and-A lines. **Composed on the SERVER**, because `public/chat.js` cannot import the module and a second copy of a prompt fragment is two things that can disagree about what the designer reads. It is a no-op with no answers, so no request that does not use the feature changes shape.
- **The look is the NUMBERED treatment (owner's pick from four).** One answer per line with a `1`/`2`/`3` chip, `esc` to skip — and the numbers are real keys, because a hint printed beside an option that does nothing when pressed teaches a shortcut and then ignores it. **A column, not wrapped pills**, and that is what the other three lost on: these are short sentences the MODEL writes, not chips we choose, so any row layout wraps unevenly on exactly the wording that turns up in practice. A column cannot degrade.
  - **`esc`, not Enter, and the mockup said Enter.** Enter is the composer's send key; bound here it either fights that or works only when the box happens to be empty — one shortcut silently doing two different things.
  - **The keys are guarded on where the FOCUS is**, which is the whole difficulty: the composer is a text field and the customer has just typed a brief into it, so a bare `1` bound globally puts a digit in the box on some paths and answers a question on others. Anything that takes text always wins, and `metaKey`/`ctrlKey`/`altKey` are excluded because `Cmd+1` is the browser's.
  - **The key chips are a FIXED width** — `esc` is wider than `1`, and without it the labels sat on a ragged left edge, measured 28px out. Caught by looking at a render, then pinned.
- **The options are plain-language answers, not theme ids.** They are folded into the brief and the designer picks theme/family/fonts as it already does — one source of truth for those, and no second place that can name a theme the registry does not have.
- **`readQuestion` checks the count AFTER the cleaning, not before.** Four options that dedupe to one is a question with a single button, which is a dead end the customer cannot click past. Deduped case-insensitively and after trimming (`"Book online"` twice is one choice wearing two buttons), and options must be **strings** — `String(["a","b"])` is `"a,b"`, one button offering two answers.
- **A typed reply during a round is an ANSWER**, not a new brief: the options cover the likely answers, not every answer, and routing a typed reply down the ordinary path starts a fresh build from those few words. A correction (*"no, make it a cafe"*) lands in the same place and the designer reads it as written. **Skip goes straight to the build with no routing call** — paying a model to reclassify "just build it" is the one question too many the button exists to avoid.
- **The buttons render only under the LIVE question**, found by scanning the thread backwards. Left on an answered one they record a pair against a question two messages old. Found by mutation; nothing held it.
- **Two of my own source-reading guards were windows sized in BYTES** and stopped covering what they were written for the moment comments were added above them. Both now run to a landmark. Same family as the overlapping-window bug from the picker change, which makes it two sittings in a row.
- 1537 tests, 38/38 mutations from a verified-green baseline. **Not proven live AT THE TIME** — the account had no balance then, so the router 503'd into its own build fallback. Topped up 2026-08-08; not re-verified since. **One mutant was inert and reported a gap that was not there**, for the third time this session: a digit past the end of the option list reaches `siteAnswer`, which refuses the empty string — so removing the upper bound changes nothing observable except swallowing the keypress. Bounded anyway, and asserted, because inert wiring is what survives a whole suite.

## ~40% of generations do not compile, and the commonest cause was OURS (2026-08-09)

**Asked why a real build came out as the placeholder, and went to the eval rather than guessing.** Compile rate over the last eight runs: 3/3, 1/3, 3/3, 1/3, 2/3, 2/3, 2/3, 2/3 — **14 of 24 samples**. A failed build is not bad luck; it is the baseline, and with no repair pass it costs the customer the whole site and charges them for it. **UPDATE 2026-08-10 — the seven runs since these fixes landed are 3/3, 3/3, 2/3, 3/3, 3/3, 2/3, 3/3: 19 of 21.** Counted off the committed `PAGE-GEN.md` at each eval commit, not recalled. That is the largest move this number has made, and it is what the entry predicts — the two commonest error shapes were OURS and both were fixed here. **Read the whole column, not the last cell**: the series hit 3/3 three times in early August and fell back to 1/3, so treat 19/21 as the new baseline only if it holds, and note that the salvage below is what stops a bad run costing the whole site either way. (The "14 of 24" above does not match its own list, which sums to 16 — left as written, flagged here, because the ratio is the point and the series is now measured rather than recalled.)

- **`'fallbackSeed' does not exist in type 'Shot'` is the single most common compile error there is** — in half the failing runs, usually three at a time in one file. **And the model was right.** `fallbackSeed` is a real `SafeImage` prop, it is documented in the prompt, `Gallery` draws every `Shot` THROUGH a `SafeImage`, and the type did not carry it. The generator reaching for it is the correct instinct; the kit refused.
- **It also does real work, which is what makes this more than type-appeasement.** The seed decides the placeholder's angle and colours and falls back to the ALT — so a gallery whose shots have similar or empty alts paints a row of IDENTICAL panels, which is exactly the state a fresh site is in before the owner uploads anything. `starter-gallery` and `evidence-list` both passed `alt=""` with no seed, so every card and every row was the same panel: the precise case `safe-image.tsx`'s own comment warns about, shipped in two components.
- **THE DERIVED GUARD FOUND TWICE WHAT I FOUND BY HAND.** A shell survey caught three types because it read only each file's FIRST exported type; the test scans every one and named `Evidence`, `Product` and `Starter` as well. Written over the kit rather than as a list of the six known today, so a seventh picture type cannot reintroduce it — and paired with a check that the scan still matches at all, since a broken regex reports a clean sweep.
- **Fixing the type is only half.** The resolved shape in `PAGE_RULES` is what the model reads, so the prompt is asserted to carry the new field too — a kit that accepts it while the prompt still shows the old shape is a fix nothing can use.
- **THE SECOND SHAPE IS NOW FIXED TOO, and the live error is what named it.** A real build failed with `TS2305: Module '@/components/ui/faq' has no exported member 'FaqAccordion'` — the module exports `Faq` and the model embellished the name. **The lint only ever checked that the FILE existed**, never that the member did, so it passed validate and lint and died at `tsc`. Two halves: `UI_EXPORTS` is derived from `COMPONENT_API` (which already carries every name — a multi-export module lists them all), and the lint refuses an import of a member a module does not have. **A module with no known export list is SKIPPED rather than guessed at** — a false alarm there teaches the model away from a component that is perfectly real, which is worse than the miss. Aliases and `type` imports are allowed, each asserted, because the cost of over-refusing is exactly that.
- **The rule is in the prompt as well, because the lint cannot save the build.** A lint problem is reported and publishes anyway, so what stops the error is the model knowing the law: **the export is the file name in PascalCase, exactly** — true for **2,026 of 2,042** modules, measured. The 16 exceptions are listed and **DERIVED**, so an oddly-named component added later appears there without anybody remembering.
- **The last recurring shape is NOT fixed**, and it is not ours in the same way: an object assigned into a row field (`Type '{ stage: string; }' is not assignable to 'string | number | boolean | null | undefined'` — the `Row` index signature, three runs) and invented export names (`activityFeedPlaceholder`, `activityFeedFallback`). Both need thought rather than a field.
- **The eval is what made this answerable at all.** One sample a run, errors committed per sample under `docs/auth-audit/pages/*/_errors.txt`, so eight runs of history turned "why did my build fail" from a shrug into a ranked list. 1843 tests, 3/3 mutations caught.

## The last recurring compile error was an undocumented call (2026-08-09)

**`Type '{ stage: string; }' is not assignable to 'string | number | boolean | null | undefined'` — four times in the last five eval runs, the top remaining failure once `fallbackSeed` was fixed.** The model was writing `update.mutate({ id, values: { stage: "won" } })`, so `values` was read as a COLUMN NAME and typechecked against a row value.

- **THE MODEL WAS GUESSING, AND THE GUESS WAS REASONABLE.** `useUpdateRow` is named ONCE in the whole ~35,000-token prompt with **no worked example anywhere**, while `useCreateRow` has nine — and create takes the columns bare, so nesting them for an edit is the natural inference. Ours, not the model's, exactly like `fallbackSeed`.
- **Fixed at BOTH ends, which is the rule this file already records.** The hook accepts either shape — the precedent is one function below it, where `useDeleteRow` already takes a bare id *or* `{id}` "because its sibling takes an object" — and the rules now show a real update call.
- **A UNION, NOT AN OPTIONAL `values` ON THE INTERSECTION.** `T` carries an index signature, so `{ values?: … } & Omit<Partial<T>, "id">` intersects that property with the row-value union and makes it uncallable — which is the very error being fixed.
- **THE TYPE WAS ONLY HALF, and a mutation proved it.** Making the runtime ignore `values` and PATCH an empty body survived the entire unit suite: a page that compiles, publishes, reports success and **saves nothing**. Killed by driving a real edit page in a browser against the stub and asserting the column is in the PATCH body — `test/integration/site-runtime.mjs` grew a `PATCH` handler for it. The worst failure shape there is, and only the browser could see it.
- **My own negative assertion was wrong first**: "a column the row does not have is refused" fails because `Row`'s index signature has ALWAYS admitted arbitrary column names (the API drops them server-side). The negative that discriminates is an OBJECT in a column, which is the original error and must still be refused.
- **Two existing guards went red on the way, both mine to fix and both the recurring shapes**: one pinned the exact spelling `{ id: RowId } & Omit<…>` and a union puts a `(` in the way; the other read `update.slice(0, 900)` and a comment pushed the header it checks past the window. Anchored on the property and on a landmark.
- 1886 tests, site-build 75/75, site-runtime 26/26, **5/5 mutations caught** — with the browser harness in the sweep, since one of them is invisible without it.

## The "confirmations are dead" finding was WRONG (2026-08-09)

**An audit reported that `confirm`, `sms` and `payment` never reach `_meta`, and it was repeated all evening as the platform's worst outstanding bug — booking confirmations, text messages and the entire card-payment path dead on every published site.** Driven end to end, **every link holds.** The finding was false.

- **`metaOut = { tables: mergedTables }` stores WHOLE table objects**, so a field on a table needs no second place to be remembered. That is why the finding was wrong, and it is the property worth knowing: adding a fifth per-table feature requires nothing here.
- **All four links measured, not read:** the designer's tool offers all three per table · `normalizeSchema` keeps them (a real spec in, all three out) · the merge is whole-object · the write path loads the stored schema, finds the table, and calls `confirmSubmitter` and `smsSubmitter`, while `handleCheckout` resolves `normalizePayment` off the same stored table.
- **`test/site-features-reachable.test.mjs` is the answer to "is it actually wired", asserted per link**, because this repo has recorded a feature dead at ONE silent link six times and every one of them looked fine from both ends. 9/9 mutations caught — each link broken in turn goes red.
- **TWO OF MY OWN ASSERTIONS MATCHED THE FUNCTION'S OWN DECLARATION.** `confirmSubmitter\(env, ctx, \{ slug, db, def, row` matches both `function confirmSubmitter(env, ctx, { slug, db, def, row })` and the call — so a mutation deleting the CALL survived, twice. The declaration takes `row }`, the call passes `row: row`. Anchored on that now. The `buildEffortHTML()` failure again, and the reason a source-read must anchor on something only the thing being asserted can have.
- **The lesson about the audit itself:** a finding with a file and a line number reads as measured and this one was inferred. Anything that says a feature is dead is worth ten minutes of driving it before it is repeated — the cost of believing it was a whole evening of describing the platform as more broken than it is.

## Every published site was served the PLATFORM's lockdown headers (2026-08-10)

**Reported as a blank preview pane — Chrome's "This content is blocked" where the customer's site should be.** Measured live on two real sites: `x-frame-options: DENY` and `frame-ancestors 'none'`, the app's own strict policy, on a customer's published website.

- **`harden()` DECIDED ON THE RAW PATHNAME, WHICH STOPPED BEING THE WHOLE TRUTH WHEN THE SITE ZONE WENT LIVE.** It picked the permissive website policy for `/s/…` and `/preview/…` — correct while a site was served from `gofarther.dev/s/<slug>/`. Both hostname rewrites replace the pathname **inside** `handleRequest`, and `harden` is handed the ORIGINAL request, so `<slug>.gofarther.app/` arrives looking like `/`, misses the test, and gets the platform policy. Same for every custom domain.
- **LATENT FOR A DAY, THEN TOTAL — and the change that finished it was mine.** `/s/<slug>/` was the one address with the right headers, and making it 301 away means nobody lands on it. The one-public-address change is still right; it removed the last accidental cover for this.
- **The sites themselves were fine, which is why nobody saw it.** The bundle is an external module script, so `script-src 'self'` does not break it. What broke: framing (the preview pane, on every site) and, silently, **map embeds** — the site policy names openstreetmap/google in `frame-src` and the app policy does not.
- **`frame-ancestors 'self'` COULD NOT HAVE WORKED EITHER, and fixing only the mount would have left the preview blocked.** It was written when a site was same-origin with the workspace framing it; a site is on its own registrable domain now. The platform is named explicitly — a customer's site stays unframable by anyone else, which is the clickjacking answer.
- **`X-Frame-Options` is DELETED on a published site, not set.** It has no syntax for "self plus one other origin" (`ALLOW-FROM` is dead everywhere), so any value either blocks the preview or is ignored; `frame-ancestors` supersedes it per spec. Deleted rather than left unset, because an upstream response carrying one would survive.
- **`isPublishedSiteRequest` lives in `site-domains.mjs` because `worker.js` cannot be imported.** (CORRECTED 2026-08-13: it CAN be — see the entry on the worker harness. The extraction was still right for its own reasons, but this was never one of them.) This was the THIRD place that had to answer "which mount is this" and the only one that was untestable — the wiring layer again. It asks through the same helpers the two rewrites use, so `/api/` and `/u/` stay on the app policy exactly as the router leaves them unrewritten, and the bare zone apex is not a site. Guarded at both ends: the helper's answers, and that `worker.js` calls it rather than keeping a copy.
- **AND THEN THE CAUSE, WHICH WAS THE SCHEMA AND NOT THE PAGES.** Reconstructed the digest from the published placeholder: `TABLE events — access "user": PRIVATE PER MEMBER … signed out, both return 401` with `usePublicRows: NO`. The brief was a MARKETPLACE — *"people post their own events to sell"* — so the site's whole purpose is browsing other people's listings, and **not one visitor could ever see a single event.** There was no home page to write. The failure surfaced two steps downstream at `stage: validate`, which is exactly why the message was useless.
- **`publicView` IS DECLARABLE AND ITS DESCRIPTION NAMED ONLY THE BOOKING SLOT** — *"USE THIS WITH A BOOKING TABLE so the page can grey out slots that are already taken"*. So the designer read a field scoped to an optional enhancement, correctly judged it irrelevant to an events site, and shipped a schema with no browsable page. The description now leads with the case that decides whether the site can exist at all — a marketplace, classifieds, a directory — states the consequence (401 signed out, own-rows-only signed in) and KEEPS the booking case, because trading one for the other swaps this bug for the one it was written to fix. Both halves asserted, both mutation-checked. **This is the second time `publicView`'s description has cost a whole site over a fact the model was never given** — the first was the digest never saying which tables had one.
- **ASKED WHY THAT BUILD FAILED AND THE PLATFORM COULD NOT SAY.** Four different model failures printed ONE sentence — no `pages` key, an empty list, a list whose every entry had no code, and a tool call that never happened — so a real failure could not be diagnosed the next day. Measured by driving all four through `validatePages`: **0 pages, 0 problems, every time.** The worst of them was a SILENT SKIP: a call naming five routes with an empty `source` on each came out as "the generator called the tool with no pages in it", which is false and sends the reader looking for the wrong thing. An empty page is a `problems` entry now, an absent list and an empty one are separated, and the **output-token count** rides on the message — the one number that tells "the model said almost nothing" apart from "it wrote a whole site and we dropped every page of it". Fifth time in this file that a failure could not name itself.
- **A charged failure claimed pages had been written when none had.** The same screenshot: `stage: validate` · *"the generator called the tool with no pages in it"* under *"the pages were written, they just didn't work"*. `validate` covers three outcomes and only one wrote anything. The charge is unchanged and deliberate — the tokens were really spent — so only the description moved, and it is **derived from `gen.input`** rather than passed in, because `settle` takes a stage precisely so a new failure mode is classified in one place instead of remembered at three call sites.
- **FRAMING TAKES TWO POLICIES AND THE FIRST FIX ONLY MOVED ONE, so the pane stayed blank.** `frame-ancestors` on the SITE says who may frame it; **`frame-src` on the WORKSPACE says what the workspace may frame**, and it was still `'self' blob:` — correct only while a preview was same-origin or a Blob URL. Chrome refuses on either and renders the SAME sentence, *"This content is blocked. Contact the site owner to fix the issue."*, so the site's header read perfectly and the preview was being refused by the parent. **Reported by the owner pointing at a screenshot I had already been shown**, which is the lesson: the header was verified, the thing the header exists for was not. Both halves are now asserted in ONE test, deliberately — two tests go green one at a time and neither knows the preview is broken.
- **`frame policy` IS THE CHECK THAT COULD HAVE CAUGHT BOTH, and it runs on every deploy at $0.** It drives the two REAL policy strings, at the REAL origins, through a REAL browser — only the transport is faked, since the headless browser has no egress here, so `curl` is what proves the server sends them and this is what proves they work together. **It frames a hostname that does not exist**, deliberately: `harden` applies the site policy by MOUNT, so a 404 on the site zone carries exactly what a real site carries, and depending on a customer's site would go red the day they delete it. Proven to fail against each half of the bug separately, with different messages — reverting `frame-src` fails "the workspace can frame the site", reverting `frame-ancestors` fails "the site zone gets the WEBSITE policy", which is the mount bug rather than a framing one.
- **VERIFIED LIVE AS A HEADER, NOT AS A RENDER, and the difference is worth stating.** Both real sites now answer `frame-ancestors 'self' https://gofarther.dev https://www.gofarther.dev` with no `X-Frame-Options`, measured after the deploy. The pane itself could not be driven from this container: **the headless browser has no outbound egress here** — `curl` gets 200 from the same site the browser answers `ERR_CONNECTION_RESET` for, re-confirmed 2026-08-10 — so a framing harness written here reports "blocked" for the allowed origin and the refused one alike. **A harness that cannot fail honestly is worse than none**, which is why the first run's two identical `false`s were not read as a result.
- **Rollout is per-POP and briefly inconsistent**: 5 of 6 requests carried the new policy within a minute of the deploy and one still had the old one. A single `curl` right after a deploy is not evidence either way.
- 1896 tests, **8/8 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline.

## A site whose first build failed could never get a photograph (2026-08-10)

**Asked why a real site had no images and the answer was three steps deep.** The first build died at `stage: validate`; photographs are bought AFTER the pages validate, so it never reached them. That attempt still claimed the slug — so every attempt after it was a **revise**, and `const imgBudget = revise ? 0 : imageBudget(family)` meant zero. The site could never get a picture however many times it was rebuilt.

- **THE RULE WAS RIGHT ABOUT ITS OWN CASE AND WRONG ABOUT THE ONE BESIDE IT.** It exists because a revise re-derives the same budget and the model writes fresh descriptions, so nothing matches what was bought last time — a customer revising a 5-photo agency site paid **~94 credits in NEW photographs on every revise**, for pictures they already owned. That is still true and still prevented. What it assumed is that a revise means the site HAS pictures. Same shape as the `publicView` description two entries down, and the third instance this week.
- **`budgetFor` asks the honest question: does this site already SHOW a photograph.** Off the **stored pages**, not by listing R2 — they are already loaded on every revise (`loadSiteSource`), so it costs no I/O, and they answer whether the site *displays* one where the upload library would only answer "are there files", which is true of an owner who uploaded a logo and would suppress photographs forever.
- **NOT KNOWING BUYS NOTHING.** Unreadable or absent prior pages, or a missing slug, answer "has photographs" — because being wrong that way costs one unbought picture, and being wrong the other way re-buys the whole set. The match is **scoped to the slug**, or one customer's uploads would suppress another's.
- **The wiring mutant survived the whole unit file**, as it always does: `worker.js` was believed unimportable (CORRECTED 2026-08-13: it CAN be — see the entry on the worker harness. The extraction was still right for its own reasons, but this was never one of them.) so `budgetFor` sat correct and unused while the call site read `revise ? 0`. Closed with a source-read that also asserts the import, since a call to a name that was never imported is a `ReferenceError` on the build path — the `OWN_ZONES` failure one file over.
- **TWO EXISTING GUARDS WERE PINNED TO THE OLD SPELLING** (`/const imgBudget = revise \? 0 : imageBudget\(family\)/`) and went red on a correct change — a test about word order, which this file already records as its own recurring trap. Both now drive the real `budgetFor` and assert the PROPERTY, with the wiring checked apart from it.
- 1899 tests, **7/7 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline.

## The model invents props on components it CAN see (2026-08-10)

**Went looking for the "component the model was never told about" gap and the evidence says that is not the failure.** Every compile error the eval has ever recorded was on a component whose signature was already in the prompt — `render` on a `Column`, `onClick` on a bulk action, `title` on an `Activity`, `fallbackSeed` on a `Shot`. The model can read the API and writes a prop that is not in it anyway, `tsc` refuses the file, and since the salvage landed that costs the customer a page.

- **`lintPages` now judges a call against the signature the prompt already carries.** Two rules: an unknown JSX attribute, and an unknown key inside the object literal handed to one. **The second is the one that pays** — measured over the recorded failures, the invented name is almost never an attribute, it is a key in the array (`{ key, header, render }` against a `Column`). 2,017 of 2,032 kit components have a signature the lint can read; 319 additionally have a named type whose fields it can check.
- **THE ONLY MEASUREMENT THAT DECIDES WHETHER THIS CAN EXIST IS THE FALSE-ALARM RATE.** A rule that cries wolf teaches the model away from a component that is perfectly real, which is strictly worse than the miss it prevents. Driven over all **328 known-good pages** — the 4 reference pages and 318 family exemplars, the same corpus the model learns the kit from — and it had to reach **zero**. Getting there took five fixes, every one a false alarm on a correct page: a second copy of the splitter that never got the `=>` guard (121 pages), a module documenting one of its several exports (55), the innermost braces read as the item (24), prose inside strings scanned as code (14), and a nested element's props read as the outer one's (1).
- **AND THEN AGAINST REAL GENERATOR OUTPUT, WHICH IS THE BETTER WITNESS.** The 328 are hand-written exemplars — pages WE wrote, in the house style, so a lint tuned on them can be tuned to us rather than to the model. An eval run landed mid-change and committed **9 freshly generated pages** across three site shapes; **0 flagged**. Worth repeating whenever the eval commits: those files are the only corpus in the repo written by the thing being linted.
- **It SKIPS rather than guesses, three ways**, all the same rule as the import check beside it: a component with no signature, a signature `gen-component-api` truncated with an ellipsis (15 components — the unbalanced brace makes the prop list look complete when it is not), and anything carrying a spread, because `{...props}` can supply anything.
- **A FLAT SPLITTER IS WRONG AND THIS REPO HAS NOW WRITTEN ONE FOUR TIMES.** A signature is full of commas, colons and semicolons that belong to something nested. `splitTop` is depth-aware and shared by both readers — and it must treat `=>` as NOT a closing bracket, or `cell?: (row: T) => React.ReactNode` drives the depth negative and every field after a function-typed prop disappears. A field the reader cannot see is a false alarm on correct code.
- **THE `=>` GUARD READ AS INERT AND IS NOT, WHICH IS THE LESSON OF THE SWEEP.** Removing it changes the extracted props of **ZERO of the 2,017 components** — measured by diffing the whole kit both ways — because `Math.max(0, d - 1)` absorbs the negative depth whenever the arrow sits at the top of a shape, which is where every un-truncated kit signature puts it. So the entire 328-page corpus stayed green and the mutant survived. It is a real hole: a PAGE can nest an arrow one brace deeper than any signature does (`at:{ on:(x)=>x, tz:"UTC" }`), and there the clamp does not save it — the comma after the arrow splits the item and `tz` is judged against `Activity`. **The kit is not the corpus that exercises the page scanner**, and a mutant that survives against the wrong corpus reads exactly like an inert one.
- **Two guards genuinely ARE belt-and-braces, and they say so in the source rather than being deleted or pretended-to-be-tested.** `attrs[at] !== "{"` cannot change output, because a scan starting at a string value stops at the first `}` returning depth to zero — the same brace's own close — so the slice always carries one unmatched `{` and yields no objects. `code[i-1] !== "="` in the element scanner cannot fire either, since every JSX expression lives inside `{}` and `d === 0` already excludes it. Both kept: each holds by an EMERGENT property of a loop one edit away from changing, and the alternative is protection that reads local and is not.
- **`aria-*` and `data-*` are passthrough and had to be exempted**, or the lint flags the accessibility attributes a good page carries — punishing exactly the pages we want more of. The corpus did not hold that (none of the 328 uses one on a kit component), so it is driven by its own test.
- **Two fixtures in `site-images.test.mjs` were invalid calls and had to be filtered, not fixed.** `<Gallery src=… alt=…>` does not compile — Gallery takes `items` — so the new rule correctly reported them. Those tests are about the IMAGE TOKEN, so they assert on the token rule's own messages and let the other rule speak.
- 1892 tests, site-build 75/75, **10/10 mutations from a verified-green baseline** (three of them only after the corpus was shown not to be the right witness).

## Every modal on every site was see-through (2026-08-09)

**Reported from a phone: the mobile menu on a published site, with the page's own headings legible straight through the panel.** Thirteen components, every site on a backdrop theme.

- **THE TOKEN IS TRANSLUCENT ON PURPOSE AND THE PANEL SHOULD NOT HAVE USED IT.** `openRootCss` re-emits `--background` at **0.35 alpha** (0.42 dark) for any theme with a decorative backdrop, and its own comment says exactly why: every generated page's root div carries `bg-background`, so an opaque token hides the wash the theme paints on the body — *"the page sat in front of it like drywall"*. Right for the ROOT. `SheetContent` is not the root, and shadcn ships it `bg-background`.
- **`--popover` is the token for a floating surface and both paths already handle it**: untouched by `openRootCss` so it stays opaque under a backdrop theme, and re-emitted translucent WITH `backdrop-blur` under a glass one, which is readable by design. Measured on the live site: `--background` was `oklch(97% .01 80/.35)` and `--popover` `oklch(96.1% .0101 79.64)` — visually the same paper, one of them see-through.
- **THE DERIVED GUARD FOUND NINE MORE THAN THE HAND SEARCH.** Checking the shadcn overlay primitives by name found 4; scanning the whole kit for `bg-background` on a line that is also `fixed` and `z-N` found 13 — including `cookie-banner` and `sticky-cta`, which are on real customer sites. Written as a property rather than a list of the known offenders, because **upstream shadcn says `bg-background` on all of them, so a component refresh is the likely way this comes back** — and paired with a check that the predicate still matches the exact string the bug was made of, since a scan that silently stops matching reports a clean kit.
- **The fix and the guard share one predicate**, applied by the same expression that tests it, so they cannot disagree about what counts as floating.
- **`menubar` and `navigation-menu` were deliberately LEFT translucent.** They sit in the page flow rather than over it, so seeing the page's own wash through them is the intended look — that is a design call, not a bug.
- **Nothing in the pipeline could see this.** It typechecks, it bundles, it publishes, and no test in the repo opens a menu. Found by a person tapping a hamburger on a phone — the same way the broken image was found an hour earlier.

## The line: `.dev` is the tool, `.app` is the customer's (2026-08-09, owner's call)

**"Everything for the customer side is on app instead of dev — dev is the tool they use, my platform; app is their domain."** Said as a clarification and worth writing down as the rule it is, because it decides where a URL is allowed to point. **A visitor to a published site must never see `gofarther.dev`.** Audited on that basis; two real leaks, one of them on the money path.

- **THE SHARED-LINK PREVIEW IMAGE WAS `https://gofarther.dev/u/…`**, hardcoded. That is the picture WhatsApp, Slack and Facebook show when a customer shares the shop — the single most visible place the tool a business happened to be built with could appear.
- **A PAYING CUSTOMER WAS RETURNED TO A 404.** The Stripe return URL was `${origin}/s/<slug>/`, which is right on the platform and wrong everywhere else: on the site zone and on a custom domain the site IS the root, so it built `<slug>.gofarther.app/s/<slug>/`, which the hostname rewrite then prefixes AGAIN. Somebody would have paid and landed on a not-found page. **A custom domain now returns to ITSELF** — an owner who bought their own domain must not have a paying customer bounced onto ours at the exact moment they are deciding whether the shop is real. Latent only because no card has ever been charged.
- **`siteOrigin` is NOT `siteUrlFor`, and the difference is the trap that already bit once.** `siteUrlFor` answers "where is this site's home page" and falls back to a PATH (`gofarther.dev/s/<slug>/`), so appending an upload to it gives `/s/<slug>/u/<slug>/photo.jpg` — precisely the shape that 404'd every image on the platform an hour earlier. Uploads and the API hang off the ORIGIN, never off the home page.
- **Verified clean, not assumed:** the data API already runs same-origin on the site zone (measured in a real `build smoke` run — every call was `<slug>.gofarther.app/api/db/…`), and `og:url` was already the pretty host. Both were right; the two above were not.
- **The first three greps for the Cloudflare beacon came back empty and the beacon was there.** It is injected only for browser-like requests, so `curl` with a default `Accept` sees nothing — a plain HTML fetch is not a proof of absence for anything the edge conditionally injects. Found by sending real browser headers.

## A menu came out in alphabetical order (2026-08-09)

**Seen on the first real site of the evening.** A pizzeria's menu read **Dessert, Pizza, Starters** — the affogato above the margherita — because the page ordered by the category column and `order` sorts by the VALUE. Not a crash, not a compile error, nothing in the pipeline can see it, and it is wrong on every menu, price list and timetable the platform makes.

- **The rule is in the prompt because it can only be there.** A lint would have to guess which columns are categorical, and this file already records that a rule wrong on a third of its hits is worse than one that is merely narrow. The worked example is the MEASURED case (`Dessert, Pizza, Starters`) rather than an abstraction, so the model reads the actual wrong answer.
