- **The leftover clause is what stops the fix creating a worse bug.** A hardcoded section list silently drops any row whose category is not in it, so a dish the owner adds next month is invisible with nothing to explain it. Asserted separately, because the ordering half passes without it.
- **INSERTING THE RULE SILENTLY DISABLED A GUARD, and that is the lesson worth keeping.** `test/page-gen.test.mjs` sliced `indexOf("14. A TABLE MARKED")` to `indexOf("15. NO EXPLANATORY")`; renumbering moved both to 15 and 16, `indexOf` answered **-1 twice**, `slice(-1, -1)` is the empty string, and the test looped over nothing and PASSED — with the whole suite green. **A number in an anchor is a fact about ordering that a later edit changes without meaning to.** Both windows now anchor on the words and assert they are non-empty before reading, and `saas-setup.test.mjs` had the identical shape one step worse — a NEGATIVE assertion over a window, where "no POST in here" is trivially true of nothing. Proved by simulating the renumbering: the guard goes red now instead of quiet.
- **The rules are asserted to be numbered 1..N with no gaps or duplicates**, which is cheap, derived, and catches the mistake actually made on the way in: renumbering by hand left TWO rule 15s for a moment.
- **Two mutation survivors were INERT and reported a gap that was not there** — renumbering a rule no longer moves an anchor that does not name the number. Recorded because that is now the fourth time this session, and chasing one costs a hunt through tests that are fine.

## One public address per site (2026-08-09, owner's call)

**"Can we have only one instead of two — only the gofarther.app."** `/s/<slug>/` now 301s to `<slug>.gofarther.app`.

- **IT CANNOT STOP EXISTING, only stop being public.** `/s/<slug>/` is what BOTH rewrites produce and what the R2 lookup keys on, so it is the internal addressing scheme rather than a URL — a custom domain works by being rewritten into it. What changes is that nobody is given it.
- **THE ORDERING IS THE WHOLE CORRECTNESS ARGUMENT.** Both rewrites REPLACE the pathname with `/s/<slug>/…`, so by the time they are done a request that arrived on the site zone is byte-identical to one that arrived on the app zone. Decided afterwards, or on the path alone, every site-zone request bounces back to itself forever. It is therefore decided FIRST and gated on `isAppHostname(url.hostname)`, which is false for the site zone (no loop) and false for a custom domain — **an owner who paid for `sharpfadebarbers.com` must never have visitors thrown onto our hostname**, which is the opposite of what they bought.
- **Skipped when there is no pretty host** — the zone dark, or a slug the build filter allows and DNS does not. A mutation making it unconditional SURVIVED, and it is a real hole rather than a contrived one: the target becomes `https://null/…` for every site on the platform the moment `SITE_ZONE_LIVE` is turned off. Asserted on the CONDITION, since a check that `siteHostFor` is merely *called* passes with its result ignored.
- **301, not 302**, because consolidating the ranking is the point and a temporary redirect consolidates nothing. Two addresses serving byte-identical HTML splits a small business's search ranking between them.
- **`build smoke` now proves the redirect WITHOUT following it** (`redirect: "manual"`), because `fetch` follows either way and a redirect that quietly stopped happening would look identical to one that works. **Everything below it then lands on the public host, and that is the point rather than a side effect**: every check in that file used to load `/s/<slug>/`, which is the one mount where uploads happened to work — which is exactly how the `/u/` bug passed CI and was found by a person opening a link.
- **The preview iframe was ALREADY on the public host and a comment claimed the opposite at length** — that it "deliberately stayed on `/s/<slug>/`, same-origin with the builder". Wrong twice: `s.url` comes from the build response's `url`, which is `siteUrlFor`, which returns the pretty host; and the frame is sandboxed WITHOUT `allow-same-origin`, so it is an opaque origin and there was no same-origin to preserve. Corrected rather than deleted — a false claim in a comment is what gets believed next time somebody edits the line.
- **Three panels hand-wrote `gofarther.dev/s/<slug>/`**, so the app was offering a link the router now redirects away from. `siteChipUrl` is the one place that answers what a site's address is.

## Every image 404'd on the only address anybody shares (2026-08-09)

**Found by LOOKING at the first real site built after the top-up** — `forno-and-co.gofarther.app`, a one-page pizzeria that is otherwise exactly right. Its single photograph rendered as the broken-image glyph with the alt text showing, and the same file served **200** from `gofarther.dev/s/forno-and-co/`.

- **THE PRETTY-HOSTNAME REWRITE PREFIXES `/s/<slug>/` AND `/u/` WAS NEVER EXCLUDED.** `/u/<slug>/<file>` already carries its own slug and is matched at the ROOT, so the rewrite produced `/s/<slug>/u/<slug>/…`, which no route matches. `/api/` was excluded from the start and the same reasoning applies verbatim one path over — the route matchers key on the pathname and never the host, so both already work untouched.
- **BOTH REWRITES HAD IT: the site zone AND custom domains.** So every uploaded picture on every published site was broken on the address a customer sends people to, and fine on the one nobody sees. Including the photographs the platform BUYS at ~19 credits each.
- **The two guards were pinned to the literal `!url.pathname.startsWith("/api/")`** — one in `site-zone.test.mjs`, one in `site-domains.test.mjs` — so each described one member of a list rather than the rule, and both stayed green throughout. `servedAtRoot` is now the one list and both are asserted through it. **Assert the property, not the spelling**, for the second time in two days.
- **A mutation found a second real gap**: written with `includes` instead of `startsWith`, `/docs/api/reference` — an ordinary page for the kind of software company this platform builds — stops being rewritten to its site and 404s. Anchoring is asserted with cases that can actually fire.
- **The cost, stated:** a site can serve another site's uploads through its own hostname. Those objects are already public at `gofarther.dev/u/…`, served `inline` with SVG refused at the door, so nothing new is exposed — and checking the path's slug against the hostname's would break an owner moving a picture between two of their own sites.
- **Nothing in CI could have caught it.** `build smoke` loads the site at `/s/<slug>/`, which is the one mount where this works.

## A killed step was billed to the customer (2026-08-09)

**Found in a real `build smoke` log, not by reading.** A revise came back `stage: "typecheck"` with `tsc was killed by SIGTERM (no output)` — the container being drained under a running build, which is the documented `stage: "build"` failure — and it arrived wearing the ONE stage that is **charged and never retried**. So our own rollout lost the customer's revise and billed them for it, and which of those two outcomes happens comes down to *which step was running when the instance went away*.

- **A SIGNAL IS NOT THE JUDGEMENT CALL `build` WAS DESCRIBED AS.** That entry says "nothing in the error text reliably separates them" — true of a genuine bundler error, where a drained container and a real vite failure look alike. A kill is not that: the process never got to judge the code, so there is nothing ambiguous to weigh. `wasKilled` is therefore deliberately NARROW — `exitReason` returns a step's real diagnosis in PREFERENCE to the killed sentence, so the two shapes cannot both match, and a page with a type error in it stays charged.
- **Reclassified at the boundary, in `compile`, rather than as a second rule.** One line turns it into `stage: "build"` and the existing retry, the existing `ourFault` exemption and the new salvage all do the right thing with nothing further to keep in step. `killedAt` preserves which step was actually killed, or the reclassification would hide the diagnosis it exists to correct.
- **The matcher is driven through the REAL `exitReason` in its test**, across three steps and both signals, rather than restating its wording. Two spellings of one fact is how this drifts, and the direction it drifts in is a customer charged for our container being stopped.
- **THE OTHER HALF OF THE SAME LOG IS THE GOOD NEWS**: the first build was 82/83 — real site, seeded rows, a real browser submitting the form, the row landing in Postgres, the owner reading it back, the site deleted and the files gone.

## The click assertion passed locally and raced in CI (2026-08-09)

**`site build` went red on three consecutive commits, on the nav-click check added the day before.** The failure text was the HOME page's words at the `/book` address, at the `/` mount, while `/s/<slug>/` passed **in the same run** — the signature of a race, not a routing bug.

- **`waitForLoadState("networkidle")` IS NOT A RENDER, and it is not even a fetch.** Routes are code-split one chunk per page, so clicking a nav link fires a dynamic import — a request that has not STARTED when the click returns, so that state is already satisfied and the read below it raced. All three assertions ran within **3ms** of each other in the failing run.
- **REPRODUCED BEFORE FIXING, and CPU throttling was the wrong instrument.** 8x slower CPU changed nothing; **400ms of network latency** reproduced the CI text exactly, on both mounts. The thing being waited for is a fetch.
- **The latency STAYS in the test**, which is the durable half. Against a local server the chunk lands in under a millisecond, so a read-immediately assertion passes here and loses on a loaded runner — a test that can only pass by accident. Verified to DISCRIMINATE rather than assumed: wait removed → 2 failures, wait restored → 21/21, under identical conditions. Costs ~8s.
- **The wait swallows its timeout on purpose.** A genuinely inert nav must be reported by the assertion, with the page text that explains it, rather than as a Playwright error saying only "timed out" — and against the restored `#/book` bug it waits its five seconds and then goes red, which is the behaviour that matters.

## One bad page cost the whole site (2026-08-09)

**`tsc --noEmit` runs over the app, so one `TS2305` in `memberships.tsx` failed the build and the customer got the data-model placeholder — no home page, no price list, no booking form, none of the five files that were fine.** With **~40% of generations failing to compile** that is the ordinary outcome, not an edge case. Measured live the same day on `pulse-fitness.gofarther.app`, which was still serving the placeholder hours later.

- **THE FAILING PAGE IS REPLACED BY A STUB AND THE CONTAINER RUNS AGAIN.** What the customer gets is their site with one page reading *"This page isn't finished yet"*, instead of nothing.
- **NOT THE REPAIR PASS THAT WAS REMOVED, and the distinction is the whole justification.** That one re-ran the MODEL — ~80% of what a build costs — to guess again at every page. This re-runs nothing but the container, on source that already exists, with one file replaced by a constant. No model call, no tokens, ~15-40s. The removal was about paying for a second GENERATION; this pays for a second COMPILE. Asserted at the dep boundary: `generate` is still called exactly once.
- **STANDING IN FOR THE PAGE RATHER THAN DELETING IT is forced by the route tree.** Every other page's `<Link to="/memberships">` is typed against the tree `tsr generate` emits, so removing the file turns ONE broken page into a compile error on **every page that links to it** — the failure already recorded when posting `index.tsx` alone left its `<Link to="/menu">` pointing at nothing. `validateSearch` is permissive for the same reason one prop over: a price row navigating with `search: { service }` is typed against the DESTINATION's validator, so a stub without one makes that call a type error. Both proved by a real build in `site-build`, which is the only thing that knows what a route id has to spell.
- **THREE REFUSALS, each of which would otherwise buy a wasted container run.** **The home page must survive** — stubbing it leaves the one address a customer shares rendering an apology, and the header that reaches every other page went with the source; same rule the `home` stage already enforces on a build with no index, applied to one that is unusable. **A file we did not write** means the error is in the kit and no amount of stubbing pages fixes it (`foreign` is returned rather than folded into the reason, because a kit file turning up there is US shipping something broken). **Nothing named** is not a page problem either. A `build`-stage failure is never salvaged — stubbing cannot fix a drained container, and it is the our-fault stage.
- **THE STUB IS WHAT GETS STORED**, so a revise edits the stub rather than being handed back a file that would not compile — which invites the model to keep the broken line. **Asserted at the PUBLISH boundary, not only the compile one**: those are two different arguments and only the second is what a revise reads. The harness dropped the second argument on the floor (`publish: (dist) => …`), so this could have shipped storing broken source with every test green — the `setTotp` lesson, and the reason the fake now forwards both.
- **The first failure survives on the response.** A salvaged build returns `ok`, so without keeping `error`/`cited` the thing the generator actually got wrong is gone. `stage` is deliberately NOT set: it names an OUTCOME, and this build did not end at the typecheck.
- **THE CAVEAT IS ITS OWN FIELD (`salvageNote`), not appended to `notes`.** `notes` is the model's summary and renders as one paragraph in `.st-msg`, so a sentence glued onto the end of it is buried mid-paragraph — the exact failure already recorded for `contextNote`, where *"couldn't read your link"* ended up inside a run-on. It joins `contextNote`/`imagesNote`/`tokensNote` in the separate note element and is composed in the MODULE, because the client is a plain script that cannot import it and a second copy there drifts toward naming a page that published perfectly well. Empty string when nothing was stubbed, so every clean build's note block is byte-identical to before. **Guarded at BOTH ends** — the route returns it and the client reads it — since either alone passes while the wire is cut.
- **A mutant widening the branch to `built.ok` was ALMOST inert and was still a real gap** — no error text means nothing is stubbed, but every successful build then reports `salvage: { reason: "the error names no page" }`, which reads in the response and in the eval as a build that nearly failed. The assertion is the ABSENCE.
- 1862 tests, site-build 72/72, **16/16 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline.

## The build steps ticked a compile that had just failed (2026-08-09)

**Found in a screenshot of a real build.** The pages did not compile, the site published as the data-model placeholder, and the step block underneath reported **"Compiled React ✓ 17.3s"** and **"Published ✓"** — directly below our own sentence saying the pages had failed. Two contradictory claims in one message, and the tick is the one people believe.

- **EVERY STEP WAS `state: 'done'`, HARDCODED.** Not a bug in the outcome logic — there was no outcome logic. `reactStepsHTML` rendered four ticks whatever happened, so the block was decoration that happened to be right most of the time. There was also no `fail` state to render: `stStepRow` knew `run`, `done` and `wait`, and none of them can say "this did not work".
- **`page` is the answer and it was already in the response** — `'app'` or `'placeholder'`, the same field the ⚠️ wording has keyed off since 2026-08-08. It simply was not carried onto the stored build object, so the renderer could not see it. **The mutation that deleted it survived the whole suite** until a source-read guard existed: the renderer is driven directly and correct, and with the field missing `b.page !== 'placeholder'` is true for everything, so every failed build ticks again with every test green. The wiring layer, for the eighth recorded time.
- **PUBLISHING STAYS A TICK and says which of the two it was** ("Published the data model"). A placeholder IS a real page at a real address — calling that a failure would be the same dishonesty pointed the other way.
- **The failure mark is a SHAPE (`✕`) and not only a colour**, so it survives greyscale, print and anyone who cannot separate the hues — the discipline the component kit already applies to status everywhere else.
- **THE PAGE PICKER HAD THE SAME LIE.** `s.pages` was taken from the files the model WROTE, so a placeholder build read "6 pages" in the header and offered five routes that all served the fallback. It is adopted only when `page !== 'placeholder'` now — right for both failures for different reasons: a failed REVISE leaves the already-published site untouched, so its existing pages are still the true ones, and a failed FIRST build published exactly one placeholder.
- **An older stored build has no `page` and must not read as broken**, since every build in every browser predates this. Asserted, because the safe direction here is the one that hides the bug.
- **Two of my own assertions passed for the wrong reason before they passed for the right one.** `st-step-tick[^]*Could not compile` is satisfied by the "Wrote the code ✓" tick earlier in the same HTML; a regex anchored on `<div class="st-step` then matched the OUTER wrapper and swallowed that row with it. The marker is read between the row header and its label now. And an existing test was pinned to the exact string `if (routed.length) s.pages = routed;`, so a correct change failed a test about word order — re-anchored on the property.
- 1839 tests, **6/6 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline.

## The clarify round dead-ended on its own last answer (2026-08-09)

**Found live, in a screenshot.** Brief *"Book classes"*, two questions answered with the buttons, and the third press came back **"I'm not sure what you'd like me to build. Tell me about your business"** — to somebody who had just told us three times, using our own buttons. Nothing was built, nothing cleared the round, and the interface sat on an already-answered question.

- **THE ROUTER WAS ALLOWED TO ANSWER A BUTTON PRESS WITH PROSE.** A clicked option goes back through `/api/site/route`, and `readRouting` could return `ask` — which renders the model's sentence and returns before `go()`, so no build starts and `site.clarify` is never cleared. **The brief was not lost**: the client sends `brief` and `qa` correctly and `askRequest` folds them in. The model simply classified an answer as a question, and nothing stopped it.
- **`answering` is the fix and it is enforced in CODE, not in the prompt.** A clicked option, or a typed reply while a question is live, is an ANSWER — so the only honest outcomes are another question or the build. It is the same asymmetry the rest of the file is built on (a wrong "build" is visible and undoable, a wrong "ask" is indistinguishable from broken) applied to the one path that was missing it. **It bounds `ask` and NOT `clarify`** — collapsing the two would silently cap the interview at one question, and a mutation proves that separation.
- **The cost, stated:** somebody who interrupts mid-round with a genuine question gets a site instead of an answer. The cheaper mistake, and they keep the site.
- **THE QUESTION WAS ALSO CUT MID-WORD** — `.slice(0, 240)` shipped *"…or hardcore and inte"* on the one message whose whole job is to read like a person talking. `clipOption`, two functions below it, had already solved this properly for the buttons; the text was written with a bare slice and nobody noticed the asymmetry. `clipQuestion` now falls back to a word boundary and trails off with an ellipsis, because a sentence that simply stops reads as a bug. **Nothing is lost when it fires**, since the model's habit is to list the options in prose and the buttons below already carry them — and the tool description now says not to.
- **THREE OF NINE MUTANTS SURVIVED THE FIRST SWEEP, ALL IN THE WIRING** — `worker.js` reading the flag off the body and `chat.js` putting it on the wire. Neither file can be imported, so `readRouting` was unit-tested and correct while the flag never arrived: **the guard watching the layer below the break, for the seventh recorded time.** Closed with source-reads anchored to landmarks rather than byte counts.
- **IT ASKED ITS TWO QUESTIONS IN THE WRONG ORDER, which is why it had nothing to build from.** Rebuilding the exact prompt shows what it was handed: an empty project, the brief *"Book classes"*, and a message that was ALREADY listed above it as the answer to question two — so the one field carrying something new carried nothing. And it was right that it could not build: "Book classes" is a gym, a pottery studio, a driving school or a yoga room, and those are four different sites. It had spent both questions on logins and mood without ever establishing the TRADE. The tool description now says the trade is the first question and nothing is close, with that measured case as the worked example. **Not enforceable in code** — whether a brief names the business is a judgement — so the guard is on the description, which is worth having because a rule nobody asserts is one a later edit quietly drops.
- **NOT PROVEN LIVE.** Reproducing it costs a real build with a clarify round. The unit path is driven end to end with fakes and every mutant dies; the screenshot is the only evidence of the original.
- 1834 tests, **9/9 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline.

## The Builder picker chooses a model now — and Effort deliberately does not (2026-08-08)

**Owner's call: "make the builder one work, the effort thing leave it off — leave it there but doesn't work, i want it like that."** Both halves are decisions and both are pinned by tests, because "we forgot" and "we decided" look identical in a list of controls a year later.

- **THE PICKER WAS READ ZERO TIMES.** `body.picker` appeared nowhere in `worker.js`, and both build calls hardcoded `claude-sonnet-5` — so all three options produced **byte-identical requests**. A shipped control with a tooltip promising a choice, changing nothing. Same class as the Attach button, one surface over. **And the REVISE did not even send the field**, so the one path where somebody has already seen a result and is reaching for a better model was the path that could not ask for one.
- **`auto` WAS REAL FOR ABOUT AN HOUR AND BROKE EVERY NEW ACCOUNT'S FIRST BUILD.** It put the schema call on Opus; a **cold** Opus schema call is **15 credits**, a new account is granted **20**, and `publishPages` refuses to generate below `MIN_CREDITS` 8 — so the customer paid 15 and got a placeholder, `stage: "credits"`. Caught by `build smoke` going red on the first run after the merge (green at 04:20 on the commit before it, red at 04:46 on the commit after). **Owner's call: two options, Sonnet default, `auto` stays out** until the grant covers ~23. Restoring it is three lines and the reason is written down in the module, so it comes back as a decision rather than a rediscovery.
  - **NO UNIT TEST COULD HAVE CAUGHT IT, and that is the lesson.** Every test asserted the picker sends the right model and prices it correctly — all true. Nothing modelled a whole build against a real starting balance. `test/build-models.test.mjs` now does: the default picker's `buildFloor` must fit inside the free grant, and Opus's must not (or the guard is passing because the floor stopped meaning anything).
  - **The fix is an up-front gate, and it is right independently of the default.** `buildFloor(model)` = a measured cold schema call at that model's rates, plus `MIN_CREDITS`. Checked against the balance `use_credits` returned — the real ledger value, no second read to race — and it **refunds the deposit**, because nothing has been spent: this is a refusal, not a failure. **This is not the generation-failed case the charging rule was written for**: the pages model was never called at all, so spending their budget on step one and then declining step two is ours, not theirs. The 402 names the cheaper picker, since "top up" is not the only way out and is the less useful one.
  - **The profile is COLD on purpose.** A gate that under-estimates takes the money and then refuses to finish, which is the exact bug. Over-estimating costs an occasional top-up message to somebody who would just have squeezed through warm — the cheap direction. Pinned, because a well-meaning "use the warm numbers, they're more typical" reintroduces the whole thing.
- **`builder/build-models.mjs` is the allow-list**, and the two halves (`design`, `pages`) are named separately because they are not the same job — `design` turns one sentence into a data model the whole site is built on, `pages` writes ~10,000 tokens of TSX against rules that already state most of the answer. Both entries send one model to each today; the split is kept because it is what a mixed picker needs and collapsing it would have to be undone to bring one back. Requires a **string**, not anything stringifiable — `String(["opus"])` is `"opus"`, the `normalizeRole` lesson — and `Object.hasOwn`, because `BUILD_MODELS["constructor"]` is truthy and that exact bug already shipped once in the Stripe plan lookup.
- **THE RATE TABLE HAD TO GO PER-MODEL IN THE SAME CHANGE, or this ships a silent 67% undercharge on output** the day after "charge for what you use". `MODEL_RATES` is Opus 5 / Sonnet 5 / Haiku 4.5 at list; a usage object now carries the `model` it was priced at, **read back off the request that was sent** so the bill and the call cannot disagree. Measured on one warm build: **sonnet 28 credits · auto 30 · opus 47.**
- **`sumUsage` became `totalCost`, and that is not a rename.** It merged the four token counts into one object and priced the result — correct exactly while every call in a build was Sonnet. Under `auto` the designer is Opus and the pages are Sonnet, so a merged object has **no honest rate to be priced at**: adding the tokens charges one call at the other's price. It sums DOLLARS now, and `pageCredits` still rounds ONCE, which is the property the old shape existed for.
- **An unpriced model fails DEAR, and the direction is the decision.** Only our own code can put a model id there, so that branch means somebody wired a model and forgot to price it. Failing cheap is a silent undercharge that looks exactly like a working platform; failing dear shows on the meter the first time and gets fixed. The dearest rate is derived **per column** — one hand-picked row satisfies today's table and stops being dearest the moment a model arrives that is cheaper on input and dearer on output.
- **The router's Haiku call was billed at Sonnet rates** the whole time — invisible only because a routing call rounds up to the one-credit floor either way.
- **Research does NOT follow the picker**, pinned with its reason: it is a factual lookup, the step where the model matters least, and the server-side search tool is **versioned per model** — moving it risks breaking searching entirely in exchange for nothing anybody sees.
- **Three mutation survivors, and two were real gaps of mine — all the same shape.** A source-reading window that runs to a distant landmark swallows the NEXT function, so a mutation deleting the designer's own `model: req.model` was satisfied by the pages call's copy a hundred lines below. `buildEffortHTML()` matched its own *definition*, so dropping the call out of the composer left the control gone from the screen and the guard green — the Bookmarks-comment failure again. And two assertions about one constant have to name DIFFERENT lines or one answers the other. **An overlapping window is this session's recurring bug**, next to "strip comments by blanking, never by removing".
- 1521 tests, 24/24 mutations from a verified-green baseline. **Not proven live AT THE TIME** — the account had no balance then. Topped up 2026-08-08; not re-verified since.

## A deep platform audit, and what it found (2026-08-08)

**Eleven parallel auditors over the builder and the platform, then an adversarial skeptic on every medium-or-worse finding.** 13 confirmed, 1 refuted, 38 low/notes. Everything below is fixed; the value is in the SHAPES, because every one of them is a shape this file already warned about somewhere else.

- **THE LEDGER IS A GATE, NOT A TILL, and the whole charging tier was written as though it were one.** `use_credits`'s WHERE clause is `balance >= cost`, so a bill larger than the balance **debits ZERO and returns -1** — it never overdrafts and never throws for insufficiency. Every call site that GATES read that -1 and refused; every site that SETTLED after the work was done discarded it. So the platform silently collected NOTHING whenever the bill outgrew the balance, and reported `charged: true`. **It was the commonest path there is**: a new account is granted 20, the deposit and schema settlement leave ~11, a warm pages call prices at ~21 — so every new account's first build published a real site and moved the ledger by nothing. `collectCredits` asks for the bill and, on a refusal, reads the balance and takes what is there. **`out.cost` is now what LEFT the ledger and `out.billed` is what the work cost** — conflating them is what let this hide. The harness fake was `async () => {}`, more capable than the real dependency: the `setTotp` lesson, one module over, and the reason the dep contract now documents a RETURN.
- **`vidRefN` was a live `ReferenceError`** — declared inside `if (kind !== "audio")` and read ~40 lines after that block closed, so every director call with a Seedance clip attached threw, **after the fee was debited and outside every try/catch**, and the client's `catch { return localAsk(text) }` swallowed it. The whole Seedance video-reference feature had been dead and silently charging since it shipped. **Nothing could see it**: `node --check` passes, no test can import a Worker entrypoint, and **esbuild does NOT catch it** — verified against a minimal repro. The new scan walks brace depth from each block-scoped declaration; it is narrowed to camelCase names because the first draft flagged `owner`, `live`, `total`, `reply`, all false alarms from property names and from PROSE inside the director's prompt templates.
- **A whole-file comment blanker CANNOT be used on worker.js.** A stray `/*` inside a string makes it eat **46% of the file** — measured while building the client↔server drift check. That is the over-blanking trap already recorded for the model-prompt scanner, at ten times the scale, and it is why the reachability guard matches extracted route MATCHERS rather than the file text.
- **Two delete buttons deleted nothing.** `/api/site/backend/delete` and `/api/site/backend/delete-all` have never existed; both 404s were swallowed by a `catch {}` and the local record dropped anyway, so the published site, its Neon database and its claimed slug kept running while the customer was told they were permanently removed — and with the local record gone there was no UI left to retry. The real `DELETE /api/site/<slug>` had **zero callers**. Its body is `deleteSiteFor` now, shared with a new `POST /api/site/delete-all`; **order is the whole point**, since `uid` cascades with `auth.users` and a site whose row has gone can no longer be authorised for deletion at all.
- **Every revise re-bought all the photographs** — same family budget, fresh descriptions, so nothing matched what was bought last time: ~94 credits per revise on a 5-photo agency site, for pictures the owner already had. A revise buys none; zero rather than "reuse", because matching a new description to an old file is a guess and a wrong guess puts the wrong picture on the page.
- **`neon_auth` was not in the model-function deny-list.** Those `_something` names are ours and most went with the hand-built auth layer — identity today is `neon_auth."user"/.session/.account`, matching none of them, so **the list guarded deleted tables while the live session store stood open**. Every model function is SECURITY DEFINER and a non-`internal` one is granted to `anonymous`.
- **`require()` passed every check and threw in the browser.** `tsc` accepts it (Node's types declare it), vite bundles it, the site publishes, and the section dies at the error boundary. It had `build smoke` red at HEAD. Linted like the `fetch` rule beside it.
- **A revise re-rolled theme, family and fonts from the instruction alone.** The fallback chain's own comment claimed `body.theme`/`body.family`/`body.fonts` anchored the look — **the client has never sent any of them**, so the anchor did not exist. The look is stored once in the site's own `_meta` and preferred on every revise.
- **The `Math.min(10, …)` on every refund was not dead headroom.** `credit_back` caps a call at 10 and a cold Opus schema settles to 15, so the one path the rule says refunds IN FULL returned 10 and kept 5.
- **Smaller, same family:** `app_team_id` could be shadowed by a declared function (the guard named one helper when it meant a namespace — it is on the `app_` prefix now); `callApi` followed redirects, making the SSRF guard a check on the first hop only; `internal: true` functions were listed in `schemaDigest` as callable; generated photographs bypassed the owner's 200-file upload allowance; `resolveTheme("__proto__")` returned `{}`; the chart catalogue listed data FABRICATORS as chart components; `useApi` was the one hook in `rows.ts` no rule ever mentioned, so the whole third-party tier was reachable by nothing; a `@@` inside a description corrupted a paid-for photograph's URL; and a correct `<Link>` to a nested index route was rewritten to `/`.
- **THE ONE REFUTED FINDING IS WORTH KEEPING**: the router billing a whole credit for a $0.0015 Haiku call is the documented 1-credit floor, deliberate and tested. A skeptic that confirms everything is not a skeptic.
- **My own fixes then failed their mutation sweep 9 times out of 24.** Every one was a test gap, not a code bug — including a source-read window that ended at a comment sitting ABOVE the function it guarded, so it matched nothing and passed vacuously. 1661 tests, 23/24 mutants caught (the 24th was an anchor that would not apply; verified by hand).

## Generated sites get real photographs, budgeted per page type (2026-08-08)

**Owner's call: "DEFAULT DEPENDS ON THE TYPE OF PAGE, BUT FIRST BUILD KEEP THE CAP AT 6."** `builder/site-images.mjs` is the whole rule as a plain module; the Worker only wires the fal call and the R2 put.

- **ONE IMAGE IS ~19 CREDITS AND A WHOLE WARM BUILD IS 21**, so a single photograph roughly doubles the price of a site. Nearly every line in that module is about not spending it, and that ratio is asserted by a test — if an image ever gets cheap the derivation is far more conservative than it needs to be.
- **The budget is DERIVED from what `site-layouts.mjs` already declares**, not hand-authored: 100 families × ~3 real pages is 324 judgement calls that go stale the first time a family moves. A `terminal` family gets none anywhere (LAYOUTS.md's own words for that structure are "no imagery, no decoration"); the home page gets the opening image, **two** where the structure is built around one (`editorial`, `full-bleed-hero`); any other page gets one only where the family's own `components` name a gallery. Measured over the 100 ready families: **0→5 sites · 1→56 · 2→14 · 3-5→25, average 1.72** — so an average build goes 21 → 53 credits and an agency 21 → 114.
- **`safe-image` is deliberately NOT the picture-led signal** even though 26 families name it: it is the guard every image goes through, so it says a page HAS a picture and nothing about whether pictures are what the page is FOR. Reading it as the signal budgets a photograph on a pricing page.
- **A page may override with its own `img`, and four do.** The derivation reads the FAMILY's component list, so `salon/work` — *"a gallery of finished sets, because nails are a visual trade"* — got nothing while being the most visual page on the site. Also `estate-agent/listing`, `store/product`, `pet-boarding/stay`. **Reading the role TEXT instead was tried and rejected on measurement**: scanning 324 roles for picture words finds 12 and four are false (a quote form where the VISITOR sends photographs, a page that "answers plainly"), and a rule wrong on a third of its hits is worse than one that is merely narrow.
- **IT DEGRADES TO ZERO RATHER THAN REFUSING, and that is the load-bearing decision.** `imagesAffordable` subtracts what THIS build's model calls really cost — measured, not estimated, because images are decided after generation — and buys out of what is left. A new account granted 20 credits affords none and gets exactly the site it would have got the day before. **A floor that included even one image would refuse every new account's first build**, which is precisely the regression the Builder picker's `auto` shipped that morning, at a fifth of this scale.
- **The token is `@@IMG:describe the picture@@` in a `SafeImage` src, and THE TAG IS THE WHOLE RULE.** An unbought token becomes an empty `src`, which `SafeImage` draws as its designed placeholder and anything else draws as a broken icon — so `lintPages` refuses one in any other tag. A sweep clears every remaining token shape, since the literal text `@@IMG:...@@` in a bundle is both a broken image and a visible leak of how the site was made. A token with **no description is dropped rather than sent**: $0.15 to find out what an image model does with an empty prompt.
- **The allowance rides in the USER message beside the layout directive**, never the ~27,000-token cached system block — a number that changes per build would miss that cache every build (measured at 13× the input cost on the family exemplar). `images` is OMITTED rather than defaulted, so the eval and every other caller sends the request it sent before this existed; `images: 0` is a real instruction and `undefined` is the absence of one, which a truthiness check would collapse.
- **RULE 7 WAS REWRITTEN WHILE IT WAS OPEN, and this is the useful part.** `safe-image.tsx` was written specifically to make forgetting the guard impossible — *"GENERATOR.md rule 7 exists entirely because of this... this makes forgetting impossible instead"* — and the rules went on teaching the manual `{row.photo ? <img …` guard it replaced, so the component sat in the kit unused. **The test asserting the guard's wording is what kept it there.** It asserts the invariant now: point at the component, and never hand the model a bare `<img>` to copy.
- **Billed through the same `settle` and the same `ourFault` rule as tokens**, priced from the same one table, rounded ONCE with them. The cost of that, stated rather than hidden: a build whose pages fail to **typecheck** is a charged stage and the pictures have already been bought, so somebody can pay for six photographs and be shown the placeholder page. Bounded by where they land — `uploads/<slug>/`, the owner's own image library, which a publish deliberately does not wipe.
- **Four outcomes render an IDENTICAL placeholder** — never wanted, could not afford, the image model failed, wanted more than the cap — so `imageNote` separates them in one server-composed sentence (the `contextSentence` shape: the client cannot import the module, and a second copy there drifts toward claiming photographs that were never made). This is why `planned` travels beside `budget`: the budget has already been cut by the balance and on its own cannot say whether anything was ever wanted.
- **1632 tests, 42/42 mutations from a verified-green baseline.** Two survivors were real gaps: every lint case used `<img>`, so `!tag.startsWith("S")` passed the entire suite (the kit is full of `Section`/`Skeleton`/`SiteChrome`); and every billing test set `made === budget`, so charging the BUDGET was indistinguishable from charging what was stored — three budgeted and one stored is the ordinary shape of a partial image-model failure.
- **THE FIRST LIVE BUILD SHIPPED A BROKEN IMAGE, and the failure was in NOT CALLING the thing that was correct.** `buySitePhotos` returned `pages` untouched when there was nothing to buy, so the sweep never ran, `SafeImage` saw a truthy `src` and the published home page rendered the broken-image glyph with its alt text showing. `applyImages` was tested directly and does exactly the right thing; nothing tested that it was REACHED. **It bit on the commonest path there is** — a new account is granted 20 credits and a build costs ~21, so `affordable` is 0 and that branch is what EVERY first build takes: the graceful-degradation path the whole feature is designed around was the one path that degraded to a broken page. One exit now, and the guard counts the EXITS rather than looking for the call, because "applyImages appears somewhere in the function" was true the whole time it was broken. Found by `build smoke` going red on *"no console errors"* and then by LOOKING at the screenshot — the log line alone could not say what had 404'd.
- **`SafeImage` ALONE WAS TOO NARROW A LINT, and the same build proved it**: it reported `work.tsx: writes a @@IMG:@@ token inside <Gallery>` — and `Gallery` draws through `SafeImage`, so the model was right and got told off. **42 kit components render their picture through it**, and those are exactly the ones a model reaches for on a page that wants a photograph; refusing them teaches it to hand-roll an `<img>`, which is the one thing that really does break. `SAFE_IMAGE_COMPONENTS` is derived from the kit and a test re-reads every ui file, both directions.
- **NOT PROVEN LIVE — no photograph has ever been generated.** Two independent reasons: the fal balance is empty, and the smoke test's throwaway account holds the 20-credit grant, so `imagesAffordable` correctly returns 0 before fal is ever called. What IS proven live is everything around it — the model writes the tokens and says so in its own reply, the lint reads them, the sweep clears them, and the site publishes intact with placeholders. Nothing is charged for a picture that fails, since `made` counts only successful puts.

## The builder writes its own reply now, out of a field it was already paying for (2026-08-08)

**Owner wanted a real reply and wanted it cheap.** Both, because the field already existed: `write_pages` has always carried `notes`, the model has always written it, the route has always returned it, and **the client rendered it nowhere.** Every build since the React engine shipped has paid for a few dozen tokens of prose no human read.

- **Rewording it costs one deploy's cold cache and ~0.098 credits a build** — measured, and it lands inside the credit rounding, so a warm build is 21 credits before and 21 after. $78 per 100,000 builds. It was "anything the brief asked for that was left out, and why. Empty if nothing was"; it is now two or three plain sentences to the customer, written *to* them.
- **Length is capped by the DESCRIPTION, which is the only free lever.** Output is billed per token at 5x input; a `maxLength` in the schema would truncate mid-sentence instead of shortening. The cap is asserted by a test on the description, not on the output.
- **IT ALSO FIXED A LIE.** A placeholder build answers `ok:true` **with a slug**, so the canned line said *"✅ Built "Sharp Fade Barbers". Tell me what to change."* over the data-model fallback — claiming a site that does not exist, on the one path where the customer most needs to know. `page` is the only field that tells them apart (`"app"` vs `"placeholder"`), and `notes` on that path is already our own sentence explaining the failure. The reply is `✅` + the model's summary when a real app published, and `⚠️` + the explanation when it did not.
- **The proportions, measured, for the next time this comes up.** A warm build is 92.8% page source, 5.0% cache reads, 2.2% fresh input, ~1.2% the reply. Cold cache is **32 credits against 21** — worth more than fifty chatty replies, and the reason an edit to `PAGE_RULES` is not free. If cost ever needs to move, it moves in the page source or the cache, never in the sentence.

## An attachment is just an attachment (2026-08-08)

**Owner's call, after two corrections of mine.** *"I just want it treated normal, like an attachment — the user will say what he wants that for, it's part of the conversation."*

- **THE NOTE IS TWO CLAUSES, AND IT SHRANK TWICE TO GET THERE.** Draft one assigned a purpose per file type ("a logo is a palette, a PDF is real content — use its prices faithfully"), which is wrong in a way that is invisible until it bites: the same PDF is the customer's own price list or a competitor's brochure, and **nothing about the file distinguishes them.** Draft two explained at length how to work that out instead — whose material is it, what to do when the brief is silent — which is teaching a frontier model something it already knows. An attachment in a conversation is an ordinary thing and the person attaching it says what it is for. What survives is only what the model cannot get from the message itself: that the files are part of the request, and which of this prompt's two "references" wins when they disagree. **A test asserts the note stays under 220 characters and names no file format**, because guidance creeps back one well-meaning sentence at a time.
- **It no longer asks for seeding, which this step cannot do.** An earlier line said to put content from an attachment into the matching table's seed rows. `write_pages` takes route files and nothing else — seeds come from the DESIGNER's `seed` field, a call that happens earlier. Unactionable instructions are worse than absent ones. **Known gap, not closed:** the designer sees attached TEXT (folded into its brief) but not images or PDFs, so a PDF menu cannot reach the seed rows at all today. Closing it means paying for those tokens on the flat-fee schema call.
- **Three fates, decided by what the model can be SHOWN.** Images and PDFs go as content blocks (PDF is native to the Messages API — `{type:"document", source:{type:"base64", media_type:"application/pdf"}}`, before the text block). Text-ish files are read as words and folded into the brief, needing no new API surface. **Everything else is NAMED to the customer with a reason** — silently dropping a file somebody deliberately attached is the same failure as silently not fetching a link they pasted.
- **THE API TAKES NO VIDEO AND NO AUDIO, in any encoding.** There is no content block for either. The client extracts a **still frame** in the browser (plain `<video>` + canvas — a seek and a `drawImage`, not the ffmpeg engine) at ~1s in, because a first frame is very often black, and it is reported as *"Used a still frame from promo.mp4"*: saying we used the clip overstates it. A codec Chrome cannot decode becomes a named refusal.
- **The file picker carries no `accept` filter, deliberately** — it was hiding PDFs and videos from the dialog, which answers the question before the user asks it.
- **The same dead-guard bug appeared TWICE from one cause:** raising a per-file cap without re-deriving the total, so the total could never be reached. It reads in the source as a bound on the outbound request and is not one; a mutation found it both times and no test did. 8 MB now, and **both branches can reach it**, asserted separately — a sweep proved the image branch's copy was unexercised because every total test happened to use PDFs.
- **AN INTERRUPTED MUTATION SWEEP LEAVES A LIVE MUTANT IN THE TREE.** The restore is in a `finally`, which a kill skips. It happened: `NOT_A_HOST` stayed stripped out of the URL guard and the next run failed three tests pointing at a feature nobody had touched. The sweep writes a **sidecar** before each mutation and repairs what an abandoned run left — the sidecar is the part that matters, being the only mechanism that survives a process that never runs a handler. **Never commit while a sweep is running.**
- **A MUTANT THAT CHANGES NO BEHAVIOUR REPORTS A TEST GAP THAT IS NOT THERE.** Two survivors came out of the last sweep and only one was real. The other anchored on `const IMAGE_URL = ` and replaced it with `const IMAGE_URL_UNUSED = <wide regex>; const IMAGE_URL = `, which declares an unused twin and leaves the guard exactly as it was — so it "survived" a suite that refuses `image/svg+xml`, `text/html` and `application/zip` by name. **This is the mirror of the failure the sweep exists to find**: a test passing against a broken implementation reads as coverage that is absent, and a no-op mutation reads as an absence of coverage that is present — and the second one costs a hunt through tests that were fine. Anchor on the whole expression being broken, never on the name in front of it. The genuine survivor was the attachment note's precedence clause, asserted nowhere; the ordering check for it goes through the both-anchors-exist helper, since `indexOf(a) < indexOf(b)` passes vacuously when `a` is the thing deleted.
- **The attachment chip's first render cut the filename to "spri…"** — the one piece of information it exists to carry — because `.st-att` is a fixed 56px square and a `max-width` on the inner span cannot widen it. Caught by looking at it.
- 1454 tests, 7/7 mutations caught on the reply change from a verified-green baseline (43/43 on the attachment work before it). **Not proven live AT THE TIME** — the account had no balance then. Topped up 2026-08-08; not re-verified since.

## The four things the model cannot do for a site (2026-08-07)

**Owner's direction: build the capabilities a model cannot reach by itself.** All four are here for the SAME two walls, and that pair is the test to apply to anything proposed next: a published page is public files on R2 so it cannot hold a key, and Postgres has no HTTP client on Neon so model-written SQL cannot make the call. Needs a credential AND a network call → platform. Everything else stays the model's.

- **Spam protection** (`site-turnstile.mjs`) — Cloudflare Turnstile. Owner pastes `TURNSTILE_SITE_KEY` + `TURNSTILE_SECRET`; `GET /api/db/<slug>/turnstile` serves the PUBLIC half so switching it on needs no rebuild. **NOT a kit component, deliberately**: the guard mounts once at the app root (`src/lib/spam-guard.tsx`) and `useCreateRow` attaches the token, because a `<SpamGuard />` the model must remember is one it will eventually forget on a form whose owner believes it is protected — the `safe-image` lesson. **Fails OPEN** (an outage or a wrong secret must not close a business's contact form) and is scoped to `collect`, or turning it on would refuse every member write. The token is STRIPPED from the row on every table whether or not the site is configured — it is not a declared column, so PostgREST would refuse the insert.
- **Inbound webhooks** (`site-inbound.mjs`) — `POST /api/db/<slug>/hook/<name>` → `hook_<name>(payload jsonb)`, model-written. **Fails CLOSED**, the opposite call from spam protection and for a stated reason: this WRITES for a stranger, so no secret stored is a 404 — the same answer an undeclared hook gets, because "exists but unauthorized" tells somebody which integrations a business runs. **The `hook_` prefix is a security boundary, not a convention**: without it the shared secret is the only thing between a caller and `confirm_booking(id)`. Shared secret or HMAC over the RAW body (re-serialising a parse fails every real signature and passes every hand-built one). A failing function answers 5xx so the sender retries; idempotency is the model's `ON CONFLICT`, not ours.
- **Third-party reads** (`site-apis.mjs`) — the site declares the WHOLE request with `{{SECRET}}` placeholders, substituted from its own vault; `useApi("rates", {base})`. One primitive, so the next integration is a line in a schema rather than a line in the Worker. Params are an allow-list and URL-ENCODED (unencoded, an `&` appends a query parameter of the caller's choosing to the owner's request). A missing secret REFUSES rather than sending `Bearer ` — which some APIs answer 200 to with degraded data, so the page would render something plausible and wrong. Nothing comes back from upstream but status and body. **A text/plain upstream reaches the page as a JSON string** (the route always `Response.json`s), which is the contract and was learned from a live run, not reasoned.
- **SMS** (`site-sms.mjs`) — `sms: {fn}` beside `confirm: {fn}`, on the owner's own Twilio / MessageBird / Vonage. Separate declaration so a site can have both. The number parser is deliberately unforgiving: `+` then 8-15 digits, nothing inferred, because a wrong-but-valid number delivers somebody's booking to a stranger. Twilio needs BOTH halves or that provider is skipped. **Vonage answers HTTP 200 on failure** — its `messages[].status` decides, or every rejected message reports as sent.

**Live coverage, and what is missing.** `confirm smoke` proves inbound webhooks (a real delivery lands as a real row, a tampered signature is refused, a retry does not duplicate), third-party reads (a keyed read refuses 503 with nothing stored and goes through once it is stored — that PAIR is what proves substitution), and spam protection's SERVER half using **Cloudflare's own published test secrets**, at $0. **SMS has no live proof and cannot have a free one** — a real Twilio account, real money per message. **The page half of spam protection is also uncovered.**

**`_meta` dropped `internal` and killed every inbound hook the hour it shipped.** `applySiteSchema` wrote `{name, args, returns}`, and `_meta` is the only copy the request path sees. Build fine, function in Postgres, feature unreachable — the sixth recorded instance of that shape. Now guarded at BOTH ends, which is the only form that cannot rot: the reader's requirement and the writer's output are asserted to agree.

## Site navigation was dead for a day, and the rule that killed it was true when it was written (2026-08-09)

**Moving the router to browser history made every nav link on every generated site a no-op**, and the change that did it shipped without touching the chrome it broke. Clicking "Book" in the header changed the URL fragment and rendered nothing.

- **`#/book` WAS CORRECT.** Under `createHashHistory()` the fragment was where the route lived, and `site-header.tsx`'s comment argued for it at length — *"a hash href is real client-side navigation"*, against `href="/"`, which really was a full reload to the server root. The router moved and the argument silently inverted. **A rule that is true because of something one layer down expires when that layer moves, and nothing announces it.**
- **NOTHING COULD SEE IT.** `tsc` sees a string, vite bundles it, `lintPages` had no opinion, the site publishes. `test/integration/site-routing.mjs` drove `/book` in a real browser at both mounts and passed throughout — **because it typed the address in rather than pressing the thing a visitor presses.** It clicks now, and that assertion goes red against the restored bug on both mounts.
- **ONE OF MY OWN THREE CLICK ASSERTIONS PASSED AGAINST THE BUG**, found by mutation: "the BOOK route rendered" matched `/Book a chair/i`, which is the label of the button just clicked and therefore on the HOME page too. Anchored on text only the destination has.
- **THE BLAST RADIUS WAS THE EXEMPLARS, NOT THE CHROME.** 4 reference pages and 318 family exemplars, 1,465 hrefs — the model learns the shape by copying those, so fixing the two components alone would have been undone by the examples. Plus 8 `location.hash = "#/x"` navigations, the same bug written imperatively.
- **`href:` WAS THE WRONG THING TO KEY ON.** The first sweep matched `href: "#/…"` and missed `manualHref`, `agendaHref`, `minutesHref` and a ternary. **What identifies this is `"#/` at the start of a string literal, never the prop name** — and `#prices` is left alone, because a fragment naming an id on the same page is an ordinary in-page anchor that still works. 266 of those survive untouched.
- **`SiteLink` is the fix that makes one value correct everywhere.** An internal path routes through `<Link>` (basepath applied at runtime), anything else stays a plain anchor, because an Instagram profile in a footer is a real thing. **`//evil.example` is deliberately not internal** — it starts with a slash and is another origin, the one shape a naive `startsWith("/")` gets wrong; the `safeNext` lesson, one layer over.
- **The lint is the durable half**, because the model's training data still has the old shape and the kit cannot stop a page writing a raw anchor. Two rules — the href form and the `location.hash` form — the second because a rule that only reads hrefs teaches the model to reach for the other one.
- **`src/family-pages` IS TYPECHECKED BY NOTHING**, found on the way in and NOT fixed. It is excluded from `tsconfig.json` and absent from `tsconfig.kit.json`. A blanket pass is not merely missing: all 318 declare `createFileRoute("/about")`-style paths that do not exist in the template's own route tree, so 207 errors are inherent and a real check needs a stubbed tree per family. Verified my own edits by isolating that error class — **0 errors of any other kind** — which is a measurement, not a check that will hold tomorrow.
- **The `#/` convention was baked into a TEST**, whose comment called hash history "the published-site reality". It went red the moment the exemplars were corrected, which is the test working — but it would have gone green forever had the convention never been touched. **Assert the property, not the spelling.**
- 1828 tests, site-routing 21/21 (both mounts, click included), site-build 67/67, **7/8 mutations caught**. The survivor is understood and recorded: a prefix-match loosening that today's corpus cannot discriminate.

## Every site has its own subdomain — `gofarther.app` (2026-08-09)

**Owner bought a second domain and customer sites move onto it: `<slug>.gofarther.app`, unless the owner brings their own.** The platform stays on `gofarther.dev`. **LIVE 2026-08-09** — built dark the same day and switched on within the hour once the zone was in the account.

- **THE FOUR PRECONDITIONS WERE MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED, and the order is the whole lesson.** (1) the zone is in the SAME Cloudflare account — proved by the deploy's read-only report finding it (`ok zone found`), which is the only thing that can answer it since a token sees nothing outside its account; (2) the zone is Active — the same report warns loudly otherwise and did not; (3) `*` and `@` both `AAAA 100::` and both PROXIED; (4) **the certificate exists** — a real HTTPS request to a subdomain came back `ssl_verify_result=0`, so Universal SSL had issued and covers the wildcard. The `522` underneath it was Cloudflare finding no origin, which is exactly what the route then fixed. Flipping before (4) is the failure that reads worst to a visitor: a TLS warning rather than a 404.

- **ONE SWITCH, AND BOTH HALVES ARE HELD TOGETHER BY A TEST.** `SITE_ZONE_LIVE` in `site-domains.mjs` and the commented Worker route in `wrangler.jsonc` must flip in the same commit; `test/site-zone.test.mjs` asserts they agree in **both** directions. Half of it is a live failure either way — a route naming a zone the account does not hold **fails the routes PUT and therefore the whole deploy** (measured 2026-08-07, three merges lost, error naming an unrelated feature), and the flag on with no route hands every customer a link that times out. The route matcher skips commented lines, and a second test proves it still SEES the app zone's live route — matching the string alone would pass against the instructions in that same file, which is the vacuous-assertion shape this repo keeps recording.
- **`isOwnHostname` NOW MEANS TWO ZONES, AND THAT IS THE TRAP IN THE CHANGE.** `isAppHostname` is the narrow question it used to answer by accident. `mountRoot` had to move to it: `isOwnHostname` is true for `<slug>.gofarther.app`, which would prefix every asset with `/s/<slug>/` on a mount whose root is `/` — every script and stylesheet on the new zone, 404. Asserted as an absence as well as a presence, since only one of the two predicates can be right there.
- **`gofarther.app` IS IN `OWN_ZONES`, and that is the security line, not tidiness.** Left claimable, somebody registers `sharp-fade.gofarther.app` as their *custom domain* pointing at their own slug — two rows answering for one hostname, and whichever the lookup finds first wins. The subdomain space is ours to hand out.
- **ONE LABEL ONLY.** Universal SSL covers `gofarther.app` and `*.gofarther.app` and nothing deeper, so `a.b.gofarther.app` has no certificate and can never be served — matching it would route a request the browser then refuses, which reads as the site being broken. **That also makes these free**: subdomains of a zone we own need no Cloudflare for SaaS and consume none of the 100-custom-hostname allowance, which stays for owners bringing their own domain.
- **`siteLabelFor` and `siteHostFor` are TWO functions because a mutation proved they had to be.** Folded together, every assertion about which slugs can be a hostname passed **vacuously** while the zone was dark — the flag returned null first, so deleting the label rules outright changed nothing any test could see, and they would have come back to life untested on the day of the flip. One answers "can this slug be a hostname" (testable today), the other "should we show it yet". `labelOk` is also the WHOLE second-label check: an explicit `includes(".")` beside it could never fire, and was removed rather than left as protection that reads real and is not.
- **A build slug is already `[a-z0-9-]` capped at 60**, so it is a legal DNS label with one exception the filter permits and DNS does not — a leading or trailing hyphen. Such a site keeps `/s/<slug>/` rather than being offered an address that cannot resolve.
- **The site zone is decided BEFORE the custom-domain lookup**, and it costs no I/O: the slug is in the hostname, where a custom domain costs a KV read or a Supabase round trip. `/api/` is excluded from the rewrite for the reason custom domains already had — a published bundle calls its own API same-origin and the matchers key on pathname. The bare `gofarther.app` **301s to the app** rather than serving a second copy of the workspace, and `/api/` is excluded from the redirect too, because a 301 on a POST is followed inconsistently.
- **A SEPARATE REGISTRABLE DOMAIN IS WORTH HAVING ON ITS OWN.** Model-written page code runs on `gofarther.app`, so a stored XSS on a customer's published site is same-origin with nothing that matters — the owner's session is on `gofarther.dev` and cookies do not cross a registrable boundary.
- **The client holds a guarded COPY of the two constants**, against this repo's usual preference for composing on the server. Measured against the alternative: a per-site address computed at build time is stale for every site built before the flip, so every existing customer would keep seeing the old link until they happened to make a change. `test/site-zone.test.mjs` reads both files.
- **`.github/workflows/site-zone-setup.yml`** does the DNS (`*` and `@`, both originless `AAAA 100::`, both **proxied** — grey-clouded, `100::` is dialled directly and every site times out), dry run by default, and **refuses to overwrite a record it did not create**. It reports the route rather than making one, because wrangler publishes routes with a PUT that deletes any it was not told about. Its step-3 wording differs before and after its precondition: a pending step that reads the same either way is one nobody acts on at the right moment.
- **Fixed on the way, because it made the new address untestable:** the page picker and the copied URL still addressed pages with `#`, left from the 2026-08-09 router move — picking a page changed the label and left the preview on the home page, and the chip printed a link that opens the home page for whoever it is sent to. Both now use real paths. **The preview iframe deliberately stays on `/s/<slug>/`** (same-origin with the builder); the public host is what the chip shows.
- 1821 tests, **14/14 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline. Two survivors on the first sweep were both real: the vacuous `siteHostFor` assertions above, and a guard that could not fire.

## Custom domains, and one-click DNS (2026-08-07)

**A published site serves on the owner's own address.** `site-domains.mjs` (what may be claimed), `site-dns.mjs` (is it pointing here yet, over DoH), `site-registrar.mjs` (whose DNS is it — 31 providers by nameserver), `site-domain-connect.mjs` (the one-click flow). Cloudflare for SaaS issues the certificate; the Worker rewrites a custom `Host` to the site's slug at the top of `handleRequest`, guarded by `isOwnHostname` so nobody can claim `gofarther.dev` and be served in place of the whole platform. The panel is Cloud → Domains.

- **PROVEN END TO END 2026-08-07, and it took FOUR fixes to get there.** A real Cloudflare custom hostname registered on a real session: 200 with Cloudflare's own ownership TXT, then the ACME challenge records on the next read, correct apex detection, `dns: "missing"` from the DoH check, 409 on a duplicate, 401 unauthenticated, `DELETE` releasing the hostname, and a second delete answering an honest 404. **Every one of the four bugs was invisible to the unit suite AND to a live probe**, and each hid the next — which is the argument for driving a real session through a feature rather than reading it.
  1. **`dm2` was missing from the dispatch condition** — the route fell through to the router's 404. Indistinguishable from `assertOwner`'s deliberate 404-not-403.
  2. **`if (!g.ok)` on `assertOwner`, which has no `ok` field** — `{}` on success, `{error: Response}` on failure. So it 404'd its own owner, with the same status and body as bug 1 for a completely different reason. Separated only by `/notify` and `/analytics` answering 200 for the same slug and token.
  3. **A `Response` assigned to `r`** where the block ends `return Response.json(r.body, …)` — `.body` is a ReadableStream, so the GET served `200 {}` and the POST threw a 500 AFTER inserting the row. The direct `return`s in the same branch were correct throughout, so every refusal worked and only the successes were broken.
  4. **`OWN_ZONES` was never imported into `worker.js`** — `cfZoneId` reads it on its first line, outside every try, so the ReferenceError propagated past both of `cfHostname`'s catches. **Every Cloudflare custom-hostname call the platform ever made threw before reaching the API.** Not a syntax error, `node --check` passes, and the unit suite cannot import a Worker entrypoint. Found by making the 500 carry the error's CLASS (and, for a `ReferenceError` alone, its message — always "X is not defined", never request data).
  - Guarded by `test/worker-imports.test.mjs` (every site-module export worker.js references must be imported, derived at both ends), plus reachability and `assertOwner`-contract checks in `api-auth`/`site-owner`. **Two lessons from building that scan**: it needs two views of the source — imports visible when collecting scope, stripped when looking for usage — and a first draft that blanked string literals to keep model prompts out of the results had to be deleted, because hand-lexing nested templates got them wrong and an over-blanking scanner HIDES real code, which is the direction that costs a bug rather than a false alarm.
- **THE WHOLE API WAS UNREACHABLE UNTIL 2026-08-07, and a live probe could not tell.** `dm2` — the `/api/site/<slug>/domains` matcher — was missing from the `if (om || mm || …)` condition guarding the block its own handler sits in, so every request fell through to the router's 404. The panel called it, the Cloudflare zone was configured for it, the answer was always 404. **Indistinguishable from correct behaviour from outside**, because `assertOwner` answers 404 for a slug that is not yours. **`api-auth.test.mjs` could not see it either**: it asserts each route sits behind `authUser`, and this one did — inside a block it could never enter, which is the guard watching the layer below the break. The check now runs matchers → condition AND condition → matchers, because the first direction alone passes when the SCAN stops seeing a matcher (its count check is a floor, not a census). Found by being asked to add a domain and test it; fixed, verified live as 401 on GET/POST/DELETE.
- **`OWN_ZONES` is the single most important line in `site-domains.mjs`.** Requests route by `Host`, so a stored row saying `gofarther.dev → some-slug` replaces the app, the sign-in page and every API route. Matched as a suffix **anchored on a dot**, or `notgofarther.dev` stops being claimable by whoever actually owns it.
- **DOMAIN CONNECT IS THE AUTO-CONFIGURE, and the owner found it, not me** — I had concluded per-provider integration was mostly unbuildable and was wrong. The open standard (GoDaddy + IONOS wrote it) needs **no OAuth app, no API key and no IP allow-list**: a `_domainconnect.<domain>` TXT record names the provider's endpoint, its `/v2/{domain}/settings` says where the consent screen is, and we send the OWNER'S BROWSER there with the records as query parameters. The provider authenticates them in their own session. We never hold a credential for their DNS.
- **Everything off that TXT record is attacker-controlled**, by whoever runs that zone — and we are about to fetch it server-side AND send a browser to a URL built from it. `safeHost` takes a bare hostname and nothing else; `urlSyncUX` must be absolute https with no credentials. An address literal is refused: four labels of digits is a request aimed at our own network.
- **THE TEMPLATE WAS INVALID AND ONLY THE REAL SCHEMA COULD SAY SO.** A CNAME or NS at `@` forces `hostRequired: true`; ours was exactly that with `hostRequired: false`, reasoned out and written up at length ("one record serving both the apex and www, the apply URL's `host` decides"). `hostRequired: true` instead would have made the **bare domain permanently unreachable**, which is the case a small business asks for by name. The answer is the standard's own `APEXCNAME` — a record with no host, implemented by the provider with ALIAS/ANAME/flattening, i.e. precisely what an owner cannot do by hand. **Two records in two groups now** (`apex` APEXCNAME, `www` literal CNAME) and the apply URL names ONE, because a hostname must be registered with Cloudflare for SaaS before it serves and applying both points a name at us we are not hosting. `groupId` rides INSIDE the signed query.
- **Two tests were deleted rather than repaired, and this is the lesson.** Both restated the schema by hand — a required-field list, an allowed-field set — and both asserted the invalid shape, so the pair passed for the entire time the file would have been rejected on review. One was titled *"conforms to the published JSON Schema"*. `domain-connect/template.schema` is now the **upstream schema vendored verbatim** and `test/domain-connect-template.test.mjs` runs the template through a draft-07 subset validator, which is itself asserted against deliberate violations first — one returning `[]` for everything passes just as quietly. My earlier hand-rolled Python validator reported PASS on the invalid file because it implemented no `not`/`contains`/`const`.
- **Signing is not optional.** An apply URL is a plain GET, so unsigned a stranger builds `…/providers/gofarther.dev/services/site/apply?domain=victim.com&target=evil.example` and the provider offers to apply it under OUR name. The standard's two answers are mutually exclusive: `warnPhishing` (an interstitial on every apply, ours included, that a determined click still passes) or a **signature**. RSA-SHA256 over the query exactly as sent — never rebuilt, or the signed bytes and the sent bytes differ and nothing verifies. **No button at all rather than an unsigned one**: with `syncPubKeyDomain` declared, providers REQUIRE the signature, so unsigned is a button that always fails while the copyable records work.
- **BOTH HALVES OF THE SIGNING KEY ARE IN PLACE (2026-08-07).** `_dck1.gofarther.dev` publishes the public half (committed as `domain-connect/dck1.pub`, put there by `saas-setup.mjs` step 4) and `DOMAIN_CONNECT_KEY` holds the private half in GitHub Actions. **The chain was proven, not inferred**: a query signed with the private key verifies against the public key READ BACK OUT OF LIVE DNS, and the same signature over a tampered `target` is rejected.
  - **A TXT record over 255 bytes is SPLIT BY DNS and anything comparing it must rejoin the chunks.** Ours is 394 bytes and returns as 255 + 139; a naive quote-strip leaves the separator in and reports a one-character mismatch against a record that is byte-for-byte correct. That happened on the first check here — worth knowing before somebody "fixes" a key that is already right.
  - **Adding the secret does NOT redeploy.** The Worker keeps the `|| 'not-a-key'` fallback until the next push, and since that fallback makes the deploy log print `***` either way, the log cannot distinguish set from unset. The observable proof is a signed button in Cloud → Domains, which also needs the template merged and an owner whose provider supports Domain Connect.
  - **Rotating means publishing a SECOND label** (`_dck2`, and changing `DC_KEY_ID`) — never editing the record in place, because every link already in somebody's browser was signed under the old key. `saas-setup.mjs` REFUSES to overwrite a different value at that label rather than leaving that to memory.
- **THE TEMPLATE IS SUBMITTED — `Domain-Connect/Templates` PR #1543, all five checks green** (JSON Schema Validation, template lint, PR Description Check, Template Labels, Automerge Check), awaiting a maintainer review. `domain-connect/PR.md` is the body; **the editor is drivable over the plain form** — fetch the page for a `_csrf_token`, POST back `_template` + `_test_template=true` + `domain` + `group` + variables — which matters because **a headless browser has NO outbound egress in this container** (`example.com` resets too) and curl does. The PR could not be opened from the session (`fork_repository` → "not configured for this session", `add_repo` → "requires approval"), so it went through a prefilled `…/new/master?filename=&value=` link.
  - **THEIR `check_pr_description.py` IS THE SPEC, and reading it beat guessing three times over.** With `hostRequired: false` it demands **two** editor runs — one WITH a `host` parameter and one WITHOUT. Both original runs were host-less (I varied the GROUP instead and flagged it as a caveat in the body; they enforce it mechanically). Which run to add was **measured**: `apex` with `host=www` correctly IGNORES the host and still writes at the apex, because `APEXCNAME` has no host field, while `www` with a host would write `www.www.example.com` — a misuse our apply URL cannot produce, since it names a `groupId` and never a host.
  - **A ~900-character token pasted through chat arrived corrupt** — `could not decode token: Error -3 while decompressing data`, so only 2 of 3 links counted and the one carrying the host was the casualty. The body has to be copied from the FILE. Fetch their script and run it locally against `PR.md` before asking anyone to paste anything; it exits 0/1 and prints the same lines the bot posts.
  - **`DCTL1038` is info-level, not a failure** — *APEXCNAME and REDIRxxx records are not widely supported* — and is exactly why there are two groups: a provider without APEXCNAME still serves through `www`.
- **`oneClickBlocked` was computed by the route and rendered by nothing** until 2026-08-07 — the dead-field shape, again. Two reasons now say themselves: a provider offering only the OAuth variant, and a subdomain that is neither the apex nor `www`. The second also **skips discovery entirely**, which was a DNS round trip per domain per panel load with nothing to do with the answer.
- **Route 53 taught the detection format.** Its brand sits mid-label (`ns-1234.awsdns-56.org`), so an implicit "match anywhere" convention silently failed on it; `PROVIDERS` now carries explicit `ns` (suffix) and `part` (infix) forms.
- **THE ZONE IS SET UP AND CUSTOM DOMAINS ARE LIVE (2026-08-07).** All three green: `saas.gofarther.dev` is `AAAA 100::` proxied, the zone's fallback origin is set to it and **active**, and the `*/*` Worker route is registered. 0 hostnames used of 100 free. `.github/workflows/saas-setup.yml` does the first two and verifies the third, DRY RUN by default because it edits DNS on the live zone; `deploy.yml` prints the same report read-only on every deploy, so the answer is visible when a token scope or a DNS record changes under it. It is also the diagnostic: Cloudflare answers a missing PERMISSION with error 10000 "Authentication error", which is indistinguishable from a bad token, so every probe names the scope to add.
  - **`1404` IS NOT A PERMISSION AND WAS REPORTED AS ONE.** The custom-hostname endpoints answer *"No quota has been allocated for this zone"* when Cloudflare for SaaS is simply not switched on, and the first draft told the reader to add `SSL and Certificates: Read` — advice that cannot work. **Cloudflare's own 1404 text is also wrong for us**: it points at an Enterprise sales form, while custom hostnames are self-serve on Free/Pro/Business with 100 included and $0.10/month each after. Enabling it is SSL/TLS → Custom Hostnames, and there is no purchase step on Free.
  - **The tests RUN the script rather than read it** (`test/fixtures/cf-stub.mjs`). A mutation making the entitlement branch unreachable survived source-reading assertions that checked the constant and both messages were present — all three still were. Spawned against a stubbed API with every non-GET recorded, the safety properties become assertions: a dry run writes NOTHING, `--apply` writes exactly two things, a configured zone is left alone, and the refusal branch writes nothing while refusing.
  - **A push was the only trigger available**, so the apply ran as a deliberate ONE-SHOT `--apply` in `deploy.yml`, reverted in the next commit — `workflow_dispatch` and `repository_dispatch` both answer 403 to this session, and apply-on-every-deploy is the shape the script was built not to have.
  - **A `*/*` Worker route, and this is the one that is total when missed.** The two `wrangler.jsonc` entries are `custom_domain` routes for `gofarther.dev` and `www`, and a customer's `sharpfadebarbers.com` is NEITHER — it matches no route, falls through to the fallback origin, and **every custom domain on the platform answers 522** while the platform's own site is perfectly fine. It must live in `wrangler.jsonc`: wrangler publishes routes with a PUT whose own source says *"PUT will delete previous routes on this script"*, so a route created by hand or by the setup script survives exactly until the next deploy.
