  - **IT IS COMMENTED OUT, AND THE ORDER IS THE REASON.** Enabled first, the deploy FAILED at `PUT /accounts/…/workers/scripts/isibi-app/routes` — script and both custom domains deployed, the routes call did not, because a bare `*` hostname matches nothing on a zone with no Cloudflare for SaaS. **Fallback origin first, route second.** The exact line sits commented in `wrangler.jsonc` and again in `saas-setup.mjs`, held to one string by a test, and step 3 reads `later` against a bare zone and `TODO NOW` once steps 1 and 2 are done — a pending step that reads the same before and after its precondition is one nobody acts on at the right moment.
  - **ADDING AN OPTIONAL SECRET TO `deploy.yml` TOOK EVERY DEPLOY DOWN.** `wrangler-action` fails the WHOLE run with *"Value for secret X not found in environment"* when a name in its `secrets:` list has an empty value, so listing `DOMAIN_CONNECT_KEY` before the key existed meant three consecutive merges uploaded nothing and deployed nothing — while the error named a feature none of them touched. Optional secrets now carry a `|| fallback` whose value cannot decode as base64, so `rsaSigner` returns null and it behaves exactly as unset. `test/deploy-secrets.test.mjs` enforces it BOTH ways: optional must have a fallback, **required must not** — a fallback on a required secret turns "the deploy stops" into "the platform ships with a broken credential". The cost, worth knowing because the trick has been used here: a masked `***` in the deploy log no longer proves this particular secret has a value.
  - **The fallback origin is ORIGINLESS — `AAAA 100::`, proxied.** Cloudflare's own documented value; the Worker answers before origin resolution so nothing is ever dialled. My first draft was `A 192.0.2.1`, reasoned out from the documentation range, and that is not the same promise — an address in a range somebody might one day route is an address traffic might one day reach. Unproxied it is the same 522 from the other direction, since Cloudflare would try to REACH it.
  - **Refuses to overwrite a record it did not create**, and refuses to set the zone's fallback origin before the record behind it exists — Cloudflare ACCEPTS an origin with no DNS behind it, which is a success that produces a broken platform.
  - **Found by driving it against a stubbed API, not by reading it:** the hostname count read `result_info` off a helper that never returned it, so it silently fell back to the PAGE length — a zone with 300 hostnames would have reported the page size and claimed free headroom already spent. An unreadable count now says so rather than reading as zero, because "you have 100 free left" is the one wrong answer here that costs money.
  - **A mutation survived and the fix is the lesson:** `assert.match(script, /proxied: true/)` passed with the CREATE changed to `false`, because the repair branch a hundred lines away satisfied it. Both call sites are anchored separately now. 9 mutations, all caught.

## Publishing is atomic, versions are real, and one colour can be changed (2026-08-08)

**Four things the owner hit in a live session, and every one was a different layer of the same publish path.**

- **PUBLISHING HAD A GAP, and that is what "changing pages changes the site" was.** `writeSiteDistToR2` wiped the prefix and then wrote the new dist over it, so for the length of ~20 R2 puts the site was half-deleted — and a generated site is **code-split one chunk per route**, so clicking through to another page fetched a chunk that had been deleted and not yet rewritten. Now write-then-sweep, `index.html` LAST because it is the pointer. Safe only because vite content-hashes asset names: a new build's assets have new names, so writing them cannot clash with the ones being served.
- **VERSION HISTORY WAS TWO LIES STACKED.** The Restore button rewrote localStorage and could not touch the published files — and there was nothing to restore them FROM, because a publish overwrote the old build. `site-versions.mjs` archives every publish to `versions/<slug>/<id>/` with a manifest, keeps 10, and `POST /api/site/<slug>/versions/restore` copies one back through the same write-then-sweep path. **A COPY, NOT A POINTER**: serving `/s/<slug>/` from a version prefix would put a lookup on the visitor path, which is the thing `site-routing.mjs` exists to remove. A version with no readable manifest is not offered — restoring one means guessing its file list, and a wrong guess publishes a mixture of two builds. **Deleting a site deletes its archive**, the `neon_teardown` leak one resource over.
  - **The list is labelled by the CHANGE, not the brand.** Labelled `brand || slug`, every row read "Sharp Fade Barbers" and only the timestamp told three builds apart — useless for the one question the panel is opened to answer. `versionLabel` lives in the module (worker.js cannot be imported — believed unimportable at the time; CORRECTED 2026-08-13, it CAN be, though extracting it was still right for the injection it bought) and takes the raw turn message, not the composed brief, which is thousands of characters with the change buried in it.
- **TOKEN OVERRIDES — anchoring the look fixed the re-roll and broke the other half.** "make the background yellow" no longer re-rolls a barber shop into a different site, and the customer then could not change the background AT ALL: every token comes from a theme in the registry and none of the 500 is "the one you have, but yellow". `builder/site-tokens.mjs` is a per-site patch of CSS custom properties, written **AFTER the theme** in the container — the same properties the theme declares, so later wins, and that ordering IS the mechanism. Stored in the site's own `_meta` and **ACCUMULATED**, because a revise names only what it is changing; a replacing merge hands back the theme's own background on the second revise. **Rides on `design_schema`**, which already reads the instruction and already returns structured output, so it costs no extra model call — the `needsWeb` reasoning.
  - **The allow-list is 14 tokens, and every exclusion has a reason** (2026-08-08). `success` and `warning` are IN on measurement rather than taste: **33 kit components paint with them** and all 33 are offered to the generator, so without them they were the only page colours a customer could not touch. OUT: `--radius` is a length, not a colour; `--chart-1..5` are a SET whose whole job is staying distinguishable from each other; the `-foreground` halves are DERIVED, not asked for; and the eight `--sidebar*` tokens are used by exactly ONE component on a platform that makes barber shops — if a generated site ever really uses a sidebar the fix is to make `--sidebar` FOLLOW `--background` the way `--muted-foreground` does, one derivation rather than eight more slots. **A token we allow that the template never declares would write a property nothing reads** — a colour change reported as applied that moves nothing — so a derived guard checks every name against the template's own `:root` and `.dark` blocks AND its `@theme` mapping (declared-but-unmapped is unreachable from a page; mapped-but-undeclared has no value to start from). Its first draft read only the FIRST `:root` block, found four tokens and reported the list broken — the template declares its palette across several.
  - **CORNERS ARE THE ONE NON-COLOUR TOKEN (2026-08-08, owner's call).** "Make the corners rounder" was unreachable — the colour parser correctly refused `0.5rem`, so there was no path at all. ONE knob, because the template made it one: `--radius` is declared at `:root` and the kit's seven sizes are derived from it with `calc()`, so overriding the single value moves every corner coherently and nobody can set `sm` larger than `xl`. Validated by `isLength` (anchored like the colour patterns; **negatives refused**, since a negative border-radius is invalid CSS and would be a change reported as applied that did nothing).
    - **A ZERO IS A ZERO, whatever unit it wears, and this was MEASURED.** The derived sizes are `calc(var(--radius) ± Npx)`, so a bare `0` makes each a `<number> + <length>` — invalid per spec, every declaration dropped, every corner square. `0px` and `0rem` are valid lengths, so `rounded-xl` stays at 4px. Rendered side by side: `0` gave 0/0/0 and `0px` gave 0/4/0 — the same instruction producing a square site or a half-square one depending on which unit the model happened to pick. Normalised to the bare form.
    - **280 OF THE 500 THEMES HARD-SET `border-radius` on buttons and inputs** as real rules rather than through `--radius` — measured, not assumed. On a majority of sites a corner change therefore moved the cards and left every button square, which is a feature reported as broken. `stripThemeRadius` drops the theme's own corner rules, **only when a radius was actually asked for** (a colour-only revise leaves the theme exactly as it is today, so no existing site changes) and **only from the theme's generated CSS** — it matches `border-radius`, never `--radius`. `validForWrite` is the ONE reading of "did they ask for a radius", shared by the strip and the write, because two readings eventually disagree and the failure is silent.
    - **The value hint is derived PER TOKEN.** Described as `#rrggbb`, `radius` would be asked for in hex, refused by the parser, and reported back to the customer as a colour we could not use.
  - **The value must be a colour we can PARSE, not a string passed through.** It is written into a stylesheet, so a `;` or `}` closes the declaration and the rule. There is no correct escape for "arbitrary text in a CSS value"; the set of shapes a colour can take is small enough to list. **The required separator was a real hole**: written `\s*[,/]?\s*`, `rgb(255)` matched, because `255` reads as the three numbers 2, 5, 5. **Two guards were then proved INERT and removed** — an explicit `;{}<>` check and a length bound, both already refused by the anchored patterns, so the anchors are what is pinned now. A guard that cannot fire reads as protection that is not there.
  - **BOTH BUGS THAT MATTERED WERE FOUND BY LOOKING AT A RENDER.** A dark background under a light theme left `--muted-foreground` as the light theme's dark grey and every line of body copy was almost invisible — it compiled, bundled, published and passed every assertion, the grey-charts lesson again. That token is drawn on the PAGE, not on `--muted`, so it follows the background (zinc 600/400, each clearing 4.5:1 on its own side). And **the ask list is not the write list**: `withContrast` derives `card-foreground` and friends, names the designer is deliberately NOT offered, and one shared allow-list had the deriving function's output dropped by the function meant to write it.
  - **A first attempt also moved `card`, `popover` and `muted` onto the new ground.** That is design nobody asked for, and it made `--muted` equal to the page so every loading skeleton became invisible. Reverted — a white card on a dark page is a strong contrast, not a bug.
- **THE "Live ↗" BUTTON IS GONE (owner's call).** A React site publishes as part of the build, so it did nothing Share does not, and it opened the raw `/s/<slug>/` URL in a new tab — which reads as "your site on some weird page" rather than the customer's own address.
- **DOCS-ONLY COMMITS NO LONGER DEPLOY.** push → Deploy → `build smoke`, and `build smoke` spends two real model calls plus a Neon project every run; three commits in one stretch touched only CLAUDE.md and docs/ and each paid for a full site build to prove an identical Worker still worked. `paths-ignore` on `**.md`/`docs/**`, guarded BOTH ways — the filter must exist, and nothing the deploy ships may be a file it ignores, checked at the **module graph** and the **served assets directory** rather than by grepping for a filename (the first draft's grep was walked past by `"see docs/components.md"`, because the pattern could not cross a space).

**Three bugs found on the way, each invisible to `node --check`, to esbuild and to the whole suite:**

- **`deleteSiteFor` read `du.id`** — a `const` declared in the router ~3,700 lines below, left behind when the function was extracted. A `ReferenceError` inside a `try` whose `catch` logs, so the legacy-project branch was silently dead. **The `vidRefN` class from the other direction**, and the existing guard could not see it: that one walks FORWARD from a declaration to a read after its block closes; this is a read BEFORE the declaration in a different function. **A general free-variable analyser was tried and abandoned with measurements** — the symmetric backward scan flagged 30 candidates and a full per-function scope check flagged 1,113, both dominated by prose and property names, and there is no parser in this repo. Narrowed to the names that can only ever mean the router, which is exactly the hazard an extraction creates.
- **`return g.error` on `assertOwner`** — its refusal is a plain `{status, body}` built by `site-owner.mjs`'s own `json()`, not a `Response`. Returned straight out of a handler, **every refusal would 500 while the success path worked perfectly**: the `dm2` bug inverted, from the same misread of the same contract. The existing guard checked that callers TEST `.error`; it now also checks what they DO with it.
- **`pruneVersions` read a negative cap as "keep 1"** — `Math.max(1, …)` on a raw number prunes HARDER than the default. Found by its own test. A nonsense cap keeps more, never fewer.

**Guards that earned their keep, and one that nearly did not.** The Dockerfile check caught `site-tokens.mjs` not being COPYd into the image, which would have stopped the build container starting. The tool-schema checker's stub was **too simple for the third time** — a plain function has no `.map`, so `design_schema` went back into `skipped` and was checked by nothing; it is now callable, a value AND a list. Two source-window guards had to stop being sized in bytes, which is this session's recurring bug. 1739 tests, 52/52 site-build, 29/29 + 37/37 mutations from verified-green baselines.

## A revise is an EDIT now, features can be removed, and words are free (2026-08-08)

**Three limits the owner asked to have fixed, found by auditing what the builder cannot do rather than what it does wrong.**

- **EVERY REVISE REWROTE EVERY PAGE FROM NOTHING.** `pagesRequest` took `{brief, spec, brand, family, attachments}` and the container wipes `src/routes` before each build, so the model never saw the site it was changing. "Change the phone number in the header" regenerated all the copy on every page — on topic, because the brief anchors it, and different words every time. **This is the OTHER half of what the owner reported as "changing pages changes the stuff inside the site"**; the publish gap was real and so was this. Now a build STORES the source it produced (`source/<slug>/pages.json` — deliberately NOT under `sites/`, which `/s/<slug>/` serves to the public) and a revise hands it back with one instruction: return every page as an EDIT, byte-identical except where the change was asked for. Deleting a page is expressible (do not return it); a site too large to inline degrades to naming the pages, which is exactly today's behaviour for the site that would have cost the most. **The prior source is appended LAST, after the trade exemplar** — two "references" that can disagree, and read in the other order the model treats its own site as one more example to draw from.
- **THE SCHEMA ENGINE COULD ONLY EVER GROW.** Every statement is `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` / `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, and the only `DROP` in the file is the managed `tsv` search column. So "remove the booking page" took the page away and left the table **publicly writable forever** — a form nobody can see, still accepting submissions from anyone who knows the endpoint. `retired: true` now withdraws every grant (`grantsFor` returns `[]`) and hides the table from `schemaDigest`, so no page can be written against it either. **WITHOUT A DROP, DELIBERATELY:** a `collect` table holds real customers' bookings, and a model reading "remove the booking page" must never be one step from deleting them. The rows stay, the owner can still export them, and a later revise brings the feature back by not retiring it — reversible in both directions.
- **A TYPO IN A HEADING COST ~21 CREDITS AND A MODEL CALL**, because the words are in the page source and the Data panel only reaches table rows. `GET`/`POST /api/site/<slug>/text` now lists the editable strings and applies changes with **no model call at all** — the same container compiles, the same publish path ships it, and the response says `cost: 0`. Free in credits, not in time, which is the honest thing to tell the customer. It falls straight out of storing the source for the fix above.
  - **The whole difficulty is telling prose from code.** A page is TSX: class names, import paths, route ids, column names. Two rules do most of it — a code-ish attribute's value is skipped (`className`, `to`, `href`, `aria-*`…), and **a string with no whitespace and no capital is an identifier whatever its length**. That second one was found by a test: `useRows("bookings", {order: "created_at", dir: "desc"})` offered all three as editable words, and renaming a column produces a page that compiles and then reads a column that does not exist. Cost of the rule, stated: a genuinely lowercase one-word label is not offered. `title` is deliberately NOT filtered — a tooltip is text a visitor reads.
  - **An edit carries the OFFSET, and a stale one is refused rather than applied.** The same sentence can appear twice on a page. And a batch is applied **back to front**, because each edit changes the length of the source and every offset after the first would otherwise be wrong — silently, landing in whatever moved into it. One bad edit fails the whole batch and returns the ORIGINAL pages: a mutation proved the first draft handed back the half-edited set, which then gets published because it looks like the pages the caller asked for.
  - **A failed compile leaves the live site alone.** The words came from the owner, so a refusal is theirs to correct; publishing a broken bundle to fix a typo is the trade nobody would make. The recompile carries the site's stored theme, fonts and token patch, or fixing a typo would silently re-style the whole site.
- **The dispatch guard earned its keep again**: `ownerSlug` is built from its own list of matchers, and adding `tx` to the condition without adding it there would have made every text edit read the slug of whichever route matched first. Same shape as the `dm2` bug, caught by the test written for it.
- **AND THEN THE SAME BUG SHIPPED ANYWAY, ONE LAYER FURTHER OUT (found live 2026-08-09).** `/versions` and `/text` were in the matchers, in the dispatch condition and in the `ownerSlug` list — all three asserted, all three green — and the block those live in sat inside an **outer** gate carrying its own list of the same routes spelled differently: `startsWith("/api/site/") && (includes("/rows") || includes("/members") || …)`. Neither new route was in it, so **both were unreachable end to end**: version history and free text editing answered the router's bottom 404 to their own owner, exactly as `dm2` had. **The guard written for `dm2` could not see it** — it checks matchers against the INNER condition and never looked above it: the guard watching the layer below the break, for the second time in the same feature. The fix is structural rather than a fourth entry: **there is now ONE list and the outer gate is a bare prefix**, safe because everything in the block lives inside that inner `if` (a path matching nothing falls through) and because the site-delete branch below it long ago stopped matching any DELETE under `/api/site/`. `test/api-auth.test.mjs` asserts the outer gate stays bare, since **two lists of the same routes is what keeps failing here**.
  - **Only a live run could have said so, which is the whole argument for one.** From outside, an undispatched route and one `assertOwner` correctly refuses are the same 404 — and the unit suite was green on all 1,794 tests with both features dead.
- **The smoke run's second turn could not be paid for.** A new account is granted 20 credits and the first build spends all of them (measured: 9 schema + 20 pages), so the revise answered **402** and the journey never ran — four red lines about money, none about code. The run now tops its own throwaway account up **between** the two builds, by a direct service-key write to the ledger rather than `add_credits` (mint-key gated, and it would write a `purchases` row that makes the account read as having paid, quietly changing watermarks, storage tier and `is_paid()`). **Between, not at sign-up**, on two counts: the first build stays on exactly the grant a real new account has, and it stays **unable to afford photographs** — one image is ~19 credits of real fal spend, bought out of whatever the model calls leave behind. A revise buys none.
- **"MAKE THE BACKGROUND YELLOW" ANSWERED 422 AND CHANGED NOTHING (found live 2026-08-09)** — the token-override feature dead the day after it shipped. The designer only sees the INSTRUCTION on a revise, so a look-only change correctly declares no tables; the route read that as *"That brief didn't describe anything to store"* and refused. The refusal sat BEFORE the ownership lookup, which is the only reason it could not be expressed properly: `existing` — this slug already has a site, and it is yours — is not known yet at that point. Moved after it and gated on `!existing`; a first build still refuses, still with a refund, since that path returns before anything is provisioned. **Letting it through moved the danger one layer down**: `_meta.schema` is the only copy the request path reads, and `applySiteSchema`'s merge is wrapped in a bare `catch {}`, so a transient read failure would have written `{tables: []}` over a real schema — every table off the data API with its rows untouched in Postgres, a site that looks perfect where every list 404s. The write is skipped when nothing is declared AND nothing merged, because then it could only destroy. The smoke run now checks the tables survive a look-only revise.
- **`revise` was `!!priorBrief` and is now ownership.** Same answer for every site built since the brief started being recorded, and the wrong one for anything older — such a revise read as a first build and would have re-bought every photograph on it at ~19 credits each. The row is already read for the ownership check, so the better signal is free.
- **The run's own cost report was a lie, in the direction that hides the schema call.** It printed `d.schemaFee`, a field that has never existed: the schema line read `charged undefined` and the pages line subtracted `|| 0` and printed the WHOLE bill — a build charging 9 + 11 reported *"pages call → charged 20"*. The two numbers the report exists to separate were one number wearing a label.
- 1798 tests, 6/6 mutations from a verified-green baseline.

## Backlog (user-triaged, do later)

- **~~DOWNLOADABLE FILES~~ — BUILT 2026-08-19. See the entry above.**
- **~~ONE ELEMENT'S SIZE OR SPACING~~ — BUILT 2026-08-19 as option B, `builder/site-tweak.mjs`. The entry below is kept as the reasoning that chose the shape; see "The cheap rung" for what shipped.**
- **ONE ELEMENT'S SIZE OR SPACING, CHEAPLY — deferred 2026-08-18, owner's call, decide the shape later.** *"Make that heading bigger"*, *"there's too much space above the prices"*. **NOT impossible today**, which is what makes it different from the gaps beside it: the `page` layer does it by regenerating the whole page (~5 credits, Sonnet) — which also rewords the copy and can fail to compile. What is missing is a CHEAP path, for the commonest small tweak there is. **Measured: 6,089 of 7,457 `className` values in a generated page carry a size or spacing utility, on all 328 corpus pages.**
  - **A — narrow and mechanical.** Find the element by its own words (the `linkSlots` pattern) and step the size or gap one notch along Tailwind's ladder. Deterministic: cannot break the build and cannot change a word. Only ever does size and spacing, so "centre that" or "put a line under it" each need building again.
  - **B — a general cheap edit, and my recommendation.** Haiku gets one page's source plus the request and returns a MINIMAL patch. Covers size, spacing, alignment, borders, moving a block — anything in that file — at ~1-2 credits against ~5, and it is told to change only what was asked so the copy stays put. Risk: model-written source can fail to compile, which salvage and the container refusing already protect the live site from. **It is the thing that stops most future "it cannot do X" needing new machinery at all.**
  - **THE REASON THIS ONE IS DIFFERENT, and it is the useful part of the whole audit.** Almost every other gap closed this week was a MISSING SLOT rather than a missing model capability — `SiteFooter` took `brand, tagline, links` and nothing else; `SiteHeader` hardcoded its height, width and stickiness; `SafeImage` had no `focus` prop. A model can rewrite those files all day and cannot produce a centred header, because the component it is obliged to use hardcodes it. **Here the slot already exists** (`className` is right there in the source), so there is nothing to add and the question is purely how cheaply a model may touch it.
  - **AND IT IS ONE GAP WEARING SIX HATS, re-measured 2026-08-19 after dark mode landed.** Every remaining frontend ask that is merely EXPENSIVE rather than impossible is "change a prop or a className in this file", so B closes all of them at once rather than one at a time: section ORDER (**1040 sections across 329 corpus pages, median 3 a page**), a section's own background, and the layout props — `ratio=` **244**, `columns=` **87**, `size=` **27**, `variant=` **20**, `align=` **1**. Building A instead buys the smallest of the six.

- **~~RIGHT-TO-LEFT~~ — BUILT 2026-08-19. See the entry above. The bullet below is kept as the reasoning that was right about the OBSTACLE and wrong about the count.**
  - It said **363 components use a physical utility against ONE using a logical one**. The real number is **1,946 physical utilities across 614 of 3,728 files**, counted on class TOKENS — the 363 came from a substring scan, and a substring scan is worthless here: `items-center` contains `ms-`, `rounded-lg` contains `rounded-l`, `theme-` contains `me-`. That same scan reported 1,158 files as already logical, which is nonsense. **The conclusion it drew was right and the evidence for it was not**, which is the shape worth noticing: a wrong measurement can support a correct call and still mislead whoever acts on it next.
  - **`rtl-preview.tsx` and `direction.tsx` are STILL reachable by nothing.** That half stands: they were on disk before this work and neither is what made RTL work. Do not read their presence as support.
  - **NOT URGENT WAS ALSO RIGHT AND STOPPED BEING A REASON TO WAIT.** The market is UK small businesses on `en-GB`, and an Arabic-owned shop writing its brief in English still gets `lang: "en"` and a correct site — the failure needs the BRIEF to be in an RTL language. What changed is the price: the sweep is a no-op for every LTR site (measured, 96/96), so shipping it costs nothing anybody can see and removes a limit that was total.

- Static voice previews — **user adds the files**: drop MP3s at `public/voices/<name>.mp3`, lowercase (rachel.mp3, aria.mp3, sarah.mp3, laura.mp3, charlotte.mp3, alice.mp3, matilda.mp3, jessica.mp3, lily.mp3, roger.mp3, george.mp3, callum.mp3, liam.mp3, will.mp3, brian.mp3, daniel.mp3). The preview button already checks these before spending a TTS call.
- Gallery view is built (browse/filter/download/delete all saved media, per-message 🗑 too). Remaining monetization TODO: server-side video-file watermark burn (fal ffmpeg), Stripe activation + billing-portal ("Cancel anytime"), media bucket size/mime caps.
- **Real background removal (owner OK'd 2026-07-16, build later):** no image model in the lineup can output true alpha (the director now says so instead of faking a checkerboard). Wire a fal background-removal utility (BiRefNet / imageutils/rembg — pennies/image, verify price first) as an ORCHESTRATOR-ROUTED step, NOT a picker model: "remove the background" → cutout endpoint → real transparent PNG; add a checkerboard backdrop in the media viewer (UI-only) so transparency reads; meter through the credit ledger (≥1 credit); chains with edits ("cutout → composite onto X"). Live test needs the fal balance top-up.

## Neon provisioning (user builds → backend connects)

- **Ownership is enforced by the layer that returns the connection.** `lookupSite` returns `{conn, uid}` and `ensureSiteBackend` refuses a slug owned by anyone else (including one claimed but not yet built — a half-finished build by another user, where `saveBackend`'s upsert would otherwise overwrite their row). The route's own pre-check **fails closed** with a 503. Before 2026-07-28 that check was `catch {}`, so one Supabase timeout turned "cannot tell who owns this" into "nobody does" and the build applied its schema, seeded rows and published pages **over another user's site**.
- **A revise generates pages against the MERGED schema.** A revise sends `{slug, instruction}` and the designer sees only the instruction, so `spec` holds only the tables that instruction named; generating from it rewrote the whole site knowing one table ("add a gallery" turned a barber shop into a gallery page). The merged spec is read back from `_meta` after the apply. **Fixed 2026-07-28:** `site_backends.brief` holds the brief the site was built from — free to read (the route already does that lookup for the ownership check) and written exactly once per site, since `ensureSiteBackend` returns early when the slug already has a database, so a revise physically cannot overwrite it. `briefForPages()` in `builder/page-gen.mjs` composes anchor + change + "keep everything the original asked for unless this change says otherwise" (the failure mode is subtraction, not addition). Deliberately NOT an accumulating log — that grows without bound and keeps contradicting itself; what the site has BECOME is carried by the merged schema.
- **`site-provision.mjs`** — `ensureSiteBackend`: reuse the site's database if the slug already has one, else create the user's Neon PROJECT on their first build (lazily — projects are capped and most accounts never build), then a DATABASE per site inside it, recording each in Supabase (`user_site_project`, `site_backends`). Injected deps like `publish-pages.mjs`; `worker.js` supplies the real Neon/Supabase calls. `test/site-provision.test.mjs` (15 tests, all mutation-checked).
- **Three bugs fixed here 2026-07-28.** (1) **Both Supabase writes were awaited and never checked.** A Neon project is a capped, billed resource whose ONLY record is a Supabase row, so a failed `user_site_project` write left the project invisible — the next build found no row and created ANOTHER, and the orphan billed forever. Very likely how the two loose projects in the old handoff notes appeared. Now: record it or **destroy it**, and a failed `site_backends` write fails the build instead of reporting success for a site whose slug is unclaimed and whose every read 404s. (2) `ensureSiteBackend` used the 5-minute-cached `siteBackendBySlug`; on the WRITE path that could hand back a connection for a slug another isolate had deleted, sending the schema apply at a dropped database — it now uses `siteBackendBySlugFresh`. (3) A failed cleanup no longer masks the original error.
- **`"already exists"` is the one recoverable database error** (a retried build hits the database it made last time; the schema apply is additive/idempotent). Quota, auth and branch-not-found still fail.
- Provisioning is **async on Neon's side**: create-project returns when the work is SCHEDULED and Neon rejects further calls while operations are in flight, so `waitForProject()` polls `/projects/{id}/operations` after creating a project AND after creating a database. Every database in a project shares one host and role, so `connForDatabase` just swaps the path — no extra API call, no password fetch at request time.
- Proven end to end against a real database by `test/integration/neon-e2e.mjs` (CI `neon-e2e`, needs `NEON_API_KEY`): provisions a throwaway project, applies a schema, checks the triggers/FTS/guards fire, deletes it in a `finally`.

## Neon projects no longer leak on account deletion (`site-teardown.mjs`, 2026-07-29)

- **A Neon project is a capped, billed resource whose only record is a Supabase row — and those rows CASCADE from `auth.users`.** Verified: `site_project`, `user_site_project` and `site_backends` all have `ON DELETE CASCADE` on `uid`. So deleting an account destroyed the record and left the project running, invisible, billing forever. Pre-existing (one per deleted account) and made worse by per-site projects (one per site). `delete_account()` cannot fix it: it is a Postgres RPC and cannot call the Neon API.
- **The fix is a `BEFORE DELETE` trigger, not a patch to `delete_account()`** — the trigger is the only place that sees every path the row can disappear by: the FK cascade, the account RPC, `DELETE /api/site/<slug>`, and an operator typing `DELETE` by hand. Patching the RPC would have covered one of four. **Proved against a real `auth.users` delete inside a rolled-back transaction**: the cascade fired and the project was captured.
- `neon_teardown` (project_id UNIQUE, attempts, last_error, next_try_at) is drained by the **existing 2-minute cron**, which is the only side holding `NEON_API_KEY`. `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, not `DO UPDATE`: a project already queued is already going, and resetting its attempts would restart a backoff that exists because the drop keeps failing.
- **404 is DONE; 401/403 is NEVER done.** Already-gone is the NORMAL case here — the delete route drops the project inline and the trigger queues it anyway — so treating 404 as failure means the queue never empties. And during a key rotation every call answers 403; a sweeper that took that for success would delete the only record of every queued project at the exact moment it cannot see Neon at all. That one mutation is the most expensive in the file.
- **There is deliberately no "give up".** The backoff escalates 2m → 10m → 1h → 6h → 24h and then REPEATS. The row is the only record, so deleting it to stop the noise recreates the exact failure the queue exists to fix. A stuck row costs one call a day and stays visible with its `last_error`.
- `forget` runs AFTER `drop`, on purpose: a queue row for an already-gone project is harmless (the next tick 404s and clears it), while the other order loses the project. An unreadable queue is reported rather than read as an empty one, or a broken sweeper looks exactly like a drained one. Batch is bounded at 5 so one unkillable project cannot starve the rest.
- **This let `DELETE /api/site/<slug>` get simpler.** It used to keep the `site_project` row when the inline drop failed, which left the site half-deleted and needed an operator. Now the row always goes — because deleting it enqueues the project — so the caller's site really is gone and the record is still held. 10 mutations, all caught.

## The 1,140 charts are WIRED (2026-07-31)

**The generator can reach them now. Until this it could not, and nothing said so.** The chart tier had the exact shape of the two things this repo installed and then deleted: the 27 blocks and the 196 examples were both on disk, both compiled, and both were reachable by NOTHING, because no rule ever told the model they existed. This one was heading the same way.

- **The reusable thing is `charts/lib/<domain>`, not the 1,140 files beside it.** A `chart-*.tsx` is a DEMO — `export default function Component()` wrapping one primitive in a Card around invented figures ("Bookings 268 / 300 · January - June 2024"). The primitives are the 882 prop-driven exports across 141 domain modules underneath, and those are what the prompt now lists.
- **The demos get a LINT RULE, and the reasoning is the inverse of the one that retired the `@/examples/*` rule.** That rule went with its folder, correctly: deleting the files made the import a missing module, caught by `tsc` like any other. These 1,140 files are **not** missing. An import of one compiles, bundles and publishes a stranger's made-up numbers to a real business's visitors, and nothing else in the pipeline can tell that from a real chart. A test asserts the demos still exist, so if they are ever deleted the rule should go the way the examples rule did.
- **Names at compose, signatures at repair** — the same split `COMPONENT_API` already uses, extended rather than duplicated. `CHART_COMPONENTS` (domain → names, ~3,751 tokens) rides in the **system block**, which carries `cache_control: ephemeral`, so it is a cache read on every build after the first rather than fresh input; `CHART_API` was handed over on a repair, scoped to the domains that page actually imported — **unreachable since the repair pass went on 2026-08-04**, along with `COMPONENT_API`, `CHART_USAGE`, `repairPrompt` and the two `imported*` helpers. Kept deliberately (they are one decision away from mattering again, if first-try turns out poor) and SAID SO in the source, which is the difference from the five features that were dead and silent about it. PAGE_RULES went 9,471 → 13,499 tokens.
- **Grouped by domain, not flat, and not only for readability**: six names are exported by two modules each (`IncidentTimeline`, `LapseCurve`, `MortalityTable`, `PowerCurve`, `ReserveRunoff`, `RouteDensity`), so a flat list names a component the model then cannot locate. The lint checks the **named export against that specific module** — stricter than the ui check, which validates only the module — and when the name lives elsewhere it says where.
- **The chart lib was typechecked by NOTHING**, discovered on the way in: `tsconfig.json` excludes all of `src/components/charts`, `tsconfig.kit.json` covered only `ui`, and the demos that import it are excluded too, so no import was ever followed into it. It happened to be clean, which is luck rather than a check. `charts/lib` is in `tsconfig.kit.json` now (43s, CI-only); the demos stay out, as a catalogue.
- **All eight reference lines in the lib were RED, and the new prompt text called the kit monochrome.** A "Now" line, a mean, a playhead, a zero crossing — all `bg-destructive`, none of them meaning anything destructive, each a position distinguished only by hue and so invisible in greyscale, in print, and to anyone who cannot see the difference. Found by RENDERING one: it typechecks, it bundles, and every other test in the repo passed. Now dashed `border-foreground`, and a test holds the claim, because a sentence in the prompt with nothing behind it is how this codebase keeps getting bitten.
- **Three source files held raw control bytes** (`charts/lib/pharma.tsx`, `ui/pivot-table.tsx` NUL; `ui/terminal-output.tsx` ESC), which made `file` call them binary and **`grep` skip them entirely**. This repo runs a lot of source-reading guards, and a file grep will not read is a file those guards silently do not cover while looking exactly like one with nothing to report. Rewritten as `\u0000`/`\u001b` escapes — byte-identical behaviour, verified by evaluating the expressions lifted out of the real files rather than a retyped copy of them.
- **A no-op guard found by mutation, in my own new code.** The demo rule's `(?!lib\/)` could not have mattered: written `[a-z0-9-]+` the path class never crosses a slash, so `charts/lib/bullet` never matched it and removing the lookahead passed the whole suite. The class allows `/` now, which catches a nested demo path AND makes the lookahead real. 10 mutants, all caught.
- **The 1,140 demos ARE wired, as worked examples rather than as components** (owner's call). `gen-chart-usage.mjs` mines one real call per primitive out of the demo that already exercises it — known-good by construction, because it is code that compiles today. Handed over on the REPAIR pass beside the signature and scoped to what the page imported. **This is what a signature cannot say**: `Waterfall(steps: Step[], …)` stops at a type NAME, 27 of the 854 do, and the example resolves it AND shows the optional `total: true` no name hinted at. Arrays are cut to two elements — the model needs the shape of a row, not the 420 points a demo generates. **An example that FABRICATES is dropped rather than cleaned up**: several modules export a generator beside the component (`sampleBoxes`, `rng`), those are real exports so such an example compiles, and the model would copy it and publish random numbers — the demo-import lint's own failure arriving through the example instead. `boxplot.BoxPlot` has no example for exactly that reason.
- **The ceiling is 45 of 141 domains, and it falls the wrong way.** The 1,140 demos import only the 45 chart-TYPE modules (boxplot, histogram, waterfall); the 96 with none are every industry one — `salon`, `restaurant`, `bakery`, `gym`, `hotel`, `garage`, `dentistry` — which is precisely what this platform builds. So **600 of the 882 primitives had never been rendered at all**, and the 48 contact sheets showed the other 282. Mined examples can never reach them; the signatures have to stand alone there.
- **Rule 3 said "These exist and nothing else does" about `@/components/ui` — an absolute sentence ten rules BEFORE the one naming 882 more components** (fixed 2026-07-31, owner's call to treat the charts as part of the kit). Nothing was broken and nothing failed: the model reads the flat denial first and has no reason to go looking, so the whole tier could have stayed unused while every test passed. It is scoped to the path now, points forward, and the charts are presented as the same kit reached by a different import — plus the rules say to pick the module matching the trade, since `salon.RebookRate` says something a bar chart cannot. Guarded, because this class of failure leaves no trace.
- **158 of 544 chart components CRASHED on empty data, and that took the whole PAGE down** (fixed 2026-07-31). The shape is uniform — `rows.reduce((a, r) => …, rows[0])` seeds with undefined on an empty array, returns undefined, and the next property read throws. React's error boundary catches it, so the customer sees *"This page didn't load"* rather than a missing chart. **This is the ordinary path, not an edge case**: `useRows` hands back `[]` before the query settles and on every site whose owner has added nothing yet, and it hit `salon` ×4, `dentistry` ×5, `garage` ×4, `gym` ×4, `hotel` ×3 — the trades this platform builds for. All 158 now carry the `if (!xs.length) return null` that 110 of the 141 modules already had.
- **Found by a per-card error boundary, after three harnesses lied.** Watching for `pageerror` reports a clean run, because React CATCHES the throw — that is how two of the 23 contact sheets were error pages and were sent out as charts. A blank-detector then printed `544 cards` from a JSON file beside a DOM-derived blank count, so an empty page read as *"544 cards · 0 rendered nothing"* — the most reassuring possible way to say nothing rendered. And a per-component probe called all 544 broken when its sub-routes simply never resolved. **A harness that cannot fail honestly is worse than no harness**; the one that worked fences each card separately and asserts the DOM node count it measured.
- **Every chart SIGNATURE shipped wrong, and was described to the model as authoritative.** `gen-chart-api.mjs` borrowed the component kit's extractor, which collapses whitespace and splits the prop block on `;` — and **the chart lib is written without semicolons**, so the whole block was one part, `indexOf(":")` found the first colon, and all 141 domains came out as one prop typed `object`: `ServiceMix(services: object)` for a five-field row array. Exactly the "a note that disagrees with the file is worse than no note" failure these are generated to prevent, arriving through a borrowed splitter. Now newline-aware, guarded by a test derived from the source at both ends.
- **`aero.FlightEnvelope` was reported as a bug and is not one** — it refuses `maxSpeed < stallSpeed`, which is a physically impossible envelope, and the synthesiser was feeding it exactly that.
- **All 882 are now verified by rendering, not just by compiling** (2026-07-31). Every one of the 600 in the undemoed domains is driven with synthesised data derived from its real prop types: **600 compile, 600 render, 0 throw on real data, 0 throw on empty**. Getting the last 56 there fixed four synthesiser gaps and, more usefully, **a real bug in `extractSignatures`**: it counted brackets on the RAW source, so a doc comment containing one threw the depth off and the scan ran past the end of a component into the next — reporting a merged signature naming props from both. Now every scan runs on a **length-preserving blank** of the comments, so indices stay valid against the real text. The same mistake appeared three separate times this session (`namedTypes` sliced raw source at stripped-source offsets; the guard inserter wrote one component's guard into another's destructuring), which makes it the pattern to watch: **strip comments by blanking, never by removing, whenever offsets are reused.**
- 616 tests. `test/chart-api.test.mjs` (26) asserts the whole chain — a file on disk, to a name in the prompt, to a lint that refuses the wrong import — because this feature has been dead at five separate layers before, one layer at a time.

## The whole backend is Neon (2026-07-30, owner's call)

**Direction change.** There is no prebuilt backend any more. The frontend keeps its component kit (`builder/lovable/template/`, the 46 shadcn components) and the database layer stays, but **the model writes the backend at build time** — its own constraints, triggers and logic — instead of choosing from a catalogue of named schema features. **Identity moves to Neon Auth.** Do not add named backend features; the answer to "the model can't express X" is now to make the substrate expressive, not to add a verb.

- **The reasoning that got here, worth keeping.** The catalogue was a guess about what businesses need, and the evidence it was the wrong shape is in this repo: ~30 schema-engine features, **11 of them undeclarable and reachable by nothing** (`roundRobin` lead routing and `sla` escalation, on a platform that makes barber shop sites). Two independent attempts at "let the model do backend logic" both landed on a menu of verbs — the deleted `/api/site/fn` was 8 verbs (`read save fetch ai email notify checkout respond`) with **zero code execution**. A menu is finite; someone must extend it forever.
- **The database is the sandbox, and that is why this is affordable.** Model-written Postgres runs inside the site's own project (per-site since 2026-07-29), so it cannot reach isibi, another site or a secret, and `statement_timeout` bounds a runaway. A wrong rule breaks that site's own form — visible at once, fixed by a revise. This is what made "run generated code" go from weeks of sandbox infrastructure to a field on a tool call.
- **The sandbox boundary, MEASURED 2026-07-30 rather than assumed — and the "cannot reach the network" half was wrong.** Refused: `pg_read_file` (permission denied), `COPY … TO PROGRAM` (needs `pg_execute_server_program`), `plpython3u` and `plperlu` (not in Neon's allowed-extensions list), `file_fdw` (permission denied to create). **Allowed: `dblink` and `postgres_fdw`** — Neon lets a project role install from a curated allow-list, which is a platform property we cannot switch off, and both are outbound Postgres connections from inside the database. So the honest claim is: **no code execution and no host access, but network egress is open.** `http` and `pg_net` are not installable (verified again 2026-08-07 against Neon's own extension table). **`pg_cron` IS installable** — this line said it was not, while the entry below already recorded the opposite; corrected 2026-08-07, and the constraint that actually matters is there, not here. **NOTHING IN THIS REPO PROVES ANY OF IT.** This bullet claimed "the e2e asserts the code-execution boundary and merely REPORTS the allow-list" — `neon-e2e` has 64 checks and not one touches an extension, and `grep` for `pg_available_extensions` / `CREATE EXTENSION` finds nothing anywhere in the repo that probes this. The whole boundary rests on one uncommitted session against a live database, which is why it drifted into two self-contradictions before anybody re-read it. Re-measure before relying on it, and if it matters, commit the probe.
- **`neon_auth."user".id` is UUID, and both halves of that were wrong in this file until 2026-07-30.** It said `users_sync.id` was TEXT; measured against a real project, **`users_sync` does not exist at all** (that name is Neon Auth's Stack Auth era) and the identity column is `uuid`, not text. That single fact is the migration: `applySiteSchema` stamped `owner_id INTEGER` off an integer identity in a hand-rolled `_users`, and every access rule, `teamScope`, the data API's row scoping, the audit log and `rows.ts` keyed off it — so `owner_id` becomes **UUID**. A real FK to identity IS possible; the run that said otherwise was declaring the referencing column `text` against a `uuid` key, which answers "incompatible types" and reads exactly like the platform refusing the reference.
- **The table is `"user"`, and the danger is NOT that it errors — it is that it does not.** Measured: `neon_auth.user` parses fine (a schema qualifier admits a reserved word), and bare `FROM user` **also succeeds**, because Postgres resolves it to the `USER` value function and hands back the current role instead of the identity table. So a query that drops the schema qualifier returns a wrong answer rather than a loud failure. Identity is therefore ALWAYS written schema-qualified, in the schema engine and in anything the model writes. Worth noting how this was arrived at: two drafts of the e2e check asserted a syntax error — first for the qualified form, then for the bare one — and both were wrong, from reasoning about the grammar instead of running it. It now asserts what comes back.
- **Enabling auth provisions nine tables IMMEDIATELY** (measured at 0s, no polling needed): `user`, `session`, `account`, `verification`, `jwks`, `organization`, `member`, `invitation`, `project_config`. That is most of what the ~12 hand-built auth modules do — `session` is per-device sessions, `account` is OAuth provider tokens, `verification` is email confirmation, `organization`/`member`/`invitation` is Better Auth's organization plugin (i.e. teams and invites), `jwks` means it mints JWTs, and `project_config` carries `social_providers` / `email_provider` / `email_and_password` / `trusted_origins` / `webhook_config` as the configuration surface.
- **Enabling it is `POST /projects/{id}/branches/{branch}/auth {"auth_provider":"better_auth"}`**, and `enableNeonAuth` runs it **on every build, not only at creation**. A project can exist with auth off — the create succeeded and the enable failed, or it predates the change — and a retried build REUSES the project, so enabling only at creation leaves that site permanently without identity while every retry reports success. Same reasoning that made `"already exists"` the one recoverable database error. A 409 or anything matching `/already/i` is a no-op; everything else throws.
- **Not best-effort, deliberately.** A build whose auth could not be enabled FAILS with `stage:"enable_auth"`. A caller can retry a failure and cannot retry a success, so quietly shipping a site nobody can sign in to is the worse outcome. And it runs BEFORE the schema apply, because `neon_auth` is still being created when the call returns.
- Neon Auth is included in every plan (60k MAU free, 1M on Launch/Scale), AWS-only — we are on `aws-us-east-1` — and unsupported with IP Allow or Private Networking, neither of which we use. **Project limits: Free 100, Launch 100, Scale 1,000 (increasable).** That cap is per Neon ACCOUNT, so per-site projects means 100 total sites across all users until Scale.
## The hand-built auth layer is DELETED (2026-07-30, owner's call)

- **"If we are not using it, I don't want it on the code."** 14 modules and 12 test files went: `site-auth` · `-routes` · `-flows` · `-methods` · `site-sessions` · `site-identity` · `site-otp` · `site-webauthn` · `site-oauth` · `site-lockout` · `site-pwned` · `site-invite` · `site-teams` · `site-audit`. `worker.js` 8,242 → 7,204 lines (the `/api/db/*/auth/*` router, `/members`, `/teams`, `/access`, `/events`, the `/reset` and `/verify` pages, `_users` DDL, the three dep factories). Also gone: `test/integration/auth-smoke.mjs` (1,314 lines) + its workflow, and the auth half of `rows.ts` (695 → 309). Tests 1,059 → 605, all passing.
- **The ORDER was inverted on purpose, and the note this replaces had it the other way round.** It said deleting must come LAST, because stripping the layer before its replacement is wired leaves the data API with no notion of who a visitor is. That reasoning was about breaking live sites — and `site_backends` was measured EMPTY (0 sites, 0 per-site projects) before a line was cut, so there was nothing to break. Deleting first also avoided threading the integer→UUID migration through twelve modules that were about to be deleted, which is most of what that migration was.
- **`site-claim.mjs` SURVIVES** — a claim token's whole point is that the holder has no account anywhere, so it never depended on identity. It absorbed the token primitives (`sessionKey`, `signToken`/`verifyToken`, `signClaim`/`verifyClaim`) from the deleted `site-auth.mjs`. **The derivation string is deliberately unchanged**: claim links sit in confirmation emails for 90 days and altering it would silently void every one already sent. `claim` is now the ONLY kind, so `verifyClaim`'s exact `use === "claim"` is the whole check — the deny-list-vs-allow-list bug class goes away with the kinds and returns the moment a second kind does.
- **Member tables now answer 401 to everybody, which is the SAFE direction — not a stub.** `resolveSiteVisitor` returns null and `site-data.mjs` already refused on that (`needsVisitor && !visitor` → 401), so `user`/`feed`/`admin` are unreachable rather than readable by strangers. The dangerous shape would have been a placeholder visitor, because then every member read matches whatever that placeholder owns. Same for the client: the hooks are GONE from `rows.ts` rather than stubbed to "signed out", because a stub compiles, ships, and shows a login form that can never succeed. `getSessionToken` alone survives — the localStorage key is what Neon Auth's session has to land in.
- **`teamScope` and `teamRead` were pulled OUT of the designer tool**, or this change would have recreated the exact trap documented five times above: a flag the model can declare that nothing acts on. Both widened a read through `_users`; the read path now scopes to the caller's own rows only.
- **`site-audit.mjs` went too, and that was a judgement call.** Every emitter was in the auth layer, so it would have logged nothing — an unused module by the rule being applied. It is in git history, so wiring Neon Auth's events means restoring 356 lines of privacy logic rather than rewriting it (`shapeMeta`'s nine-field allow-list, the per-site address fingerprint, /24 and /48 IP truncation).
- **The drift guards did their job.** Rewriting the generator's rules broke four tests in `page-gen.test.mjs` that assert every hook the rules name exists in the template — which is precisely the check that stops the model being told to import something that is not there. The list-hook invariant was kept but re-derived: it named `useSessions` explicitly, and now discovers list hooks by pattern, so it survives one being deleted.
- **`api-auth.test.mjs`'s `/api/db/*` check was reading the FIRST dispatch**, which made it depend on route order — when the auth block above it went, the first dispatch became visitor uploads and the test went red for a reason unrelated to gating. It now checks every `/api/db` dispatch.
## Neon Auth is WIRED (2026-07-30)

- **`owner_id` is UUID.** Not text, which is what this file claimed and called "the single fact the migration rests on". The type appears twice — the `CREATE` and the revise-path `ALTER` — and `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` does not change the type of a column already there, so a disagreement is invisible on a fresh site and wrong on every revised one. A source-derived test asserts it sees BOTH sites before comparing them; with one found, "they agree" is vacuous. `_audit.actor_id` moved with it, since a trigger writes `NEW."owner_id"` into it. **No foreign key to `neon_auth."user"`, deliberately** though the reference is accepted: `neon_auth` is Neon's schema synced by their service, and this DDL re-runs on every revise against a table that may hold rows, where adding the constraint retroactively FAILS if any `owner_id` no longer matches a live user. An orphan reads as belongs-to-nobody, which is what `site-owner.mjs` already decided for a deleted member.
- **`resolveSiteVisitor` is one SQL read, not an HTTP call** — Neon Auth keeps sessions IN the site's own database (`neon_auth.session.token`, `userId` → `neon_auth."user"`), so verifying a visitor is a join on a connection the request already holds. No third party on the visitor path, nothing to keep in sync. Expiry is compared in SQL rather than against a JS clock, so two clocks never have to agree. Better Auth's `banned` is honoured HERE and not only on an account screen, because this is the half that reads rows; a ban whose `banExpires` has passed has lapsed, spelled out rather than left to truthiness.
- **Sign-in is PROXIED through the Worker** at `/api/db/<slug>/auth/*` → the site's Neon Auth server. Three reasons and each decides it: the page never learns an endpoint or a key, so nothing has to be decided about what is safe to bake into a static bundle; it is **same-origin**, and a published site served from isibi.ai calling Neon directly would need CORS plus a cross-site cookie — the configuration most likely to work in development and fail in Safari; and the URL the client uses is **unchanged**, so generated pages never learn identity moved. Responses pass through as-is, because a proxy that reinterprets "wrong password" is a second place it has to be spelled. Rate-limited per source (`AUTH_PROXY_PER_MIN` 20) on `CF-Connecting-IP` only — the `X-Forwarded-For` fallback used elsewhere is client-settable and would let one caller mint a fresh bucket per request.
- **The auth endpoint comes from the provisioning response**, which `enableNeonAuth` used to discard — the one call that creates identity told us nothing about reaching it. Now returned and stored in the site's own `_meta` as `auth_info` (per-site, read only on a request already holding that connection, gone when the site is). Recording it is best-effort while *enabling* is not: auth being on is what makes the site usable, and a lost note of where it is gets re-fetched by the next build. **The one thing here not measured against a real project is the FIELD NAME**, so `siteAuthBase` tries the plausible names and falls back to the first `https://` URL in the body; tighten it once a real build logs the shape.
- **`/members` is rebuilt on `neon_auth."user"`** — list, grant a role, suspend, reinstate, remove. Suspension writes Better Auth's own `banned` rather than a flag of ours, or the auth server keeps honouring a session we decided to refuse. Roles stay allow-listed against what the site's own tables name in `writeRoles`. A member id is a **UUID**, checked before it reaches SQL — malformed against a `uuid` column is a type error the owner would see as a 500 rather than "no such member". Deleting a member still leaves their rows.
- **Every reference is schema-qualified and `"user"` is quoted, and that is load bearing.** Postgres resolves a bare `user` to the `USER` value function and returns the current role name instead of erroring, so an unqualified members list hands the owner one row containing `neondb_owner` and looks exactly like a site with one member. Asserted.
- **The client hooks are back**: `useMember`, `useSignup`, `useLogin`, `useLogout`, `useRequestReset`, `Member.id` a UUID string. `tokenOf` reads the session token in ONE place because sign-up and sign-in both answer that shape, and a token is stored only when there IS one — writing `undefined` leaves a page believing it is signed in with a session nothing accepts. A 401 on `get-session` CLEARS the stored token rather than letting every later read fail with a 401 the page cannot explain.
- **The deleted hook-existence drift test came back DERIVED.** The old one listed eleven hook names by hand, so it guarded only the eleven somebody remembered and had to be deleted rather than fixed when the layer went. The new one scans the rules for cited `useThing(` and checks each against what `rows.ts` exports — it covers whatever the prompt happens to say today. Mutation-checked by naming a hook that does not exist. The failure it prevents has happened more than once: the model imports something absent, `tsc` refuses the page, and the site publishes as the placeholder.
- **Teams are Neon Auth's ORGANIZATIONS now (2026-07-30).** `site-teams.mjs` is a REWRITE, not a restore: membership used to live in our own `_teams` table with an integer `_users.team_id`, and it is `neon_auth.member.organizationId` read off a join. Two consequences — a team id is a **UUID**, so `team_id` is a uuid column; and there are no team-CRUD routes any more, because creating an organization and adding people is Better Auth's API through the same `/api/db/<slug>/auth/*` proxy. One membership is used, oldest first, because "which team am I acting in" is a question a small business tool does not have.
  - **The assertion that matters is unchanged: a member with NO team sees ONLY their own rows.** Every naive "same team" implementation fails outward (`IS NOT DISTINCT FROM` matches null to null), and since a site starts with nobody in an organization that is the DEFAULT state, not an edge case. The clause is parenthesised, or ANDing it onto a caller's filters lets the OR re-associate and escape them.
  - **Reachability is asserted as a CHAIN from the source**, because this feature has been dead at five separate layers and each time four of the five links were fine: the designer can declare it · it survives the normaliser's allow-list · the table gets a uuid `team_id` · the visitor query reads `neon_auth.member` · **the visitor OBJECT carries it** (the fourth failure was selecting it and then dropping it) · the read path widens · a write stamps.
  - **A team reads AND edits**, unlike the deleted `teamRead` which was a hierarchy and widened reads only — a tool where a colleague cannot correct a record is not the tool anybody asked for. That edit scope is a SECOND call site, and a mutation that nulled it survived the whole suite until a PATCH test existed. 13 mutations, all caught; the last two survivors were both real gaps.
  - **The test harness taught something worth keeping**: its `sqlExec` answered `{changes:1}` without recording, so every write was invisible to assertions and a delete with its scope removed looked exactly like a passing test. A fake that is less capable than the real thing hides bugs the same way one that is MORE capable does (see `setTotp`).
- **Still to do:** confirming the bearer token the client stores is the same `session.token` the resolver looks up — the one seam only a real sign-in proves. The audit log, restorable from git rather than rewritten. **And the two Neon features that overlap code we still own: the Data API** (auto-generated REST + RLS, which is much of what `site-data.mjs`'s 482 lines do) **and RLS-with-Auth** (access control in the database instead of `owner_id=?` appended in the Worker). **The owner's call is Neon-only — not both** — so `site-data.mjs` and the `/api/db` row routes go, and the published site talks to the Data API directly.

## What the database ACTUALLY has (measured 2026-07-30)

**98 extensions.** Asked `pg_available_extensions` instead of guessing names, which is what the earlier probe did — it tried four, swallowed every refusal reason, and turned an inference into a stated fact. Two of those statements were wrong.

- **`http` is genuinely ABSENT**, and so is `pg_net`. Confirmed twice, by the database and by Neon's docs. So there is no outbound HTTP from inside Postgres, and anything that has to reach the world still belongs in the Worker. That part of the earlier answer stands.
- **`pg_cron@1.6` IS available and I said it was not.** With a real constraint attached: *"Jobs must be scheduled from the database configured in `cron.database_name`"*, so a job cannot live in a site's own `site_<slug>` database, and per Neon's docs jobs *"only run when your compute is active"* — which for a per-site project that scales to zero means a reminder for a site nobody is visiting never fires. Usable for busy sites, not a general scheduler, so the Worker's 2-minute cron stays the answer.
- **`pg_session_jwt@0.5.0` — Neon's own extension for the thing `site-rls.mjs` hand-rolled.** It verifies the session JWT inside Postgres and exposes `auth.user_id()`. The first version of that file built the same thing out of `current_setting('request.jwt.claims')`, PostgREST's convention, and I flagged it as a guess. Now both forms are emitted and the engine picks by whether the extension installed — the fallback is not dead weight, because a function referencing a function that does not exist fails to PARSE and takes every policy with it.
- **Things we hand-built or deleted that Postgres has**: `anon@2.5.1` (PostgreSQL Anonymizer — column masking, which was `maskFields`), `pgaudit@17.1` (the audit log deleted this morning), `pg_search@0.15.26` (BM25, better than the tsvector engine), `moddatetime@1.0` (the `updated_at` trigger), `pgjwt@0.2.0` (mint/verify tokens in the database), `pg_uuidv7`/`pgx_ulid`, `pg_jsonschema@0.3.3` (which is `jsonShapes`), `pg_partman`, `pg_repack`, `hypopg` + `online_advisor` (index advice), `pgtap@1.3.3` (tests that run inside the database).
- **Things worth knowing exist**: `plv8@3.2.3` — **JavaScript in the database**, so a model-written function does not have to be PL/pgSQL. `vector@0.8.0` and `postgis@3.5.0`, both confirmed installable. `pg_graphql@1.5.11` (a GraphQL API over the schema). `rag` + `rag_bge_small_en_v15` + `pg_tiktoken` — Neon's embedding extensions, so semantic search needs no external service. `timescaledb@2.17.1`, `pg_duckdb`, `pg_mooncake` for analytics. `tcn` for change notification.
- **The lesson worth keeping**: a four-name `CREATE EXTENSION` probe that discards its errors reads as an inventory and is not one. The catalogue is one `SELECT` away and should have been the first question.

## Row-level security (`site-rls.mjs`, 2026-07-30)

- **The first half of that swap, landed BEFORE the deletion on purpose.** Every generated table gets `ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` and a policy per access level, keyed on `app_user_id()` — **OUR** function, reading PostgREST's `request.jwt.claims` setting, so if Neon exposes identity differently ONE function body changes and every policy keeps working. `current_setting(..., true)` is missing_ok, and without it the call RAISES on every connection with no claims set — i.e. every Worker connection — turning each policy into an error rather than a refusal.
- **Applying it changes nothing today, which is why it could ship first**: a table's owner bypasses its own policies and the Worker connects as the owner. So the policies can be written, reviewed and asserted while the tested path still carries the traffic, instead of delete-and-replace in one step where a policy permitting one clause too much is discovered by a customer. **GRANTS are what actually expose a table**, and they land in the same change that enables the Data API — guarded by a test that fails if `grantsFor` is ever called early.
- **`collect` is write-only by OMISSION** — no SELECT policy at all, and with RLS on, no policy means no rows. That cannot be weakened by putting a filter in the wrong clause. `display` reads for anyone with no write policy. `user` is own-rows on reads AND writes (`WITH CHECK` on insert, or a member could create a row owned by somebody else; BOTH clauses on update, or a row could be reassigned to another owner on the way out). `feed` shares the read and scopes the write. `admin` reads for anyone signed in and **refuses writes at the database**, because `writeRoles` names roles that mean something to this application and nothing to Postgres.
- **Every policy shape is dropped before any is created**, not only the ones the current level needs: a revise changing a table from `display` to `user` would otherwise keep its old `USING (true)` read policy, and Postgres ORs permissive policies together. The trash clause is ANDed and asserted so — one draft of that test matched the `OR` inside `FOR SELECT` and failed on a correct policy.
- 18 tests.

## `internal` protected nothing — Postgres grants EXECUTE to PUBLIC (2026-08-07)

- **A declared function marked `internal: true` was executable by `anonymous` and by `authenticated`**, measured by `neon e2e` on the first run that asked. Every layer above had done its job: the designer could declare the flag, `normalizeSchema` kept it, and `functionSql` skipped the GRANT. **Three correct layers over a Postgres default that undid all of them** — `EXECUTE` on a new function is granted to `PUBLIC`, a role every other role belongs to, so withholding ours withheld nothing.
- **The exposure was real, not theoretical.** A `confirm.fn` takes a row id and returns a customer's name, message and email out of a `collect` table; every model function is `SECURITY DEFINER` so it runs as the owner and **bypasses RLS**; ids are sequential. Anyone who could reach the site's Data API could have read the whole table by counting — the enumeration the claim-token design exists to prevent, reached by an easier route.
- **The fix is `REVOKE ALL … FROM PUBLIC` on EVERY declared function, before the grants.** Public ones are revoked too, so their two grants are what actually reaches them rather than decoration over a default that already admitted everybody. **Order is load-bearing** — revoked after granting takes the privilege straight back off and every public function 404s. The signature is on the REVOKE, since the bare form is ambiguous the moment a site declares two functions of one name.
- **THE ANALOGY THAT CAUSED IT, written down so it is not re-derived.** The design was copied from `_secrets`, which is safe by never passing through `grantsFor`. That works for a TABLE, which Postgres grants to nobody by default. It does **not** transfer to a FUNCTION, which Postgres grants to PUBLIC. Omission protects one and not the other, and only the table half of that pair was ever true.
- **The unit test asserted "no GRANT is emitted" and passed for the entire time the function was wide open** — a property that was true and worthless, which is the sharpest form of this codebase's recurring failure. It asserts the REVOKE now, in both directions, plus the ordering. **Only a real database could have said so**, which is the argument for the e2e paying for itself: the check cost one extra assertion on a project that was already provisioned.
- **`app_user_id()` and `app_team_id()` are deliberately NOT revoked** — policies reference them and evaluate as the caller, so PUBLIC execute is what makes them work. Safe for the reason already recorded: the first reads the caller's own verified JWT (connection state, not role state) and the second takes no argument, so neither can answer for anybody else.
- **`neon e2e` is 74 checks and filtered to `main`.** Landing work here is two pushes of one commit (feature branch, then fast-forward main) and with no branch filter both matched, so every change provisioned **two** real Neon projects. Projects are capped per Neon ACCOUNT and per-site projects already consume that cap, which makes this the one form of CI waste that can block a customer's build.

## The member tier could not evaluate its own policies (2026-08-05)

- **Every member read and write answered `42501 permission denied for schema auth`** — not a policy refusing a row, the policy could not be RUN. `app_user_id()` is SECURITY INVOKER, so it executes as whoever asked, and Neon's Data API runs a request as `authenticated` or `anonymous`, neither of which had USAGE on the schema `pg_session_jwt` creates. The function existed, the owner could call it, every GRANT on every table was right, and `user`/`feed`/`admin` did not work on any site. **Invisible from both sides of the same file:** `display` reads are `USING (true)` and `collect` writes `WITH CHECK (true)`, so the two levels with live coverage were the two that could not break.
- **The fix is SECURITY DEFINER on `app_user_id()`, NOT a grant** — and the grant is what this line said first, so the correction is the useful part. `GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA auth` **reports success and changes nothing**: measured against a real project, both statements returned `ok:true` while `has_schema_privilege` stayed false for both roles, so that schema is Neon's to open and not ours. Silent success is the worst of the three outcomes, and finding it out cost a CI cycle reading a job log for an error line that was never written — which is why `applySiteSchema` now RETURNS `authGrants` and the e2e prints it. Definer rights are safe here because of what the body reads: `auth.user_id()` decodes the session's verified JWT, which is connection state and not role state, so a database role cannot get an identity out of it, and an unreadable claim returns NULL — the direction that shows nobody anything. `SESSION_JWT_GRANTS` stays (one statement per role, the documented mechanism, correct on a deployment where the schema IS ours) but nothing depends on it.
- **Nothing stamped `owner_id` or `team_id`, so the member tier could READ and never WRITE.** The Worker's data path did, and it went with `site-data.mjs` on 2026-07-30 — so a member's insert reached Postgres with `owner_id` NULL, `WITH CHECK (owner_id = app_user_id())` evaluated NULL, and every write answered 403 while every read worked, which is why it read as partial success rather than a missing column default. Both columns now `DEFAULT` the caller. **The default and the policy are both needed**: the default fills the column in for a client that omits it, the WITH CHECK stops one that SENDS it from claiming another member's id. The revise path needs its own `ALTER COLUMN … SET DEFAULT`, because `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` does nothing to a column that already exists. **`team_id`'s cast is GUARDED, not bare** — `app_team_id()` returns text and a plain `::uuid` on anything else THROWS, i.e. every write to a team table fails; guarded, a non-uuid becomes NULL and the row is merely not shared. (`neon_auth.member."organizationId"` was measured as `uuid`, so the guard should never fire.)
- **`member smoke` is 35/35** — `user`, `feed` and `admin` are proved live for the first time since `auth-smoke.mjs` was deleted: own-rows scoping across two real members, a shared feed, an admin table that reads and refuses writes.
- **The team clause got the OPPOSITE treatment and the asymmetry is the point.** It selected `neon_auth.member` inline — same wall, one table over — but that table is every member of every organization on the site, and one policy clause is not a reason to publish it. `app_team_id()` is SECURITY DEFINER with `search_path` pinned, every name qualified, and **no argument**, so it can only answer for the caller. **Compared as `::text` on both sides**: `team_id` is uuid, `organizationId` is whatever Neon's Better Auth made it, and `uuid = text` is not an operator — a type guess there is a policy that fails to CREATE, which the apply loop logs and carries on from, leaving the table with no read policy at all.
- **The guard was watching the layer below.** `neon e2e` asks `has_table_privilege`, which answers whether a role may ASK, not whether the policy attached can be EVALUATED — and every query in it runs as the table's OWNER, which bypasses its own policies. It now runs a real SELECT under `SET LOCAL ROLE`, and reports an **honest skip** if the connection cannot assume the role, because silence there reads as coverage.
- **A session token is not a Data API token, and it takes a second member to see it.** `member-smoke` handed member two the token straight out of sign-up, so both own-rows checks were failing on the credential rather than the scoping. `rows.ts` does that exchange in `keepAuthHeaders`; a test that skips it tests a path no page takes.

## The generator's last three error classes (2026-08-05)

- **An inline object literal was collapsed to `object` over 46 characters** — the same failure as the truncated string union, one type-kind over, and worse: `COMPONENT_TYPES` only resolves `export type` names, so a shape lost in `shortType` is unrecoverable. Measured live: `BulkActions(actions: object[])` was called with `{label, onClick}` against `{label, onSelect, destructive?}`. `FilterBar`'s equivalent was under the limit and worked, so whether the model could write the call came down to how many characters the shape happened to spell. **Nothing is collapsed now** and a shape too long to print keeps its property NAMES, which is most of the value; 35 `object` props and 10 `function` props are both zero. +807 tokens on a block that rides in the cache at 0.1×.
- **`Row` names `created_at: string`.** A declared column gets a type in the model's own `type Deal = Row & {…}`; a platform-managed one is left on the index signature as `string | number | boolean | null`, which is TS2322 against anything wanting `string | number | Date`. Non-optional because `site-schema.mjs` adds it to EVERY table unconditionally — `updated_at` is the one behind the `timestamps` flag — and a test asserts that, since the type is a claim about that file.
- **`Row` is a LITERAL index signature, not `Record<…> & {…}`** (2026-08-05). Naming `created_at` fixed one half and the model went straight to the other: `at: deal.updated_at ?? deal.created_at` is `string | number | boolean` (the `??` only strips the `null`). **An intersection cannot carry an optional property** — the member type is intersected with the index signature's, so `updated_at?: string` collapses to plain `string` and claims a column that only exists behind `timestamps`. Written as one literal with `| undefined` in the index signature, the optional marker means what it says. The date prop had failed in two consecutive evals wearing two different types, which is a column and not variance. **Guarded by COMPILING the page that failed** (`site-build`), negative first, so a `Row` that ever became `any` fails the check instead of passing it.
- **`data-table` and `Column<T>` were both invisible to the model** — the same class twice, one layer apart, and each was found by fixing the other. `export function DataTable<T extends Record<string, unknown>>` did not match the extractor's `<[^>()]*>` (the class stops at the FIRST `>`), so the component had NO signature and the model guessed `data=` where the prop is `rows=`; with `T` unable to infer it fell back to the constraint and every `cell: (row: Deal) => …` became a contravariance error — **eight of nine errors in one sample from one absent entry**.
