- **A NEON BLIP WAS CACHED AS A PERMANENT FACT.** `neonOrgId` swallowed every error and wrote `_orgId = null` — a value meaning one specific thing, *"this key cannot address /users/me, so it is org-scoped"* — so one 5xx during a first provision made every later build in that isolate create its project in the PERSONAL account instead of the org. It succeeds, it is recorded, the site works; it just bills the wrong home and counts against the wrong project cap. Per-isolate, so it heals on recycle, **which is also why nobody would ever catch it.** A 4xx is cached and nothing else is.
- **A STALE NUMBER IN THE HIGHEST-LEVERAGE PROMPT ON THE PLATFORM, sitting directly above a DERIVED list.** Rule 3 said *"That holds for 2,026 of the 2,042 modules"* as prose, written once and never re-measured, over an exceptions list computed from `UI_EXPORTS`. **Three sources, three different answers**: the prose said 2,042, the audit measured 2,112, and the population the LIST is drawn from is 2,040. All three are defensible counts of something — 2,112 is every file in the kit, 2,040 is those with an extractable signature — and only one of them describes the same set as the sentence's own list. **That is the argument for deriving it rather than measuring it**: even an auditor measuring live picked the wrong denominator. Both halves come from `UI_EXPORT_EXCEPTIONS` now, guarded in both directions (every named exception really lacks its PascalCase export, every module that has one is absent). The `pagesRequest` docstring's *"`PAGE_RULES` is ~7,000 tokens"* was the same class at 5x — the string is ~33,000 — and is now marked as the original measurement rather than a present-tense fact, because the conclusion rested on the RATIOS and a re-measured number goes stale the same way.
- **TWO NO-PHOTO CAUSES WORE EACH OTHER'S SENTENCE, and `imageNote` is the ONE field built to separate them.** A full 200-file upload library and an empty balance both clamp the budget to zero, and the credit sentence was the only answer for both — so an owner at the cap was told to **buy credits that cannot possibly help**, on the one build where the fix is to delete a few uploads. And a `@@IMG:@@` token with nothing inside it is DROPPED rather than sent (a deliberate refusal to pay $0.15 to see what an image model does with an empty prompt) while the customer was told *"couldn't make the photographs"* — blaming us for something never attempted and giving them nothing to do. Four causes, four sentences, each naming something the customer can act on. **`planImages` has returned `empty` since it was written and no caller had ever read it.** The discriminator for a REAL failure is `error` rather than the empty count, so a page with one described picture and one bare token still reports our failure when ours is what failed; and **an unreadable upload listing keeps the credit sentence**, because "your library is full" said when we could not look is an instruction to delete photographs that may not need deleting.
- **`setTitle` WAS THE ONE EXPORT IN `site-meta.mjs` WITH NO BEHAVIOURAL TEST — and it is where the `$$`/`$&`/`$'` corruption bug sat.** Its only guard anywhere was a source-read asserting the Worker CALLS it: the wiring layer watching the layer below the break. Its escaping, its no-op rule, its truncation and its replacement were held by nothing.
  - **THE `$` CASES ASSERT THE CLASS, NOT THREE SPELLINGS**, and had to be compared through an UNESCAPE — `$&` contains an ampersand, which `setTitle` correctly turns into `&amp;`, so asserting the raw string fails on the escaping the test is not about.
  - **THE PRESENCE CHECK IS PROVABLY INERT AND IS KEPT ANYWAY.** `String.replace` with no match returns the string unchanged, so `if (!TITLE_TAG.test(src)) return src` cannot change the answer — measured, and **no behavioural test could ever catch it**, because nothing observable separates "returned early" from "replaced nothing". It holds by the tested pattern and the replaced pattern being identical, which is one edit away from false, so the two literals became ONE shared constant and the sharing is asserted STRUCTURALLY. The eleventh inert mutant this repo has recorded, and the first where the fix made a structurally-catchable property out of a behaviourally-invisible one.
- 2813 tests, **27/27 mutations as expected** from a verified-green baseline — 26 caught plus a deliberate comment-only control that had to SURVIVE, since a sweep where everything dies is not measuring what it thinks. Three further mutants on the `setTitle` sharing, all caught.

## The contrast check called the commonest hero there is unreadable (2026-08-14)

**Round 18.** `probe()`'s contrast pass read `backgroundColor` and nothing else — and **a gradient sets no background COLOUR**. So a hero with a photograph, a `from-black/60` scrim and white text walked past both branches of `solidBehind`, reached `body`, and measured white on white. **Measured in a real browser: 1:1.**

- **IT SHIPS TO THE CUSTOMER, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES IT MORE THAN A NOISY LOG LINE.** The verdict rides the build response AND every edit lane as *"has text that is nearly invisible"* — so we were telling somebody their correct page was broken, on the single most common hero this platform builds. This repo rates a false alarm strictly worse than the miss, and this is that rule's own check failing it. **The colour lint could not have caught it either**: `TAILWIND_PALETTE` lists no `white` and no `black`.
- **TWO HALVES, BECAUSE THE ANCESTOR FIX COVERS ONLY HALF.** The family exemplars NEST the caption inside the gradient; the kit's own `CoverImage` puts the `SafeImage`, the scrim and the caption as **SIBLINGS**, which an ancestor walk never visits. Measured before the fix: both forms reported 1:1 independently.
- **ORDERING IS LOAD-BEARING, in both halves.** The colour branches run FIRST, and `painted` skips an image sitting over an opaque colour — because `.bg-primary` on a gloss theme is an opaque `--primary` with a gradient laid over it, so the COLOUR is the honest answer there. Get it the other way round and every button label on a gloss site silently stops being checked. Pinned by a real gloss-button case that must keep being reported.
- **THE ANCESTOR BRANCH LOOKS REDUNDANT AND IS NOT — and the way that was settled is the lesson.** A mutant removing it **survived all seven cases**, because both customer-facing shapes are caught by `coveredByPicture` instead. It reads exactly like an inert mutant, which this file has now recorded ten times. It is not: a picture-backed ancestor with **no box of its own** (`display: contents`) never enters `painted`, so only the walk can see it — constructed as case 8 and measured, that page reports 1:1 without the branch while every other case stays clean. **A mutant that survives against the wrong corpus is indistinguishable from one that changes nothing**, and the answer is a new case rather than a deleted line.
- **THE OVER-SKIP IS REAL AND IS STATED IN THE SOURCE RATHER THAN LEFT IMPLICIT.** Text over a photograph with NO scrim stops being reported. Defensible on exactly two grounds: the old answer there was 1:1 **regardless of what the photograph actually looks like** — right by accident at best — and this check's charter prefers a miss to a false alarm.
- **`test/integration/contrast-cases.mjs` IS ITS OWN HARNESS because it needs no container, no dist and no model** — a static page and about two seconds — so it can afford the cases that PIN the boundary (the ordering, the ancestor with no box) rather than only the two shapes a compiled site can show. It **lifts the real `probe()` source** the way `render-check.mjs` hands it to `page.evaluate`, so it drives the real one and not a copy, and refuses to run if it cannot find it.
- **EVERY CASE ASSERTS THE OBSERVER WAS ALIVE.** Five of the sixteen checks pass by an ABSENCE, and a probe that scanned nothing satisfies all of them — the vacuous-clean failure this repo has already shipped once in this exact check, where `[].every(…)` reported a fixed panel clean while the render step had returned `ok:false`. `out.scanned > 0` is asserted per case.
- **AND THE TWO CUSTOMER-FACING SHAPES GO THROUGH A REAL COMPILED SITE** in `site-build` — nested and sibling, plus the faint true positive, since without that last one the fix is indistinguishable from the contrast pass switched off. Gated on `render.ok === true` for the same reason the panel assertion beside it is.
- 2786 tests, `site build` **120/120**, contrast-cases 16/16, **4/4 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline. **Not proven on a customer build** — the Anthropic account is empty — but every claim here was measured in a real browser rather than reasoned.

## The comment said ONE COPY and there were two (2026-08-14)

**Round 17.** `makeSitePhoto`'s own header reads *"EXTRACTED SO THERE IS ONE COPY… the sniff is the only thing standing between an image model's answer and an SVG served inline from our own origin, so a second copy that forgets it is a stored XSS."* It was written the day that function shipped and was **false from the first line** — `buySitePhotos` kept the identical generate → sniff → hash → put chain inline, and `makeSitePhoto` had exactly one caller elsewhere. An extraction parked directly above its intended caller and never wired in.

- **THE FIX IS THE WIRING, AND THE `try` STAYS.** An earlier attempt deleted the per-shot `try`/`catch` on the grounds that a never-throwing reader makes it dead — which is wrong twice over. `Promise.all` **rejects** if any element does, and that rejection propagates into `publishPages` where `applyImages` never runs and the raw `@@IMG:…@@` tokens ship: a truthy `src` and a broken-image glyph on the published page, which is the live failure the neighbouring sweep guard was written after. It is belt-and-braces held by a property of a function one edit away from changing — kept, and **said so in the source**, which is this repo's own convention for that case.
- **BOTH CALL SITES IN ONE COMMIT, because the return shape changed.** `makeSitePhoto` answers `{url, error}` now, and a caller still reading `if (made)` is truthy for `{url: null}` — charging for a photograph that was never made and handing a page `[object Object]` in a `src`. Asserted over every `= await makeSitePhoto(` in the file rather than the two known today.
- **THREE GUARDS WERE HOLDING THE DUPLICATE, NOT THE ORIGINAL.** They sliced `buySitePhotos` and matched the inline chain, so they described the copy and said nothing about the function whose comment claims to be the only one. They read the shared chain now.
- **MY OWN ONE-COPY CHECK FALSE-ALARMED ON CORRECT CODE, in the check written to prevent exactly that.** Counting `SITES_BUCKET.put(uploadKey(` gives 2 — the second is the LOGO store, which writes bytes the OWNER attached and `runLogoEdit` already sniffed. A legitimately separate path. Keyed on `genSitePhoto(` instead: what must never be duplicated is the chain handling bytes an image MODEL sent.
- **AN ABSENCE GUARD MATCHED ITS OWN FIX'S COMMENT, for the fourth time this session.** *"the catch must not rethrow"* went red because the new catch explains that `makeSitePhoto` "does not throw today". Comments blanked before the absence is judged.
- **TWO MUTANTS PROVED THE PER-PICTURE REASON WAS HELD BY NOTHING** — dropping it from a refusal, and destructuring only `url` at the call site, both passed the entire suite. That field is the difference between a reported failure and a site quietly missing its pictures. Guarded as a property: every failure exit reports a reason except the empty-prompt one, which must stay silent so a token nobody described does not set `images.error` on a build where nothing failed.
- 2786 tests, `site build` 117/117, **7/7 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline. **Nothing here has ever run** — the fal balance is empty, so no photograph has been generated.

## Four things the build knew and would not say (2026-08-14)

**Round 16 — the works-but-cannot-say-so disease, four more instances, and one operational lesson that cost the whole batch once.**

- **THE BUILD RESPONSE REPORTED THE STAMPED PRESET, NOT THE RESOLVED PAIR.** `normalizeSchema` stamps `access: "collect"` on any table that did not name one of the five presets — and the design tool tells the model to leave `access` OUT when it sets a `read`/`write` pair instead. So a public menu declared as a pair was reported to every reader as **"collect"**, the opposite of what it does, including to `build smoke`'s own access assertions. **The fifth copy of this misread**: the lint, the digest, the seeder and the owner routes were each fixed in turn; this was the last.
- **`failed` WAS COMPUTED BY THE MODULE AND DROPPED BY BOTH LANES.** The seed top-up sets it when the model CALL threw; a call that RETURNED junk answers with the same empty `rows` and the same `gaps` — so on the wire a dead provider and a nonsense answer were byte-identical, and the customer's site has an empty price list either way. Strictly `=== true`, and omitted when false so a working build's response is unchanged.
- **THE EMPTY-SCHEMA 422 SAID ONE THING FOR FOUR CAUSES, with nothing logged.** No tool call · called it and declared nothing · an answer that would not parse · every table NAME refused. `designSiteSchema` had `use`, `stop_reason` and the block list all in scope and **threw them away**, so by the time anyone read the refusal the shape was gone and a real generator outage was indistinguishable from a brief that genuinely described nothing. A `shape` field now rides back — **types and the stop reason only, never `use.input`**, which echoes the customer's brief and this goes to a log.
- **`og:image`'S COMMENT CLAIMED "the first thing the owner uploaded" AND THAT IS NOT WHAT IT PICKS.** `siteUploadList` never sorts and never projects R2's `uploaded`, so objects arrive in key order — and a key is a SHA-256 of the file's own bytes. The pick is the smallest hash among the owner's objects. **Corrected rather than acted on** (owner-triaged: the residue is that you cannot CHOOSE which): sorting by time would silently swap the social card of every live site with two eligible objects, and would be worse than arbitrary rather than better — a first build's bought photographs and the logo are the EARLIEST objects, so they would win permanently over anything the owner uploads later.
- **`git stash` RACED A BACKGROUND AGENT AND LOST FOUR UNCOMMITTED FIXES.** A skeptic agent — running with write tools to *measure* a proposed fix — edited `worker.js` mid-flight, applying finding #19's change **without** the correction its own sibling skeptic had given (the per-shot `try` deleted, which is exactly what makes `Promise.all` reject and the `@@IMG:@@` sweep never run). Three photo guards went red, the diff showed hunks nobody in this session wrote, and a `git stash`/`pop` around a diagnostic run came back to a clean tree. **The rule this repo already has for mutation sweeps applies to agents: never let two writers share the working tree, and never `git stash` while one is running.** Cheap here only because the four changes were small and fresh.
- 2784 tests, **9/9 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline. One survivor first time round: a substring match satisfied by `if (false) console.warn(…)`, so the diagnostic could be switched off with the guard still green — anchored at the start of the statement now.

## An auth blip locked the app, and the gate could not tell (2026-08-14)

**Round 15. `authUser` was the last gate in the file still reading "cannot tell" as "no", and this codebase had already fixed that exact class twice one layer down** — the ledger read ("a zero balance and an unreadable one are different answers") and `assertOwner` ("cannot tell" is 503, "not yours" is 404).

- **THE CUSTOMER-FACING HALF IS NOT SUBTLE.** `apiFetch` calls `showAuthGate()` on ANY 401, and `showAuthGate` sets `shell.inert = true`. So a GoTrue outage, a timeout or a network throw threw a **modal sign-in screen over a live session, made the whole app non-interactive, and invited a retry against the same down provider.** Silently: no distinct status, and not one `console.error`, so support had nothing to go on either.
- **A SIDE CHANNEL, NOT A RETURN VALUE, and that is the non-negotiable part.** 39 call sites read `authUser`'s answer AS A USER, so any truthy sentinel is an authenticated caller at every one of them — the obvious refactor (`return { down: true }`) is a total authentication bypass that reads as an improvement. A `WeakSet` keyed on the request object: no cross-request race in a shared isolate, collected with the request, and **asserted** — a test reads every `return` in the function and requires it to be `null` or the user.
- **READ IN `harden`, WHICH ALREADY TAKES THE SAME REQUEST.** Three lines, not forty: `servedAtRoot` keeps `/api/` off both hostname rewrites, so the object `harden(res, request)` gets is the one `authUser` saw. Threading `request` through 38 refusals would be the `safe-image` failure in test form — a guard every caller must remember is one a caller eventually forgets — and it covers any auth refusal added later for free.
- **429 IS CANNOT-TELL, decided explicitly rather than left in the 401 bucket.** It means the provider would not look, not that the token is bad, and telling somebody to sign in again is the one instruction guaranteed to make rate limiting worse. **Not flagged:** no token at all (the ordinary signed-out case, which never reaches the provider) and a real 401/403.
- **IT MAKES ONE COMMENT PARTLY FALSE AND SAYS SO.** `/api/site/route`'s header reads "NEVER 5xx", and `harden` can now answer 503 there. The rule's INTENT is untouched — the client reads that route as `if (!r.ok || !d) return go();`, so a 503 and a 401 both fall through to the build exactly as before — but an unqualified "never 5xx" one line above a response that can now be 503 is a comment somebody will believe.
- **DRIVEN THROUGH THE REAL ROUTER, not read.** The flag is set in `authUser` and acted on in `harden`, ~1,100 lines apart with the request object as the only thing joining them — the wiring layer where this repo has recorded twelve dead features. `test/auth-tristate.test.mjs` stubs the global fetch (GoTrue is its only caller here) and drives 500/502/503/429, a throw, a timeout, a real 401, an anonymous request, and **a second request after an outage** — because a module-level boolean would have turned one blip into 503 for everybody until the isolate recycled. 11 checks; reverting the `harden` upgrade turns 7 of them red, un-flagging the 5xx branch 5, un-flagging the throw 10.
- 2780 tests, `site build` 117/117.

## A build that made its own photographs had no link preview (2026-08-14)

**Round 14 — three audit findings whose fixes were all CORRECTED by the skeptic before landing, and the corrections are the useful part.**

- **`og:image` WAS RESOLVED BEFORE THE BUILD, AND PHOTOGRAPHS ARE BOUGHT IN BETWEEN.** `siteOgImage` scans `uploads/<slug>/`; `deps.images` writes into exactly that prefix; the route captured the value before `buildAndPublishPages` ran. So a build that generated its own pictures — the picture-led families, up to ~19 credits each — published with **no `og:image` at all**, and every share of it degraded from a large card to a small one. Self-healing only on the owner's next edit, which is the one thing they have no reason to make after a build that looked fine. Resolved inside the publish closure now, **byte-identical to the cheap-edit spine's line**, so the answer stays derived in one place.
  - **THE TRACE MARK MOVED WITH THE WORK IT TIMES.** Leaving `tr.at("og")` at the old site would have timed nothing and named a step that no longer runs there. It is a build-level mark now, like `fonts`, and the guard asserts BOTH that it exists and that the image is resolved *inside* the publish closure — the second half is what actually holds the fix.
- **THE BUILD LANE BOUGHT SEED ROWS FOR TABLES THE SITE ALREADY HAD.** `EDIT_RULE` invites a revise to NAME AN EXISTING TABLE (to add a column, or make it payable), and `access` is compelled on every table — so `services` arrives looking like an unfilled display table. `seedGaps` asks about access, columns and the seed, never Postgres, so the rows were bought and then discarded by `seedSiteRows`, which skips any table that already has some. **The addon lane was narrowed in round 4 and this one was not.**
  - **NARROWED FROM NAMES ALREADY IN HAND, at zero I/O** — `editState.tables` comes from a `_meta` read three statements above. The rejected alternative probed Postgres per table: up to six sequential round trips on the critical path in front of a model call, duplicating a question the seeder asks anyway. **An empty set keeps today's behaviour byte-identically** (a first build, or an `editState` read that failed), so it fails toward buying — the cheap direction.
- **`cost` MEANT TWO THINGS ONE LINE APART.** The deposit refusal answered `cost: SITE_BUILD_FEE` (2, what was being taken) and the floor refusal `cost: floor` (20, what a build needs), on consecutive branches of one route — introduced by the floor branch being inserted directly below a pre-existing refusal. **And the deposit branch carried no `msg` at all**, while the client renders `d.msg` and otherwise falls back to a sentence with no figure in it, so the customer most likely to be short was told the least. One meaning now, and it says so. **No balance clause on that branch, deliberately**: `use_credits` answers -1 both for "the bill exceeds the balance" and for an unparseable RPC answer, so a figure there is a claim we cannot support.
- **THE SKEPTIC WAS RIGHT ABOUT THE WINDOW, TO THE BYTE.** It predicted that hoisting `floor` would push `creditBack` to exactly +900 in `build-models.test.mjs`'s `w.slice(i, i + 900)` — excluded — and `Sonnet 5` to +1290. Both went red on a correct change. Re-anchored on `tr.at("gate")`.
- **AND THEN MY OWN NEW GUARD HAD THE VACUOUS-WINDOW BUG, in the test written to fix a windowing bug.** `workerCode` is comment-stripped, so anchoring on the prose above the deposit found nothing, `indexOf` answered -1, and the window opened at the GAME route's `let balanceAfter;` — sweeping in refusals from a different feature (`genCost`, `rCost`). Anchored on code now.
- **A COUNT OVER A REGION IS NOT A PER-BRANCH ASSERTION**, and a mutant proved it: `msgs >= refusals` stayed true with the deposit's `msg` deleted, because the neighbouring 503's own `msg` kept the total up. Asserted on the branch itself.
- 2769 tests, `site build` **117/117**, **8/8 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline.

## Four guards were watching a file they could not see (2026-08-14)

**Round 13, and the through-line is that every one was found by MEASURING a check rather than reading it.** All four defects had been live for weeks with a green suite; three of the four fixes were corrected by an adversarial skeptic before landing, and a fifth bug was created and caught inside the same sitting.

- **THE IMPORT GUARD WAS BLIND TO 2,592 LINES OF `worker.js`, and its comment blanker was why.** It blanks block comments before line comments, so `// \`/api/*\` IS DELIBERATELY LEFT ALONE` at 6689 — a LINE comment whose text contains `/*` — read as an opener and ran to the next real `*/` at 9281. **Measured: of the 115,502 non-space characters in that span, the old order left ONE.** So the guard written after the `OWN_ZONES` outage, whose whole job is catching a name used without being imported, could not see the serve path, the SPA fallback, the uploads routes or the Turnstile config, while reporting a clean file. Line comments are blanked first now and a block opener must START a line — the second runaway was `"https://*.supabase.co"` in the CSP, 57,001 more characters. **This is the failure CLAUDE.md already recorded ("a whole-file comment blanker CANNOT be used on worker.js") living inside a guard.**
- **AND THE BUG IT WAS BLIND TO WAS REAL.** `MAX_FILES_PER_SITE` appears ONCE in `worker.js` — a bare read in `buySitePhotos` — and was imported nowhere: a `ReferenceError` inside a try whose catch logs *"upload headroom check failed"*, so **the owner's 200-file allowance had never once been enforced against generated photographs** and the only trace read like R2 being unavailable. The guard's own comment predicted it and called it an acceptable gap: *"a bare read like `const x = MAX_BODY;` is not matched."*
  - **THE WIDENING IS AFFORDABLE ONLY BECAUSE IT IS NARROWED, and that was measured before it landed.** A bare read now counts when the name is UPPER_SNAKE — an English sentence does not contain `MAX_FILES_PER_SITE`. Over the 235 UPPER_SNAKE exports the scan sees, the naive form flags EIGHT, all of them the constant being discussed in a comment; against properly blanked source it is **ZERO**, and removing the real import still reports the bug.
  - **The guard and its test were TWO COPIES OF ONE RULE and a mutant proved it**: replacing the widened arm left the new test green, because the test asserted its own copy. `usesName` is the one reader now.
- **A LINT TOLD 4 CORRECT PAGES OFF, AND THEY WERE PAGES THE MODEL LEARNS FROM.** The full lint over the 324 family exemplars was 4 problems, all one shape: `import { RepairStatus, type RepairStage }` against a file whose line 33 is `export type RepairStage`. **The cause was the EXPORT LIST, not the rule.** `COMPONENT_TYPES` keeps only types with an object body, so 30 modules whose one exported type is a string union looked as though they exported nothing of the sort. `COMPONENT_TYPE_NAMES` is the names-only scan; the gap is now 0 of 367, held by a derived test, and **`PAGE_RULES` came out byte-identical**, so it cost no prompt tokens and no cache.
  - **SKIPPING TYPE SPECIFIERS WAS THE FIRST FIX AND WAS STRICTLY WORSE.** With an honest list, `import { X, type Invented }` is a real `TS2305` that a skip lets through — and measured, the skip changed **no answer at all**, because dropping the `type ` strip made the name fail the capital-initial test one line down. Inert protection reading as real; the ninth recorded instance.
  - **~18 OF THE ~20 LINT RULES WERE SWEPT BY NOTHING.** Every corpus sweep filtered to the rule family it was written for. The family exemplars now go through the WHOLE lint; the reference pages stay filtered, because `{tables: []}` is a lie about them and makes 8 spurious table problems.
- **ONE CUSTOMER'S THEME COULD SURVIVE INTO THE NEXT CUSTOMER'S STYLESHEET.** `writeTheme` and `writeTokens` both APPEND to `src/styles.css`, and the only thing that ever put it back was a side effect of `writeFonts`, gated on two conditions that can each be false — and when either was, the fallback read `src/styles.css` ITSELF and appended to that. **Measured over three builds: 94 → 120 → 146 bytes with every earlier build's marker still present.** The reset moved into `resetRoutes`, beside the `dist` and `dist-ssr` wipes it belongs with; the fallback arm is KEPT, because it is correct in a fresh sandbox and the Dockerfile is now asserted to bake the base. **Deleting both `cp` lines from the Dockerfile passed 150 of 150 Dockerfile-reading tests** — neither pristine base was asserted anywhere.
- **`og:url` NAMED THE HOME PAGE ON EVERY PRERENDERED PAGE OF EVERY SITE — a REPEAT finding, raised five days earlier and still true.** So a booking page shared into WhatsApp carried the right title and description over an address pointing somewhere else, and a crawler was told two addresses are one page. `fileForRoute` moved beside `routeOf` (it is its inverse, and there were already two copies of it), and the publish path keys the route list it already computes. A mixed-case page misses the map and keeps the site URL — the safe direction, stated in the source so nobody "fixes" it by lowercasing.
- **AND THEN I SHIPPED A `ReferenceError` OF MY OWN, WHICH ONLY THE CONTAINER COULD SEE.** `export { X } from "./y.mjs"` forwards X and creates **NO local binding**, so `fileForRoute(p)` in the same file threw. `node --check` passes, esbuild bundles it, **2,766 unit tests were green, and the build service died at the first prerender of every build.** Found by `site-build` against the real container. The two-line form (`import { X }; export { X };`) binds AND forwards; a derived guard now refuses the bare form for any name the module also calls, and it fires on exactly that mutation.
- **THREE OF FIVE PROPOSED FIXES WERE WRONG BEFORE THE SKEPTIC READ THEM**, and each correction is the useful part: the import widening would have false-alarmed eight times, the styles fix would have broken the eval harness's font path, and the `og:url` fix named ONE spelling-pinned guard when there were TWO. **Five existing guards went red on correct changes**, every one pinned to a spelling rather than a property.
- 2767 tests, `site build` **117/117** against the real container, **12/12 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline (a deliberate no-op control survived, as it must). **NOT PROVEN LIVE** — the Anthropic account is empty, so no customer build has run against any of it.

## A fifth copy of one mapping, and the link idiom nothing checked (2026-08-14)

**The first of these is a follow-on from my own change and worth reading as one: correcting a shared mapping is only safe once every reader of it is actually shared.**

- **`validatePages` KEPT A FIFTH PRIVATE COPY OF THE FILE-TO-URL MAPPING, and this is the second bug it has produced.** First `menu/index.tsx` derived as `/menu/index`, so every correct `<Link to="/menu">` was rewritten to `/` as dangling and a false problem was reported on a site that published. Then `about.team.tsx` derived as `/about.team`, where TanStack's flat-route convention routes it at `/about/team`. Both are shapes `cleanPath`'s `SAFE_PATH` admits, so both are reachable output rather than hypotheticals.
  - **CORRECTING THE OTHER READERS MADE THE DOT CASE WORSE, not better.** Before 2026-08-14 every reader agreed on the wrong answer, so a dot-form page was consistently mis-addressed; afterwards the container and the published manifest said `/about/team` and this one still said `/about.team` — so the link that `tsr generate` really accepts became the one this rewrote away, and the reported problem named the wrong cause. **A shared mapping is only shared once the last copy is gone**, and the two guards written for Round 8 could not see this one because it was a local `const` shadowing the import that did not exist yet.
- **A LINK WRITTEN AS A PLAIN ANCHOR WAS CHECKED BY NOTHING.** The dangling-link rewrite covers `to=`/`to:` only — the TYPED forms, where a missing route is a `TS2322` and the build dies. `<a href="/quote">` compiles, bundles and publishes, and lands the visitor on the router's not-found page; nobody gets a signal, not the owner and not us. **500 route hrefs across the 324 family exemplars**, which are exactly what the model copies.
  - **THE MEASUREMENT IS WHAT LET THIS EXIST.** Zero of those 500 are dangling — driven through the REAL `validatePages`, one family at a time, as if the model had returned that family's pages: **0 flagged across all 100 families.** The same bar `lintPages` had to clear over its 328 pages, and it is pinned as a regression test with a floor on the href count, because a corpus that stopped using the idiom would make the zero meaningless.
  - **REPORTED, NEVER REWRITTEN, and the asymmetry with `to=` is the decision.** That rewrite exists because the alternative is losing the whole site to a failed compile; there is no build to save here, the page publishes either way. And an href may legitimately hold a non-route path — `/u/` uploads, `/api/` calls, `/sitemap.xml` — where a wrong exemption in a REWRITE breaks a working link while a wrong exemption in a REPORT costs one noisy sentence. The corpus offers no evidence at all about those cases (0 of 500 use them), which is precisely where a false alarm would come from.
- **THE SURVIVOR WAS THE GATE THE `to=` RULE ALREADY HAD, and forgetting it would have put a false problem on every cheap edit.** A partial set with no `knownRoutes` cannot judge a link: the edit and addon lanes return only what the model WROTE, so every unchanged page of the site reads as nonexistent — reproduced by the 2026-08-13 audit for `to=`, where a one-page edit's correct `<Link to="/book">` was rewritten and `problems` told the customer their live booking page did not exist. `PAGE_RULES` teaches in-body links and the exemplars are full of them, so for hrefs it would have fired on essentially every edit.
- 2757 tests, **8/8 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline (7/8 on the first sweep; the survivor was the gate above, closed and re-proven).

## One bad table name cost the whole build (2026-08-14)

**Three audit lows in `normalizeSchema`, the tolerant boundary every schema passes through. All three are about the SHAPE of a failure rather than a wrong answer, which is why they sat at low and why they are worth fixing anyway.**

- **THE TABLE NAME WAS THE ONE IDENTIFIER NOBODY CHECKED.** Every other one in `coerceTable` is regex-checked; the name accepted any truthy value — `a"; drop--` and a 60-character name were both measured straight through. It was judged for the first time at `applySiteSchema`'s `sqlIdent`, which throws **outside any per-table try**, so the route's catch turned ONE bad name into a 502 for the WHOLE build: **the customer's good tables died with the bad one**, the refund booked it as our fault, and the log read as the schema engine failing when the cause was model output. No safety hole either way — `sqlIdent` is the boundary and it holds — so this was purely the failure shape. It loses one table now, and `refusedTables` says which.
- **THE BARE MAP SWALLOWED THE WHOLE SPEC.** `spec.tables || spec` is deliberate — a name→definition map instead of a list is a model habit this boundary is tolerant of by design — but with no `tables` key it read EVERY top-level key as a table. Measured: `{functions, fonts, seed}` yielded three zero-column `collect` tables with those names, which passed the "did it describe anything" refusal, were CREATEd in the caller's own Neon database, landed in `_meta`, and were described to the page generator.
  - **THE FIRST DRAFT OF THE FIX BROKE THE FEATURE IT WAS PROTECTING.** Requiring evidence of a table refused `{a: {}, b: {}}` — which is the exact shape the bare-map path exists to accept and has a test of its own. The rule that works is narrower and the distinction is real: **an entry with NO keys is an under-specified table; an entry with keys that are none of a table's is not a table at all.** A shape test, not a deny-list of top-level names, because a list has to be kept in step with every key added beside it and the day it is not is the day the junk table comes back.
- **A GUARANTEE THE TOOL OFFERS THAT FAILS ITS SHAPE CHECK WAS NULLED WITH NO TRACE.** `droppedFields` deliberately skips `TOOL_TABLE_FIELDS` — it reports what the designer reached for and we do not HAVE — so the inverse had no reader at all. A marketplace owner whose designer emitted `publicView: {columns: ["id", "owner_id"]}`, both stripped because a projection may carry neither, gets a site with no browsable listing and nothing saying the projection was refused. `refusedFields` is that reader: **declared-truthy and normalised-falsy**, which is the whole test and is what keeps it from crying wolf — a boolean declared `true` comes back `true`, an absent field is never declared, and `access` defaults to something truthy, so only a shape check that binned the value can produce the pair. Measured against a fully-declared good table: zero.
- **THE SWEEP FOUND TWO REAL GAPS AND ONE GENUINELY INERT GUARD, and the third is the one worth writing down.** Removing `read`/`write` from the not-a-guarantee exclusion changed NO output, because all four excluded fields survive normalisation whatever they are declared as. **Kept and said so in the source rather than deleted or falsely asserted**: it holds by a property of four other lines, each one edit from changing, and the day `read` gains a validator it is the difference between a correct report and one that flags every pair-declared table on the platform. The two real gaps — the shape test leaking onto the explicit `tables` path, and an all-refused `columns` list reading as alive — both had no discriminating case until the mutation named them.
- 2750 tests, **15/16 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline, the sixteenth proven inert by measurement rather than assumed.

## The look lane could lose an instruction, charge for a no-op, and go quiet (2026-08-14)

**Five audit lows in the one lane every look change is routed to FIRST, and the through-line is that all five are silent.** Nothing errors, nothing logs, the customer is told it worked.

- **THE CAP DROPPED MORE THAN IT HAD TO, and it was measured through the real module rather than argued.** `[...Object.keys(b), ...keys].slice(0, MAX)` lists every axis the edit RESTATES twice — once from the edit, once from the merge — so slicing before the `Set` let a duplicate occupy a slot and the kept set came out SHORT. Six stored axes plus an edit restating one and adding one merges to seven, the cap allows six, **and it kept five**. The customer lost an earlier instruction they never asked to change, silently — precisely the "first instruction being forgotten" failure the merge exists to prevent, arriving through the mechanism meant to prevent it. **Identical code in `mergeTokens`**, so the same was true of colours.
- **AN ASK THAT WAS ALREADY SATISFIED BOUGHT A ~21-27-CREDIT REBUILD.** Every escalation falls through to the full revise by contract, and "nothing moved" escalated — so a customer repeating an instruction that had already applied (a stale preview is the ordinary trigger) paid for every page to be regenerated and a byte-identical site to be published. **The discriminator is whether the model NAMED anything**: fields that all equal what is stored is *"you already have that"*; naming nothing at all is *"I could not express this"*, which is the case the escalation was written for and is untouched. Keeping only the first half would turn every unexpressible look ask into a dead end, so both are asserted.
- **"YOUR SITE IS UNTOUCHED" WAS TRUE OF THE PUBLISHED SITE AND FALSE OF THE STORED STATE.** The look is written to `_meta` before the recompile because `recompileAndPublish` reads it from there — and so does every other publish path, so a failed compile left the change waiting for the customer's next unrelated edit to apply it silently, under a version label naming only the typo they were fixing. **The worst shape is a rename**: the rewritten pages are discarded with the failed build while the stored brand is new, so the next publish gives the tab and the link preview one name and every heading another — the exact disagreement `renamePages` exists to prevent, arriving through the failure path. Rolled back key by key now, with an absent patch DELETED rather than restored as `{}` (which later reads as "a patch exists"), and **a rollback that itself fails says so** rather than repeating the sentence that makes it undiagnosable.
- **THE ONE LANE EVERY COLOUR IS ROUTED TO WAS THE ONE THAT COULD NOT REPORT A REFUSED COLOUR.** Ask for "the background yellow and the buttons cornflower", and if the model emits something unparseable for the buttons the reply was *"✅ Updated the look — page colour."* with total silence about the rest. `site-tokens.mjs`'s own doc says a dropped token must be NAMED and the full build path does name it. **And `styleNote` was worse than missing: it was returned and rendered by nothing** — the existing guard titled *"THE CUSTOMER IS TOLD, at both ends of the wire"* matched the BUILD reply's note block, a different code path, so it read as covered while the look lane said nothing. Vacuous-by-scope, this repo's most-recorded test failure, and the audit only caught half of it.
- **THE CONTAINER SAID THE THEME FAILED AND NOBODY CARRIED IT.** `writeTheme`/`writeFonts` never fail a build — a site whose data layer is live must not be lost over a typeface — so they answer `applied:false` with a sentence instead, on every build, forwarded by nothing at either layer. Latent today (all 500 registry themes render), and the class is not: **a stored theme LATER REMOVED from the registry** — a deletion this repo performs regularly — would make every subsequent publish of that site ship the default look while reporting success, for ever. Carried on both publish paths, because every cheap edit republishes through `recompileAndPublish` and a build-path-only fix leaves a text fix, a colour change and a picture swap all silent about it.
- **TWO GUARDS WENT RED ON CORRECT CHANGES AND BOTH WERE THE RECORDED CLASSES.** One counted "the customer is told their site survived" phrases against compile branches and matched a COMMENT explaining the bug (`repeating "your site is untouched" is the one answer that…`) — comments blanked before counting, third instance this session. The other pinned the whole one-line `if (…) return escalate("no-change")` and failed when it became a block — re-anchored on the property.
- **The sweep's one survivor was a real gap and the shape is the usual one:** `lookNote` was guarded at the server end only, so the route composed the nothing-to-do answer and the client rendered it nowhere — an already-satisfied ask would have printed *"✅ Updated the look."* with an empty list, which reads as a change that silently failed. Closed and re-proven; **20/20** across both sweeps.
- 2740 tests. The blip finding filed with these was already fixed in Round 5 and is recorded there.

## A publish must not damage a site that already works (2026-08-14)

**Four audit lows with one theme, and the theme is the part worth keeping: every piece of the publish path was designed against a FIRST build, where the alternative to a degraded outcome is nothing at all. On an existing site the alternative is the site they already have.**

- **SALVAGE COULD REPLACE A WORKING PAGE WITH AN APOLOGY.** Its own framing — *"their site with one page reading 'this page isn't finished yet', instead of nothing"* — is true exactly when the alternative is nothing. Pre-salvage, a revise whose pages failed to compile deliberately left the published site untouched, so *"change the phone number in the header"* could not cost the customer anything; with salvage and no notion of what is live, one page failing to compile publishes a stub OVER a working menu. `livePages` is the fix: **a page the site is already serving is never stubbed.**
  - **REFUSED WHOLESALE, NOT PARTIALLY.** Stubbing the new pages and leaving the live one broken publishes a site worse than either outcome — the change half-landed AND a page they had still failing. The fallback (leave the published site exactly as it is) is what they had before salvage existed.
  - **A NEW page that fails is still stubbed, which is the whole value.** The rule is about what would be DESTROYED, not about build-versus-revise, so a first build passes an empty list and behaves byte-identically to before. Driven with `undefined`, `null`, `[]` and an empty `Set`, because the safe direction here is the one that silently switches the protection off.
  - **A kit file still wins over a live page.** `foreign` means no amount of stubbing helps and is a fact about US shipping something broken; reporting "the page is live" instead sends the reader to the customer's site looking for a bug in our kit.
- **THE SALVAGE RECOMPILE HAD NO DRAIN RETRY** — a bare `compile`, not `compileWithRetry`. A container drained mid-recompile is the exact race that wrapper exists for, measured live twice, so unlucky rollout timing silently downgraded a salvageable site to the data-model placeholder for a reason that had nothing to do with the customer's pages.
- **`foreign`'s COMMENT NAMED A CONSUMER THAT DOES NOT EXIST** — *"because the eval reads it"*. The eval never calls `publishPages` at all, and the route forwarded no salvage object, so the signal designed to catch "we shipped a broken kit component" — `fallbackSeed`, `require()`, `FaqAccordion`, all of which really happened — died in the module return. Carried on the response now, and only when it explains something, so its presence means the compile failed and here is why nothing could be rescued. **A false claim in a comment is what gets believed next time somebody edits the line.**
- **ONLY `index.html` WAS ORDERED LAST, AND ROUND 8 MADE THAT WORSE BY DESIGN.** A document names the new hashed bundle, so nothing may see it until the bundle is fully written — which was the whole truth exactly while a site was one HTML file plus a bundle. Each route is prerendered to its own document now, with a STABLE name, written in `collectDist`'s readdir order, which does not promise to put `assets/` first. So a visitor deep-linking to `/book` during the seconds of a republish could get HTML whose module script 404s: a blank page at a public URL, and the exact failure write-then-sweep was introduced to end, reopened one file type down. The docstring's *"atomic from a visitor's side"* was true of the home page and of nothing else. **`rollbackVersion` had the identical one-file sort** and is fixed with it.
  - **The sort is DRIVEN, not matched.** A comparator that matches the right pattern and compares the wrong way round reads identically in a source-read — so the test lifts the real expression out and sorts a real list through it, and a mutant that inverts it dies.
- **CONSIDERED AND DELIBERATELY NOT DONE: the sweep's grace period.** `deleteSitePrefix` runs in the same request as the writes, so the previous build's hashed chunks vanish seconds after the new build lands — a visitor who had the site OPEN clicks to a route they have not visited, the browser asks for the old chunk name, and gets a hard 404 mid-session. The write-then-sweep docstring presents exactly this click-through 404 as the bug it fixed; it fixed it for NEW visitors during the window, not for in-session ones. The fix is a one-publish grace period, and the honest way to do it is a marker object outside the served prefix recording what the last publish orphaned (self-healing: a lost marker delays a delete rather than leaking it, since an orphan is still an orphan next time). **Not built now because it is storage-lifecycle machinery on the publish path Round 8 just changed, and `build smoke` — the thing that would catch a mistake in it — cannot run while the model account is empty.** Written down so it is a decision rather than an oversight. **BUILT 2026-08-15 (`site-sweep.mjs`) — see the entry below; the second half of that reasoning was wrong, and the correction is the useful part.**
- 2733 tests, **13/13 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline. The suite was green when the salvage change landed and before any test was written for it, which is the honest measure of what was holding the old behaviour: nothing.

## Model-written page code ran in the build server's own process (2026-08-14)

**The last open medium from the 30-step audit that was mine to fix, and it is the one place this platform EXECUTES what a model wrote.** `prerender()` `import()`ed the generated SSR bundle into the build service and called it — so a route component, written from a brief this codebase already treats as attacker-controlled prose, ran with the service's privileges, in the loop that answers `/health`, in a container that is **long-lived and shared by every customer on the platform.**

- **TWO FAILURES, AND ONLY ONE OF THEM IS THE OBVIOUS ONE.** `renderToString` is synchronous and every other step in `build-server.mjs` is a subprocess with `STEP_TIMEOUT` and a SIGKILL behind it; this one had neither, so a `while (true)` blocked the event loop, `/health` stopped answering, and `oneAtATime` queued **every other customer's build** behind the wedge until Cloudflare recycled the instance. And `resetRoutes` restores `src/routes` and nothing else — `/app/.routes-base`, `.index-base.html`, `.styles-base.css`, `src/components/**` and `node_modules` all persist, so a write to any of them would be in every LATER customer's build: cross-tenant stored XSS on published sites, from one hostile brief.
- **`builder/prerender-child.mjs` WRITES NOTHING, and that is the whole design rather than a detail.** Not a temp file, not a snapshot, not a log — it reads the bundle, renders, and prints; the parent does every write there is. **That is what makes the privilege drop possible at all**: a child that touched the filesystem could not be run as a user that cannot write. Asserted as a property of the FILE (no `node:fs`, no `child_process`, no `fetch`), not of a test run.
- **PROVEN LIVE, BOTH DIRECTIONS, and the pair IS the finding and its fix.** Undropped, a render really does write into the build service's own directory (`wrote=YES`, measured). Dropped to uid 65534 the identical page comes back **`refused:EACCES`** and still renders perfectly. Pinned as a test that **SKIPS rather than passing** when it cannot run — it needs root and an unprivileged user — because a security check that silently goes green on every CI machine is worse than no check.
- **AND A REAL BUNDLE RENDERS UNDER IT.** The one thing a fixture could not answer is whether React + TanStack survive a dropped uid, so `site-build` was run with `PRERENDER_USER=nobody`: **117/117, three routes prerendered, 1382ms against 1300ms undropped.** The drop costs nothing and breaks nothing.
- **NDJSON, ONE LINE PER ROUTE, and that is not a style choice.** A child SIGKILLed mid-loop has already flushed the lines it wrote, so **a page that hangs costs its own snapshot rather than the whole site's**. A kill can cut a line in half, which the parent detects by that line failing to parse — the one shape it must not do is accept a truncated body as a page. The parent therefore parses the output BEFORE it judges the exit status.
- **THE VERDICT RIDES ON EVERY BUILD (`prerenderUnprivileged`), because the drop is not guaranteed.** It needs the service running as root and needs the user to exist, neither of which the code can promise — and "we thought this was sandboxed" is a worse position than knowing it is not. **Carried only when FALSE**, at both hops, so the ordinary response is byte-identical and the field's PRESENCE is the alarm rather than its value.
- **NO AUTOMATIC FALLBACK TO AN UNDROPPED RUN, deliberately, and it was considered.** It would convert "the drop broke the render" from a silent snapshot loss into a working build — and it is reachable by the attacker: a page that hangs on the FIRST route produces no lines at all, which is exactly the condition such a fallback would fire on. A retry that a hostile page can trigger is not a sandbox.
- **`finish()` EXITS FROM A WRITE CALLBACK, so the child is one function with early returns.** A straight-line script carries on past a fatal and prints a second, contradictory verdict about the same run — asserted by counting them. Exiting explicitly is required rather than tidy: `entry-server.tsx` builds a QueryClient per route and says in as many words that "a retrying client would only schedule timers in a process that is about to exit". In-process that was true; here the process IS the thing that has to exit, and a stray timer holds it open until the parent's SIGKILL — 150 seconds of a customer waiting for work that finished.

**And four cheap failures in the same two files, all from the same audit.**

- **THE SSR BUILD FAILURE SAID `"ssr build failed"`** — four words naming the step we already knew, which is the exact sentence `exit-reason.mjs` exists to abolish, on the ONE failure that costs every snapshot on a site. An operator asking why sites stopped getting snapshots after a template change had to reproduce the build by hand. **My own absence guard then matched its own fix's comment** — the comment explaining the bug contains the bug's spelling — so it blanks comments before judging, for the second time this month.
- **`routePaths` REIMPLEMENTED THE FILE-TO-URL MAPPING, AND FIXING ONLY IT WOULD HAVE BEEN WORSE THAN THE BUG.** TanStack's flat form reads a dot as a DIRECTORY separator (`about.team.tsx` → `/about/team`) and the container read it as a character, so such a page got no snapshot and a junk file landed where nothing could reach it. But the published route manifest comes from `siteRoutes` → `routeOf`, so correcting one side alone makes Round 7's fallback answer **404 for a page that used to load**. There is ONE reading now — `routeOf`, imported by the container — and it also learned the two underscore conventions (a trailing `_` is not in the URL; a leading `_` is a pathless layout with no address, which is where `__root` falls out too). A `$` still passes through on purpose: `siteRoutes` needs to see it to mark a site dynamic.
  - **`public/chat.js` WAS A FOURTH READER**, with an existing test asserting it agrees with `routeOf` — over six fixtures that all happened to be the simple shapes, so it stayed green while the client answered `/about.team` for a page really served at `/about/team`. The fixture list is the whole strength of that test and now covers every shape `safeRoute` admits.
  - **PROVED AGAINST THE THING THAT DECIDES.** `site-build` now builds a real `about.team.tsx` through the real container: `tsr generate` is the authority on the route and `routeOf` on what we prerender, and this is the only place the two are MADE to agree rather than assumed to. It fails both ways — measured `prerender → ["/about/team","/","/menu"]`, and nothing at the literal-dot address.
- **A MALFORMED PERCENT-SEQUENCE IN A PAGE URL KILLED THE WHOLE BUILD SERVICE.** `decodeURIComponent` throws, and an uncaught throw in a Node request handler does not 500 — it kills the process, mid-build, for every customer queued behind it, and Cloudflare reports the restart as "the container is not running": the two-messages-one-cause misdiagnosis the Dockerfile warns reads as flaky infrastructure. The input is a model-written href — `/services/50%-off` is enough — so it is **deterministic for that site**: every rebuild kills the container again and the site can never be built.
- **THE RENDER CHECK'S 25s BUDGET TRUNCATED SILENTLY.** A run that stopped early produced findings from the pages it reached and a report otherwise byte-identical to a full clean pass — "nothing wrong" said about pages nobody opened. Live `renderMs` sits at ~30s against that budget, so this is the ordinary path rather than the edge. `partial` is not `ok: false` (what ran really ran) and is omitted when the run was complete, so an untruncated report is unchanged.
- **The comment claiming a skipped route serves the app shell was corrected rather than acted on.** Since the prerender exists, `index.html` IS the home page's snapshot, so what such a route actually serves is the home page's content, painted and torn down by `createRoot` — a brief wrong-page flash and the home card in that route's link preview. That is a degradation back to pre-feature behaviour, not a regression past it; the honest fix is the comment, because a comment claiming the safer of two behaviours is the one that gets believed.
- **The image's COPY line is guarded by a DERIVED scan now.** The child is SPAWNED, not imported, so the transitive import walk could never see it — and a missing COPY would not fail that guard: it would fail every build's prerender with MODULE_NOT_FOUND and, since the prerender is best-effort by design, **publish every site with the home page's snapshot at every address.** Any `"<name>.mjs"` literal in the entrypoint that names a real sibling is treated as a second entrypoint, and the scan is asserted to still find one.
- 2720 tests, `site build` **117/117** (110 before), **29/30 mutations caught** from a verified-green baseline. Two sweep notes worth keeping: the one survivor was a contrived shape (`const child = {code:0, out:""}`) whose realistic form — reverting to the in-process import — **is** caught, and the boundary it points at is honest (the unit suite cannot prove the prerender produces snapshots; `site-build` can and does); and my second attempt at it was **INERT**, appending a comment, which is the tenth recorded inert mutant and the reason a no-op check has to compare BEHAVIOUR rather than strings.

## Every published site was invisible to a crawler, and told nobody when it broke (2026-08-14)

**Round 7, the audit's remaining buildable gaps — four of them, all POST-publish, which is the half of the platform the audit called thinnest.** `site-seo.mjs` is the search-engine surface as a plain module; the Worker supplies the publish and serve wiring.

- **ONE MECHANISM, NOT THREE, and that is what made it cheap.** The publish path already knows the route list (it just compiled those pages) and the serve path's SPA fallback already reads `index.html` when a path has no prerendered document. So the route list and the redirect map ride INSIDE index.html as two meta tags in the existing `isibi:meta` fenced block — **the fallback parses them out of bytes it has already buffered, so the verdict costs no extra R2 read on any path** — and the sitemap is derived from the same list at publish. No new table, no new column, no new store.
- **THE SOFT-404 WAS THE ROOT OF IT.** The fallback answered **200 with the app shell for EVERY unknown address**, so a crawler could not tell a real page from a typo, a deleted page's old link served the bare not-found text with a status saying it was fine, and junk addresses counted as page views in the owner's analytics. Now: `redirect` (301) · `ok` (200) · `notfound` (404 **with the shell**, so the branded 404 still renders). **A 404 is deliberately not a page view.**
- **ABSENT MEANS TODAY'S BEHAVIOUR, which is what makes it safe to deploy.** Every site published before this has no manifest and keeps answering 200 for everything — one deploy cannot change what any existing site serves; a site heals on its next publish. `parseSiteManifest` answers **null** (no opinion) rather than an empty route list, and `routes: null` inside a manifest means the same thing one level down. The `normalizeLang` rule: a refusal to guess, not a default.
- **A `$` ROUTE PUBLISHES NO ROUTE LIST AT ALL.** The manifest cannot enumerate a dynamic segment's addresses, and a list that omitted them would 404 real deep links — so a dynamic site fails open to 200. Zero of the 318 exemplars declare one, so this is the rare case rather than the ordinary one.
- **THE ORIGIN IS SUBSTITUTED AT SERVE TIME, NEVER BAKED AT PUBLISH.** The same bytes serve at `<slug>.gofarther.app`, at `gofarther.dev/s/<slug>/` and on the owner's custom domain — a host baked into a sitemap is wrong on two of the three, and on a custom domain it is OUR domain inside the owner's sitemap, which is the leak the `.dev`/`.app` line exists to stop. The files carry `SITE_ORIGIN_TOKEN` and the Worker replaces it, the same reasoning as the `./` asset rewrite one branch over. **Not `immutable`** — a republished sitemap must not be cached for a year.
- **REDIRECTS ARE DERIVED BY DIFFING, so no lane has to remember to say so.** The publish reads the PREVIOUS `index.html` before overwriting it and compares route lists: a route that existed and is gone redirects **to the home page**. There is no rename verb anywhere in the product, so old → new is a guess this deliberately does not make. Accumulated across publishes, dropped when a page comes back (a re-added page must serve itself), and a kept entry whose target has since gone is **re-pointed at home rather than chained** — two hops is where redirect loops come from.
- **RUNTIME ERRORS WERE BUILT, PACKAGED AND DELIVERED TO NOBODY.** `error-reporting.ts` has captured every error a visitor's browser hits since the preview shell existed, and on a published site it went to the visitor's console and died — the owner learned their booking form was broken when customers stopped coming. The page POSTs it same-origin to `/api/db/<slug>/error`, it lands in the site's own `_errors` (created by every build beside `_secrets`, never through `grantsFor`, so no Data API role can touch it), and the owner reads it in **Cloud → Errors**. **ALWAYS 204, whatever happened**: an error response only produces a second console error about reporting the first, and a distinct status for "no such site" would let a stranger probe slugs through an unauthenticated endpoint. Rate-limited BEFORE the body is read, `CF-Connecting-IP` only, and the write rides `ctx.waitUntil` — the audit-log lesson, where a detached promise nobody holds is cancelled with the request and the log keeps a random subset.
  - **THE PANEL LEADS WITH WHAT IT COST THE VISITOR, never the raw enum.** `source` is the only severity signal there is — an `error_boundary` means they saw a broken page, an `unhandledrejection` may have been invisible — and "unhandledrejection" tells a barber nothing. Three sentences, plus a fallback so an unknown value degrades rather than rendering `undefined`. Found by LOOKING at the render: the field was stored, selected, returned and shown nowhere.
- **THE BRANDED 404 WAS ONE LINE THAT HAD NEVER BEEN WRITTEN.** `createRouter` set `defaultErrorComponent` and **no `defaultNotFoundComponent`**, so TanStack's own bare text rendered — the render check had literally measured it as *"blank: only 9 characters"* — while the kit's own `not-found.tsx` sat unused. `homeHref` carries the basepath, or a workspace-mounted visitor is sent to the platform's root instead of the site's.
- **RULE 18 ENDS THE OTHER "REACHABLE BY NOTHING" CASE.** `seo-jsonld.tsx` shipped with a purpose-built `localBusinessJsonLd()` and was used by **0 of 318 exemplars, 0 reference pages and 0 rules** — the exact class the 27 blocks and 196 examples were deleted for. The rule is scoped hard: **only facts the brief actually states**, because invented structured data is what gets a site's rich results switched off entirely, and a brief that states none means no `SeoJsonLd` at all.
- **AND WRITING IT FOUND A LIVE FALSE-ALARM CLASS IN THE EXPORT LINT, live since that check shipped 2026-08-09.** A page obeying rule 18 *exactly as written* was told off: *"imports { localBusinessJsonLd } … which does not export it"*. **The rule did not create this — it walked into it.** `UI_EXPORTS` is derived from `COMPONENT_API`, whose extractor matches `export function Name({ … })` — a **destructured props object, i.e. a COMPONENT** — and then harvests names with `/[A-Z][A-Za-z0-9]*\(/`. So a lowercase helper exported beside a component is invisible to it **twice over**, and the lint believed it knew a module's exports while knowing only half of them. **Measured across the kit: 123 helpers across 108 modules** — `useUndoRedo`, `formatBytes`, `luhn`, `parseCsv`, `scorePassword` — every one of which the lint would have refused. That is precisely the failure this check's own comment calls worse than the miss: *"a false alarm here would teach the model away from a component that is perfectly real"*.
  - **Narrowed to capital-initial names, which is exactly the class the data covers.** The `FaqAccordion` protection is untouched (an invented component name is capital-initial by React's own rule) and what is given up is a mistyped lowercase helper, which `tsc` refuses anyway — the same trade the skip-an-unknown-module rule one line above already makes. Guarded by **driving all 123 real helpers through the real lint** and requiring zero flags, paired with a scan-still-matches floor (a scan that silently stopped matching would report a clean kit and prove nothing) **and** an assertion that an invented capital-initial name is still refused — a narrowing that quietly disabled the whole check would pass the first test perfectly.
  - **Found by an adversarial reviewer, not by the suite.** 2651 tests were green with it live, because every existing case used a capital-initial name.
- **THE RESEARCH EXECUTOR HAD NEVER BEEN RUN BY ANYTHING.** `siteWebResearch` — the fetch loop, the `pause_turn` continuation, the source dedup, the usage accumulation the bill depends on — had no test that executed it (only its model constant was source-read), no integration harness could reach it (smoke briefs are trades, so `needsWeb` is false by design), and **its designed failure mode is silence**: every error path breaks the loop and returns empty facts. So a provider changing the response shape would ship undetected and every customer whose brief needed real facts would silently get training-data ones. It is exported now and driven with a stubbed `fetch`; nothing about the function changed to make it drivable.
- **THE SEARCH CAP WAS PER REQUEST AND IS NOW PER BUILD.** `max_uses` bounds ONE API request and the loop makes up to four, so a flat `MAX_QUERIES + 1` re-granted the allowance on every paused turn — one call could legitimately run ~16 searches where everything customer-facing promises ~3. Each round now asks only for what is LEFT, measured from the API's own count, and a paused turn with nothing left is not continued at all.
- **A FAILED SEARCH IS A FACT NOW, not a quiet zero.** `searched: false` was byte-identical to a build that never needed research — so when the designer declared the pages need current facts and the lookup died, the customer was handed a site built on training data with nothing saying so, against this module's own **THE FAILURE MUST BE LOUD** headline. `searchWanted` rides on `!!researchPromise` (the same expression that decides whether research runs, so the two cannot disagree) and the sentence goes to the CUSTOMER only — `contextBrief` still says nothing to the model, because telling it invites an apology written into the site, exactly as failed links already work. **Facts with no searches stays quiet**: the model answered from what it knew, which is degraded rather than empty-handed.
- **THREE EXISTING GUARDS WENT RED ON CORRECT CHANGES, all pinned to spellings** — `injectMeta(v.t, pm)` (the home page now merges the manifest in), `(await obj.text())` (the fallback buffers the shell to read its manifest, and **a body reads ONCE**, so reusing that buffer is correctness rather than thrift), and a `miss + 500` byte window the new comment outran. Re-anchored on properties and on a landmark. **The recurring own-goal, three times in one change.**
- **AN ADVERSARIAL AUDIT OF THIS CHANGE FOUND NINE MORE, two of them bugs this change INTRODUCED.** Five parallel dimension reviewers over the diff, each finding put to an independent skeptic. All nine below are fixed; **the suite was green at 2651 with every one of them live**, which is the honest measure of what a test suite over one's own new code is worth.
  - **RULE 18 PRESCRIBED AN ARGUMENT SHAPE DOCUMENTED NOWHERE, and it would have cost whole sites.** The first draft wrote the call as ES6 shorthand — `localBusinessJsonLd({ name, telephone, address, openingHours })` — which reads as four scalars. The real signature nests `address` and takes `openingHours` as a string ARRAY, and that shape appears in NOTHING the model can see: `COMPONENT_API`'s entry for the module is truncated and never mentions the helper, `COMPONENT_TYPES` has no entry, and no reference page or family exemplar uses it. So the natural call is `address: "42 High Street, Leeds"` → `TS2322` → and because the rule puts it on the HOME page, **which salvage refuses to stub**, the customer loses the whole site to the placeholder. The `fallbackSeed` and `useUpdateRow` class for the third time: the model reaches for a shape we never gave it. The rule now carries a worked call with the real nested shape, **verified by compiling it against the real component**, and the guard is derived from that component's own signature in both directions.
  - **`searchFailed` KEYED ON THE SEARCHES INSTEAD OF THE FACTS, which excluded the half that costs money — mine.** The condition required zero searches AND zero facts. The expensive failure is the other one: the searches ran, the answering round was lost, `facts` comes back empty. That has `searches > 0`, so the flag stayed unset while `searched: true` made the reply say **"Looked up current details on the web"** — the customer affirmatively told their site reflects current facts while `contextBrief` added no CURRENT FACTS section at all and page generation ran on training data, AND billed ~5 credits for the searches. The discriminator is the FACTS. The two sentences are now mutually exclusive as well, because `searched` is factually true even when nothing came back and that is precisely the claim which must not be made.
  - **THE REPORTED ROUTE WAS THE LAST ANCHOR CLICKED, NOT THE PAGE — the one finding that survived adversarial verification.** `currentRoute()` preferred `location.hash`, and its own comment still said *"`#/book` is the part that identifies the page"* — true under `createHashHistory()`, which was removed **2026-08-09**. The hash is an ordinary in-page anchor now: `PAGE_RULES` blesses `#prices`, `lintPages` exempts it, and **the reference page puts three of them in the SITE HEADER**, so they are on every page of a reference-shaped site (256 hash hrefs across the family exemplars). Harmless for a year because the value only reached the console — and **Round 7 made it the ONE field telling the owner where their site broke**, and persists it. A visitor who had clicked "Prices" and then hit a throw was filed against `prices`, a section anchor that is not a page, plausible enough that nobody could tell it was wrong. The mount prefix is stripped too, or one fault reads `/book` on the customer's domain and `/s/<slug>/book` in the workspace; the fragment rides as a suffix, because which section they were on is real information and just is not the page. **Stale code following a stale comment**, surfaced only because a change downstream started depending on it.
  - **THE CAP AND THE BILL READ THE SAME SELF-REPORTED NUMBER, so one response-shape change breaks both at once.** `usage.searches` comes only from `server_tool_use`, and it drove the new per-build grant AND `pageCost`'s `SEARCH_USD` multiplication — so a renamed or dropped field would have removed the cap (four rounds at a full allowance apiece, ~16 searches of real spend) and silenced the charge simultaneously, the two failures compounding rather than one catching the other. The grant now falls back to `granted`, our own tally of what we ASKED for, and the fallback is chosen by whether the field was **reported at all** rather than by its value — an absent count and a genuine zero are the same number after the coalesce, and an honest provider must keep its accurate, LOWER figure. Logged loudly when it fires, because a provider that stops reporting is spending our money and billing the customer nothing.
  - **THE 301 HAD NO LIFETIME, which defeated this feature's own contract.** `Response.redirect` sends `location` and nothing else, and a 301 with no freshness is heuristically cached by browsers effectively forever — it survives a restart. So `mergeRedirects` dropping an entry whose source is a live route again — done deliberately, *because "a re-added page must serve itself"* — was necessary and **not sufficient**: every browser that had already followed the old redirect never asked again, and a restored page was unreachable to exactly the returning customers it was restored for. `cache-control: public, max-age=600` now: a search engine still consolidates (Google honours the status, not the header) and a real visitor is back within ten minutes.
  - **BREAKING ON A SPENT SEARCH BUDGET THREW AWAY THE ROUND THAT ANSWERS — mine, introduced by the per-build cap.** `pause_turn` means the server tool loop was cut MID-turn and the model writes its factual brief AFTER it finishes searching, so "paused with the budget spent" is precisely the round about to produce the answer. Breaking there spends every search and returns `facts: ""`: the build pays for the lookup, page generation gets nothing, and the customer is told *"Looked up current details on the web"* over a site written from training data — **worse than the overspend it was meant to prevent.** One round of overtime now, then stop; and the tool stays in the request on that round rather than being omitted, because the conversation already carries `web_search_tool_result` blocks and whether the API accepts a continuation dropping their definition is untested here — losing the answer is the worse failure.
  - **`_errors` WAS CREATED ONLY BY `applySiteSchema`, WHICH THE CHEAP EDIT LANES NEVER CALL.** Text, look, picture, logo and data all publish through `recompileAndPublish`, which applies no schema — so an owner who has only ever changed wording and colours never got the table, every report threw `relation "_errors" does not exist` into a swallowing catch, and the panel told them **"Nothing has gone wrong"**: the exact failure the feature exists to prevent, wearing a reassuring sentence. The write path heals it on first use, **create-and-retry rather than create-first** so the ordinary path stays one statement and the extra round trip is paid once per site ever. The `_sessions` lesson, for the third time.
  - **THE REDIRECT CAP KEPT THE OLDEST FIFTY.** `mergeRedirects` wrote carried-over entries first and `redirectsContent` slices from the front, so on a site at the cap the pair dropped was the page deleted **just now** — the one whose address is in Google's index today and in somebody's messages this week — while a redirect from a year ago was kept forever. Unrecoverable, too: a dropped pair never reaches the manifest and the route is already gone from the page list, so the next publish cannot see it ever existed. Newly-gone routes are written first now, and the ordering IS the cap's meaning.
  - **AN UNREADABLE PREVIOUS `index.html` TURNED EVERY ACCUMULATED REDIRECT INTO A HARD 404.** `prev` is null whether the object is absent or the read threw, and the two must not behave alike: with the route list still published and the redirects lost, `decideFallback` answers `notfound` for exactly the addresses that were 301ing a moment earlier. A throw now suppresses the whole manifest and the site falls open to 200 — the same rule every other unknown here takes — while a genuinely missing object still publishes normally, because a site with no previous publish has no old addresses to protect.
- **The serve tests run on the SITE ZONE, and that is not incidental**: `/s/<slug>/` on the platform 301s to the subdomain (the one-public-address rule), so a test that hits `/s/` there is testing the redirect rather than the serve path. The workspace-mount arm is driven through a slug with a **trailing hyphen** — a legal build slug and an illegal DNS label, so `siteHostFor` answers null and such a site really is served at `/s/`, which is the exact class that keeps that mount alive.
- 2667 tests (+62), template typechecks, `site build` 110/110 against the real container. The whole serve path is driven through the REAL router with a fake R2, because the twelve recorded wiring deaths all lived in the layer a module test cannot see; the manifest is asserted as a ROUND TRIP through the real producer chain (`routesContent` → `metaTags` → `injectMeta` → `parseSiteManifest`) rather than against two hand-written fixtures that agree with each other and with neither end — the `routeOf` lesson. **NOT PROVEN LIVE**: it takes effect on each site's next publish, and the model account is empty.

## Every site's data has a second copy now (2026-08-14)

**The audit's one HIGH-value gap, closed: a site's booking list existed in exactly one place — its Neon database — so one bad bug or one wrong delete erased every customer permanently, with no copy anywhere.** `site-versions.mjs` archives page FILES only, and the "Backups" panel was the manual CSV export. `site-backup.mjs` is the whole design as a plain module; the Worker supplies nothing but the wiring.

- **ZERO NEW INFRASTRUCTURE, and that is the design.** The dump goes to R2 (already paid for, no egress fees), driven by the existing 2-minute cron. **The object KEY is the daily stamp** — `backups/<slug>/<YYYY-MM-DD>.json` — so "did today's happen" is answered by the store itself: no new table, no new column, and two racing ticks that write the same key write identical content. The `backups/` prefix is private BY CONSTRUCTION — nothing serves it — and a derived guard requires every `"backups/"` literal in the Worker to sit in one of the three allowed places (the cron deps, the owner route, the teardown), so a public route naming it fails a test before it ships.
- **WHAT A BACKUP IS: the DECLARED tables plus an allow-listed slice of `_meta`** (schema + the look keys). Tables come from the site's own stored schema, never a listing of the database, so `_secrets`/`_meta`/`_sessions` are unreachable by construction — and the meta half is an ALLOW-LIST, because `_meta` also holds service endpoints and whatever lands there next, and a backup that copies keys nobody listed is how a credential-shaped value ends up in a second store. **Truncation is a FACT in the dump** (the reader asks for cap+1, so "there was a row we did not keep" is observed, not inferred), and the worker's table read is `ORDER BY id ASC` so a truncated dump keeps the OLDEST rows — the newest are still visible in the owner's panel; the oldest are the ones only a backup still has.
- **RETENTION (keep 7) RUNS ONLY AFTER A SUCCESSFUL PUT.** Being unable to write a new copy must never become a reason to lose an old one — a night of failures that DELETED history would be the feature attacking the thing it exists to protect. Night window from 03:00 UTC (before it the runner is a total no-op — zero listings, zero heads — and everything after is retry window, so an outage in the small hours does not cost the day); ≤5 sites a tick so one slow database cannot starve the night; one site's failure warns and never stops the rest; the runner never throws.
- **THE OWNER ROUTE IS READ-ONLY, DELIBERATELY** — `GET /api/site/<slug>/backups` lists, `GET …/backups/<date>` downloads as an ATTACHMENT (owner-held customer data must not render inline on our origin — the export route's own rule). A backup an owner could DELETE is a backup an attacker with a stolen session could delete, and the whole point of a second copy is surviving exactly that moment; retention is the cron's job. **No restore-in-place either**: restoring rows over a live table is a merge-vs-replace decision nobody has made, and a wrong guess destroys the exact data this exists to keep — the download gives the owner everything. The panel is Cloud → Backups & export: the nightly copies above the per-table CSV/JSON export, driven in a real render.
- **DELETING A SITE DELETES ITS BACKUPS** — a second copy of customers' data must not outlive the site they gave it to; the wipe is cursor-followed, sits BEFORE the registration row (a failed wipe stays retryable through the same route), and rides `deleteSiteFor` so the account-deletion path gets it too.
- **The cost, stated:** each site's scale-to-zero compute wakes once a night for a few seconds — the price of having backups at all — and R2 holds ≤7 small JSON objects per site. **Not proven live** — no night has passed since it shipped; the first green signal is `backups/<slug>/` objects appearing after 03:00 UTC.

## The 30-step audit, and the first two fix rounds (2026-08-14)
